CBLE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The prohibition against receiving unlawful compensation in connection with customs business): the customs broker regulations prohibit a broker from accepting unlawful compensation, such as a kickback tied to referrals, in connection with the broker's customs business. This scenario has nothing to do with the five-year recordkeeping retention period, which concerns document retention rather than improper payments. It similarly has no connection to the supervision duty, which concerns oversight of the work done on the broker's behalf rather than referral arrangements. The triennial status report is an unrelated administrative filing confirming the broker's continued engagement in business, not an ethics provision governing referral payments.
  2. The correct answer is C (A national permit authorizing the broker to conduct business throughout the customs territory): A national permit issued under 19 CFR 111.19 is sufficient permit authority for a licensed broker to transact customs business throughout the customs territory of the United States. Requiring a separate permit for every location is the older approach that the national permit replaced. An informal verbal agreement with individual port directors carries no legal weight and is not a recognized form of authorization. Broker authorization to operate nationally does not need to be renewed on a per-shipment basis.
  3. The correct answer is B (The bond amount is generally based on the importer's duties, taxes, and fees paid, subject to a minimum): continuous bond amounts are generally calculated with reference to the importer's prior duties, taxes, and fees, and are also subject to an applicable statutory minimum bond amount. A flat rate identical for every importer nationwide ignores that bond amounts are tailored to each importer's own activity level. Basing the bond solely on shipment count in a single month ignores the actual dollar exposure the bond is meant to cover. Bonds are not simply waived once an importer crosses a particular entry count, since the bonding requirement continues regardless of filing history.
  4. The correct answer is A (It funds the maintenance and dredging of the nation's harbors and channels): HMF is assessed on ocean cargo specifically to support harbor maintenance activities such as dredging, which keeps shipping channels navigable. It has no connection to land border customs officer salaries, which are funded through general appropriations rather than this fee. Antidumping investigations are conducted and funded separately from the harbor maintenance program. General immigration enforcement is likewise a distinct function unrelated to the harbor maintenance fee's dedicated purpose.
  5. The correct answer is D (Advise the client and pursue correcting the record, such as through a prior disclosure): once a broker becomes aware of a material false statement in a client's prior filing, the broker has an obligation to address it rather than let it stand uncorrected, and a prior disclosure is one recognized way to do so. Who originally filed the entry does not eliminate the current broker's obligation upon discovering the problem. Waiting passively for the client to raise the issue is inconsistent with the broker's own duty to act once aware of a material misstatement. Simply declining future work for the client does nothing to correct the existing false statement already on file with CBP.
  6. The correct answer is B (The correct individual HTSUS classification number for each separate line item): each distinct line item on a 7501 must carry its own accurate HTSUS classification number, since different items on the same entry can fall under entirely different tariff provisions and duty rates. Averaging a single classification across multiple genuinely different items would misstate the duty owed on each one. Classification is required for every line item, not merely optional because an entry happens to include several items. Classification numbers must be included at the time of filing rather than deferred and added only if CBP later requests them.
  7. The correct answer is A (The felony conviction can serve as grounds for suspension or revocation of the license): the customs broker regulations list a felony conviction as a basis for CBP to pursue suspension or revocation proceedings against the broker's license, regardless of whether the underlying offense related directly to customs business. Character and fitness are ongoing requirements for holding a broker license, not just requirements at the time of initial licensing. A felony conviction does not merely affect a bond and leave the license itself untouched. Licenses are also not automatically transferable to another employee simply because the original license holder faces a conviction.
  8. The correct answer is B (The requirement to notify CBP of the address change within the required timeframe): the customs broker regulations require a broker to promptly notify CBP whenever business information such as the office address changes, so CBP's records stay accurate. A simple relocation within the same city does not require reapplying for an entirely new license. The continuous bond itself is generally unaffected by an address change and does not need to be reissued for this reason alone. CBP does not automatically track a broker's address changes, so relying only on client notification would leave CBP's own records outdated.
  9. The correct answer is D (The MPF is capped at both a statutory minimum and a statutory maximum dollar amount): the ad valorem MPF calculation on a formal entry is bounded on both ends, so a very low calculated fee is raised to the statutory minimum and a very high calculated fee is reduced to the statutory maximum. Describing only a maximum with no minimum, or only a minimum with no maximum, each captures just one side of the actual dual-cap structure. Treating the fee as an uncapped flat percentage ignores that both bounds exist and can each change the amount actually owed. New brokers must check both caps every time before finalizing the MPF line on a 7501.
  10. The correct answer is A (Five years from the date the entry or activity was completed): the customs broker recordkeeping regulations generally require brokers to retain records related to their customs business for five years from the date of the entry or other activity. One year and two years are both shorter than the actual required period and would leave the broker exposed during a later CBP audit. Ten years overstates the general requirement, though certain specific records can carry different retention triggers. Maintaining records for the full required period protects the broker if CBP later requests documentation to support a prior filing.
  11. The correct answer is C (A continuous bond covering all entries made during the bond period): a continuous bond covers all of an importer's entries at any port for the duration of the bond period, making it well suited to an importer with frequent, ongoing shipments. A single transaction bond only covers one specific entry and would require the importer to obtain a new bond for every shipment throughout the year. A carrier bond covers the carrier's own obligations and does not substitute for the importer's bonding requirement. Many entries, including some informal ones, can still require a bond depending on the circumstances, so bonding cannot simply be avoided based on entry type alone.
  12. The correct answer is D (The harbor maintenance fee applies only to the ocean shipment, not the truck shipment): HMF is assessed specifically on cargo loaded or unloaded at a port subject to the fee, which limits it to ocean shipments and excludes truck, rail, and air entries. Merchandise processing fees generally apply based on entry type rather than mode of transport, so truck entries do not automatically pay a higher MPF. Duty rates are determined by the HTSUS classification and origin, not by the mode of transport used to bring the goods into the country. Truck shipments are not automatically MPF-exempt simply because they cross a land border from Canada.
  13. The correct answer is C (A general power of attorney authorizing the broker to act as the importer's agent): the customs broker regulations require a valid power of attorney before a broker may transact customs business on a client's behalf. This document establishes the broker's legal authority to sign and file entries and other documents for the importer. A bill of lading, commercial invoice, and certificate of origin are each separate documents tied to the shipment itself rather than to the broker's authority to act. Without a properly executed power of attorney on file, the broker generally cannot lawfully represent the importer before CBP.
  14. The correct answer is D (The duty of responsible supervision and control over the firm's customs business): the customs broker regulations require a licensed broker to actually supervise employees who conduct customs business, not merely delegate tasks without oversight. Failing to review employee work or provide guidance is a direct breach of this responsible supervision and control obligation. A continuous bond, the triennial status report, and displaying a license number are each separate, unrelated broker obligations that this scenario does not describe. CBP can take disciplinary action against a broker who fails to exercise adequate supervision, even if no individual entry error has yet occurred.
  15. The correct answer is A ($377.12, combining the calculated MPF and HMF together): although duty is $0.00 because the merchandise qualifies for duty-free treatment under the trade preference program, MPF and HMF are still assessed on the entered value. MPF is $80,000 times 0.3464%, or $277.12, and HMF is $80,000 times 0.125%, or $100.00. Adding $277.12 and $100.00 gives a total of $377.12 still owed. Duty-free status only eliminates the duty line, not the separately authorized processing and harbor maintenance fees.
  16. The correct answer is D (The merchandise processing fee CBP charges for processing this entry): MPF is a separate user fee, distinct from duty, that CBP assesses to fund the cost of processing formal and informal entries. It appears as its own line on the 7501 alongside, not combined with, the regular duty calculation. Internal revenue tax and bond amounts are tracked separately from the MPF line entirely. Brokers must calculate MPF independently using the applicable ad valorem rate or flat fee rather than folding it into the duty figure.
  17. The correct answer is B ($1,534.64, combining the duty amount with the calculated MPF): duty is $10,000 times 15%, or $1,500. MPF is $10,000 times 0.3464%, or $34.64, which falls within the statutory range. Adding $1,500 and $34.64 gives a total of $1,534.64 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is a truck shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  18. The correct answer is A ($611.96, combining the total duty with the calculated MPF): the three line items produce duty of $100, $280, and $180, for a combined duty of $560. MPF is calculated on the combined entered value of $15,000 times 0.3464%, or $51.96. Adding $560 and $51.96 gives a total of $611.96 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is a truck shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  19. The correct answer is B ($2,635.42, combining regular duty, countervailing duty, MPF, and HMF): regular duty is $16,000 times 4%, or $640, and the countervailing duty is $16,000 times 12%, or $1,920. MPF is $16,000 times 0.3464%, or $55.42, and HMF is $16,000 times 0.125%, or $20.00. Adding $640, $1,920, $55.42, and $20.00 gives a total of $2,635.42 owed. Countervailing duties are assessed in addition to regular duty and must appear as a separate line on the entry summary.
  20. The correct answer is C ($8,447.12, applying the statutory MPF cap of $634.62): duty is $250,000 times 3%, or $7,500. The raw MPF calculation of $250,000 times 0.3464% equals $866.00, but this exceeds the statutory maximum, so the $634.62 cap applies instead. HMF is $250,000 times 0.125%, or $312.50, since this is an ocean shipment. Adding $7,500, $634.62, and $312.50 gives a total of $8,447.12 owed.
  21. The correct answer is A ($2,207.84, combining the specific duty with the calculated MPF): the specific duty is $0.50 per kilogram times 4,000 kilograms, or $2,000. MPF is still calculated on the ad valorem entered value of $60,000 times 0.3464%, or $207.84, since MPF applies regardless of whether duty is specific or ad valorem. Adding $2,000 and $207.84 gives a total of $2,207.84 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is an air shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  22. The correct answer is D ($1,308.50, combining the duty amount with the calculated MPF): duty is $14,000 times 9%, or $1,260. MPF is $14,000 times 0.3464%, or $48.50, which falls within the statutory range. Adding $1,260 and $48.50 gives a total of $1,308.50 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is an air shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  23. The correct answer is B ($1,142.35, combining the duty amount with the calculated MPF): duty is $18,000 times 6%, or $1,080. MPF is $18,000 times 0.3464%, or $62.35, which falls within the statutory range. Adding $1,080 and $62.35 gives a total of $1,142.35 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is a truck shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  24. The correct answer is A ($6,934.62, the duty, the capped processing fee, and the harbor fee combined): entered value is $240,000, made up of the $200,000 invoice price plus the $25,000 mold assist and the $15,000 royalty, with the separately identified freight excluded. Duty is 2.5 percent of $240,000, or $6,000, and the raw MPF of $831.36 exceeds the $634.62 maximum, so the cap applies. HMF is 0.125 percent of $240,000, or $300.00, because the entry is an ocean shipment, and adding $6,000, $634.62, and $300.00 gives $6,934.62 owed.
  25. The correct answer is D ($94.10, combining the duty amount with the flat MPF fee): duty is $2,200 times 4%, or $88.00. Because this is an informal entry, the flat $6.10 MPF fee applies rather than the ad valorem calculation used for formal entries. Adding $88.00 and $6.10 gives a total of $94.10 owed. No harbor maintenance fee applies because this is an air shipment rather than an ocean shipment.
  26. The correct answer is A ($2,780.13, the duty, the processing fee, and the federal excise tax combined): duty is three percent of $52,000, or $1,560.00, and MPF is 0.3464 percent of $52,000, or $180.13, which falls within the statutory range. The federal excise tax of $1,040 is an additional charge collected on the entry and is added along with the other amounts. Summing $1,560.00, $180.13, and $1,040 gives $2,780.13, and no harbor maintenance fee applies because this shipment arrived by air.
  27. The correct answer is C ($2,617.85, combining duty, MPF, and HMF together fully): duty is $25,000 times 10%, or $2,500. MPF is $25,000 times 0.3464%, or $86.60, which falls within the statutory range. HMF is $25,000 times 0.125%, or $31.25, since this is an ocean shipment. Adding $2,500, $86.60, and $31.25 gives a total of $2,617.85 owed.
  28. The correct answer is B ($2,237.13, combining duty, MPF, and HMF together fully): duty is $45,000 times 4.5%, or $2,025. MPF is $45,000 times 0.3464%, or $155.88, which falls within the statutory range. HMF is $45,000 times 0.125%, or $56.25, since this is an ocean shipment. Adding $2,025, $155.88, and $56.25 gives a total of $2,237.13 owed.
  29. The correct answer is A (The duty changes even though the HTSUS number and rate stay the same): the duty in column 35 results from applying the rate to entered value, so an overstated value produces an overstated duty even when the classification and rate are right. The chapter notes that a value error has the same cascading effect as a classification error. A voyage by vessel affects HMF rather than whether a value error changes the duty. CBP does not need to change the rate for the duty figure to be wrong.
  30. The correct answer is B (6%, the rate that produces exactly $1,800 in duty owed): dividing the duty paid of $1,800 by the entered value of $30,000 yields 0.06, or 6%. Checking the math, $30,000 times 6% equals exactly $1,800, confirming the effective rate. The 5%, 8%, and 4% figures would each produce a different duty amount than the $1,800 actually paid. Brokers frequently need to reverse-engineer a duty rate this way when verifying a client's prior filings.
  31. The correct answer is C ($4,853.55, combining duty, MPF, and HMF together fully): duty is $75,000 times 6%, or $4,500. MPF is $75,000 times 0.3464%, or $259.80, which falls within the statutory range. HMF is $75,000 times 0.125%, or $93.75, since this is an ocean shipment. Adding $4,500, $259.80, and $93.75 gives a total of $4,853.55 owed.
  32. The correct answer is A ($3,000.00, multiplying the entered value by the eight percent rate): column 35 receives the dollar result of applying the column 34 rate to the entered value, and eight percent of $37,500 is $3,000.00. The other figures correspond to different rates applied to the same value and would reflect a different HTSUS provision than the one described. Classification comes first because the classification controls the rate, and the rate then controls the duty recorded in column 35. A broker should confirm the rate against the HTSUS provision before doing the multiplication.
  33. The correct answer is B ($8,884.62, applying the statutory MPF cap of $634.62): duty is $200,000 times 4%, or $8,000. The raw MPF calculation of $200,000 times 0.3464% equals $692.80, but this exceeds the $634.62 statutory maximum, so the cap applies. HMF is $200,000 times 0.125%, or $250.00, since this is an ocean shipment. Adding $8,000, $634.62, and $250.00 gives a total of $8,884.62 owed.
  34. The correct answer is C ($272.71, applying the statutory minimum MPF of $32.71): duty is $8,000 times 3%, or $240.00. The ad valorem MPF calculation of $8,000 times 0.3464% yields only $27.71, which is below the $32.71 statutory minimum, so the minimum applies instead. Since this is a truck shipment, no harbor maintenance fee is assessed at all. Adding $240.00 and $32.71 gives a total of $272.71 owed.
  35. The correct answer is A (The broker must make the records available to CBP upon lawful request): the chapter states that a broker must keep records of transactions for the period required by law and must make those records available to CBP upon lawful request. Client permission is not a condition for complying with a lawful request of this kind. Producing only the records CBP happens to name, or withholding records because they contain pricing information, does not satisfy the duty. Recordkeeping allows CBP to verify after the fact that the broker's other duties were performed.
  36. The correct answer is D ($4,200.00, the duty computed from the net quantity in HTSUS units): a specific rate in dollars per kilogram is applied to the net quantity in HTSUS units, which column 32 carries, so 12,000 kilograms times thirty-five cents equals $4,200.00. The 13,500 kilogram gross weight shown in column 30 describes the shipment's weight for statistical reporting and does not drive this duty. The other two figures come from applying rates of ten cents or forty-five cents rather than the stated rate. Using the right quantity column prevents an overstatement or understatement of the duty entered in column 35.
  37. The correct answer is C ($106.18, the duty, the flat processing fee, and the excise charge combined): duty is five percent of $1,800, or $90.00, and an informal entry pays the flat merchandise processing fee of $2.18 rather than an ad valorem calculation. The excise charge of $14.00 applies to the entry and is added along with the other amounts. Adding $90.00, $2.18, and $14.00 gives $106.18, and no harbor maintenance fee applies because the goods arrived by mail rather than by vessel.
  38. The correct answer is D ($60,000, which produces exactly two thousand seven hundred dollars in duty): dividing the duty of $2,700 by the rate of 4.5 percent gives an entered value of $60,000, and checking forward, $60,000 times 4.5 percent equals $2,700. The other values each correspond to a different duty amount under the same rate, so none of them matches the charge shown on the entry. Working backward this way lets a broker verify entered value when the duty figure is known. Entered value is the figure reported in column 33, while the duty appears in column 35.
  39. The correct answer is C ($96,000, the invoice price plus the royalty and the molds, with freight excluded): entered value starts with the $80,000 price, adds statutory additions of $4,000 for the royalty and $12,000 for the buyer-supplied molds, and excludes the separately identified freight of $5,000. That produces $96,000 for column 33. Leaving out an addition, or adding the freight, produces a different figure such as $84,000, $92,000, or $101,000. A broker should work through additions and exclusions one at a time to avoid mixing them up.
  40. The correct answer is A (No HMF applies because the merchandise does not move by vessel): HMF is assessed on cargo loaded or unloaded at a port that benefits from federal harbor maintenance, which means shipments moving by vessel through a US port. Cargo that moves by rail across a land border is outside that description, so no harbor maintenance fee is calculated for this entry. Crossing an international border by itself does not trigger the fee, and there is no reduced rail rate. A later transfer to a vessel would involve its own movement rather than making HMF apply retroactively to this rail entry.
  41. The correct answer is B (The entry number, the entry type code, the port, and the entry date): the header identifies the entry number, the entry type code, the port of entry, and the entry date, along with the importer of record number and consignee number. Entered value, duty and internal revenue tax, and net quantity in HTSUS units are all reported in the line-item area of the form. A broker completes the header first so the entry is tied to the right shipment, port, and filing date. Knowing which fields belong in the header helps a broker audit a form before it is submitted.
  42. The correct answer is D (The commercial invoice, the packing list, and the bill of lading or airway bill): the chapter directs a broker completing Form 7501 to cross-check every figure against the underlying commercial invoice, packing list, and any bill of lading or airway bill. Those documents carry the description, quantity, and value information that support the line items. A tax return, commission statement, or fee schedule does not establish the shipment's classification, quantity, or value. Checking the source documents before filing is part of the broker's duty to use reasonable care in the information reported to CBP.
  43. The correct answer is A (Apply GRI 3, beginning with GRI 3(a) and then GRI 3(b)): because the goods are prima facie classifiable under more than one heading, the analysis moves from GRI 1 to GRI 3, and the parts of GRI 3 are applied in order. GRI 3(a) looks first for the most specific description, and GRI 3(b) is reached only when GRI 3(a) does not resolve the matter, as happens with a set whose headings each cover only part of the goods. Skipping straight to essential character, repeating GRI 1, or choosing the lowest rate all depart from the sequence the rules require. Following the order shows how the final heading was reached.
  44. The correct answer is C (The sum of duty plus all applicable fees and taxes owed on the entry): the chapter describes the bottom figure as the total amount due, formed by adding duty to all applicable fees and taxes. That includes the merchandise processing fee, the harbor maintenance fee where it applies, and any antidumping or countervailing duty deposits. Duty alone, or the two common fees alone, would understate what the importer must pay. Entered value with freight added describes a valuation calculation rather than the amount owed.
  45. The correct answer is D (Refuse the instruction and report the true transaction value on the entry): the chapter states that the correct answer in every scenario where a client asks a broker to underdeclare value or misclassify goods is that the broker must refuse and must not file the incorrect information, regardless of instructions from the client. Responsibility for the entry does not shift to the importer because the broker signs and files it. Reporting a false value with a disclosure letter, or with a seller-signed revised invoice, still places an incorrect value before CBP. The broker should report the true value and explain the duty to the client.
  46. The correct answer is A ($30,000, the invoice price combined with the tooling value supplied by the importer): entered value begins with the price actually paid or payable of $21,000, and the free tooling is an assist whose full $9,000 value must be added because the buyer supplied it for use in producing the imported goods. The separately stated international freight and insurance of $1,800 is not part of dutiable value and is excluded rather than added. Adding the freight to the invoice price would produce $22,800, and adding both the tooling and the freight would produce $39,000, so both of those figures overstate the entered value. Column 33 of Form 7501 should show entered value of $30,000.
  47. The correct answer is C (The importer of record and consignee numbers may identify the same or different parties): the chapter states that these two header entries may be the same party or different parties depending on how the transaction was structured. A retail importer and a separate distribution center are a normal example of two different parties appearing in those fields. The idea that the numbers must always match, or that the consignee number replaces the importer of record number, does not reflect how the form is completed. The importer of record number is required for the entry, and the consignee number is reported separately when the receiving party differs.
  48. The correct answer is A (File for a refund of the duties paid during the lapse period after renewal.): the chapter states that GSP has periodically lapsed when Congress did not renew it before the prior authorization expired, and that renewal has sometimes been retroactive, allowing importers to file for refunds of duties paid during the lapse period once the program is reinstated. GSP is not a quota program, so entries are not converted into quota entries. The refund is claimed from the United States government rather than from the foreign exporter. Ignoring the retroactive refund opportunity is one of the practical risks the chapter warns brokers about.
  49. The correct answer is D (Column 35, which reports the duty and internal revenue tax amount for the line): column 35 holds the dollar figure that results from applying the rate in column 34 to the value in column 33 or the quantity in column 32. Column 34 holds only the rate, and column 33 holds only the declared entered value, so neither one shows the computed duty. Column 29 holds the classification number that determines which rate applies. Reading the line-item area in order helps a broker see how classification, value, rate, and computed duty connect.
  50. The correct answer is B (The component's value, bulk, and role in the activity the set serves): the chapter identifies the electric clipper as giving a grooming set its essential character because it performs the primary function, represents the highest value and most significant bulk, and is the reason a purchaser buys the set. Those substantive factors guide the same judgment in any set. Display order, the length of an invoice description, and the duty rate do not measure essential character. The duty rate follows from the classification rather than determining it.
  51. The correct answer is A (Whether the imported concentrate was transformed into a different article before export.): manufacturing drawback applies where imported merchandise is used to manufacture or produce an article that is then exported or destroyed, while unused merchandise drawback applies where the goods leave essentially unchanged. The determining fact is whether the imported concentrate became part of a new article, which it did when the suppression units were assembled. Warehouse storage, a visa, and the buyer's country do not change which drawback category applies. The broker must identify whether the goods were used or transformed before choosing the provision.
  52. The correct answer is B (Each article is classified under its own heading because the goods are not a retail set): the set definition requires articles put up together to meet a particular need or activity and packaged for sale directly to users without repacking, and goods individually packaged and priced for separate retail sale do not meet that description. Sharing a master carton for freight convenience does not turn unrelated articles into a set. Applying the most valuable article's heading would be the essential character approach, which is reached only for genuine retail sets. The mode of transportation does not change how the goods are classified.
  53. The correct answer is D (When the cargo is unladen from the vessel for the import shipment): the chapter states that HMF is applied at the time the cargo is unladen from the vessel for an import shipment. Ordering merchandise from a seller, and the departure of the vessel from a foreign port, both occur before the cargo reaches the US port and are not the assessment point. Liquidation comes much later and concerns the final determination of duties rather than the application of this fee. Identifying the correct moment keeps the fee tied to the ocean movement that justifies it.
  54. The correct answer is A (Substitution unused merchandise drawback under section 1313(j), covering destruction as well as export.): the chapter states that substituted merchandise may stand in for the imports so long as it is exported or destroyed within the required time frame, and section 1313(j) covers both outcomes. Because the imported and other almonds share the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading and specific lots cannot be traced, the claim rests on substitution rather than direct identification. Surplus inventory is a business condition, not a nonconformity or shipment error, so section 1313(c) does not apply. The almonds were destroyed without being manufactured into a new article, so the manufacturing provisions are not the right category.
  55. The correct answer is C ($25,759.80, the regular duty, the antidumping deposit, and the processing fee): regular duty is four percent of $75,000, or $3,000, and the antidumping deposit is thirty percent of $75,000, or $22,500. MPF is 0.3464 percent of $75,000, or $259.80, which falls within the statutory range. Adding $3,000, $22,500, and $259.80 gives the total of $25,759.80 owed, and no harbor maintenance fee applies because the shipment arrived by truck.
  56. The correct answer is C (Articles classifiable in different headings, meeting one need, packaged for retail): the chapter describes a set as at least two different articles that are prima facie classifiable in different headings, are put together to meet a particular need or activity, and are packaged for sale directly to users without repacking. Articles falling in the same heading do not present the two-heading question that the set rule addresses. Meeting several unrelated needs, or being sold without retail packaging, falls outside the described conditions. Matching all three conditions lets a broker reach GRI 3(b) properly.
  57. The correct answer is A (Drawback applies because a shipment to Canada counts as an export from the United States.): drawback is granted when imported merchandise, or a substitute, is subsequently exported from the United States or destroyed under CBP supervision. A shipment to a foreign buyer in Canada is an export, and the mode of transport or the presence of a land border does not change that. The goods also satisfy the unused condition because they were never used in the United States. The availability of the claim does not depend on Canada granting the buyer a matching refund.
  58. The correct answer is B ($2,347.06, the specific duty combined with the merchandise processing fee): the specific duty is $1.20 per dozen multiplied by 1,800 dozen, which equals $2,160.00. MPF is 0.3464 percent of the $54,000 entered value, or $187.06, and that figure falls between the statutory minimum and maximum so no adjustment is needed. Adding $2,160.00 and $187.06 gives $2,347.06. No harbor maintenance fee applies because the merchandise arrived by air rather than by vessel.
  59. The correct answer is D (Withholding the information is treated the same as a false statement): the chapter states that the duty of honesty extends to withholding information the broker knows would change the determination CBP makes, and silence in the face of known inaccuracy is treated the same as an affirmative false statement. The client's role as the source of the facts does not excuse the broker. Waiting for CBP to ask, or documenting the issue only for the client, does not satisfy the duty to report accurately. The broker should not participate in concealing information that affects classification.
  60. The correct answer is A (Production records, bills of materials, and inventory accounting.): the chapter states that direct identification manufacturing drawback requires a documented, traceable link between the particular import entry and the particular export, often supported by detailed production records, bills of materials, and inventory accounting. Those records prove that the specific imported granite went into the specific exported countertops. Textile visas and quota category numbers belong to the quota and textile sections of the chapter, not to drawback documentation. A regional value content calculation is a free trade agreement origin concept and does not establish the import to export link.
  61. The correct answer is B (The headings are regarded as equally specific because each covers part only): the chapter explains that when two or more headings each refer to only part of the items in a set, those headings are regarded as equally specific and GRI 3(a) does not resolve the classification. Value, order of appearance in the tariff schedule, and the duty rate are not the measures GRI 3(a) uses. Because GRI 3(a) leaves the question open, the analysis continues to GRI 3(b), which classifies a retail set by the component giving it essential character. Recognizing this step prevents a broker from stopping at a heading that describes only part of the goods.
  62. The correct answer is C (Column 29, the line-item column holding the ten-digit HTSUS classification number): the chapter places the ten-digit HTSUS number in column 29 of the line-item area, and that number drives the applicable duty rate. Columns 30, 31, and 32 report gross weight, manifest quantity, and net quantity in HTSUS units, which are quantity and weight fields rather than the classification field. Finding the classification in its own column lets a reviewer trace how the rate in column 34 was selected. A wrong classification number in column 29 changes both the rate and the duty owed on the entry.
  63. The correct answer is D (Column 28, which carries the short merchandise description for the line): column 28 of Form 7501 is the description of merchandise, a short description that matches the invoice for that line. Column 29 holds the ten-digit HTSUS classification number, column 33 holds entered value, and column 35 holds duty and internal revenue tax, so each of those answers points to a different field. A broker reading the line-item area needs the description in its own column to confirm the entry matches the commercial documents. An inaccurate description can signal that the wrong classification or value was reported.
  64. The correct answer is A (Accounting and recordkeeping standards showing the substitution relationship and quantities.): the chapter states that a claimant relying on substitution cannot trace individual units and instead relies on accounting and recordkeeping standards that demonstrate the substitution relationship and the quantities involved. That approach reflects the practical reality of a manufacturer drawing from a mixed pool of imported and domestic material of the same specification. A bill of lading naming each imported sheet in each exported frame describes direct identification, which the facts rule out. Supplier statements and textile visas are not the evidence the chapter identifies for substitution manufacturing drawback.
  65. The correct answer is D (GRI 1, which looks to the terms of the headings and the notes): the chapter states that classification is determined first by the terms of the headings and any relevant section or chapter notes, and GRI 1 is applied before any later rule. GRI 3 enters the analysis only when goods are prima facie classifiable under two or more headings, which is not the case for an article fully described by one heading. GRI 3(a) and GRI 3(b) are parts of that later rule and do not displace GRI 1. Applying the rules in sequence keeps the reasoning defensible if CBP later reviews the entry.
  66. The correct answer is C (The fee stops increasing once the calculated amount reaches the maximum): the ad valorem MPF has both a floor and a ceiling, so a very high value shipment pays no more than the ceiling amount no matter how large entered value grows. The calculated amount is compared against the maximum before the fee is finalized on the entry summary. The fee does not keep rising with value, does not drop back to the minimum, and does not disappear once value passes the cap. A broker checks both bounds every time rather than assuming the straight percentage always governs.
  67. The correct answer is A (Direct identification manufacturing drawback under section 1313(a), because the lots were traced.): the imported cartons were printed and folded into a new article, and that article was exported, so the transaction falls under manufacturing drawback. Because the importer can trace the specific imported carton lots into the specific exported boxes, section 1313(a) direct identification applies. Unused merchandise drawback requires the goods to leave in essentially the same condition, and printing and folding changed them into manufactured articles. The change in form is production rather than a defect or shipment error, so section 1313(c) is not the trigger.
  68. The correct answer is A (Drawback is a statutory privilege that requires strict recordkeeping and timely filing.): the chapter states that drawback is a privilege granted by statute and regulation, not an automatic entitlement, and that strict recordkeeping and timely filing are essential. A broker who mishandles drawback documentation can cost a client a substantial refund. The claimant must be able to establish the chain from import to export along with proof that duties were paid, and that obligation applies across the drawback categories. Export records support one part of the claim but do not replace the import records or the duty payment evidence.
  69. The correct answer is B (A certification of origin with the required data elements must be supportable by records.): the chapter states that to claim USMCA preference, the importer, exporter, or producer must possess a certification of origin containing specific minimum data elements set out in the agreement. USMCA does not require a single prescribed government form, but the certification must still contain the required elements and must be supportable by records. The importer claiming preferential treatment bears responsibility for exercising reasonable care and must be prepared to produce supporting documentation if CBP requests verification. A textile visa and a customs bond are not substitutes for the certification.
  70. The correct answer is D (The goods must meet an origin test and a value-added threshold for the country.): the chapter states that GSP treatment generally requires the merchandise to be a product of a designated beneficiary developing country, to be classified under a GSP eligible tariff provision, and to meet an origin and value-added test. The value added test normally requires the cost or value of materials produced in the beneficiary country plus the direct costs of processing there to equal at least a specified minimum percentage of the appraised value. Vessel registration, investor ownership, and a textile visa are not the requirements described. Materials from certain other beneficiary countries in the same regional grouping may sometimes count toward the threshold under GSP accumulation provisions.
  71. The correct answer is A (January 1, 2005, when the WTO phase-out took effect.): the chapter states that the old bilateral textile quota system was progressively phased out as global textile and apparel quotas were eliminated under WTO commitments effective January 1, 2005. Since that phase-out, most textile and apparel quotas that once dominated the trade have been eliminated. The broker should still watch for special safeguard mechanisms, country specific restrictions, and visa or certification requirements tied to particular agreements. The USMCA entry into force and the drawback modernization are separate developments and not the date of the textile quota phase-out.
  72. The correct answer is B (By the HTSUS subheading that carries the in-quota and over-quota rates.): the chapter states that the precise quota category is often expressed as a specific quota category number for textiles, or by HTSUS subheading for agricultural tariff-rate quotas. Filing against the wrong quota category can cause merchandise to be rejected from the current period even when the goods were available and compliant. A continuous bond number and a visa endorsement number are not the quota category identifiers described in the chapter. Textile quota category numbers apply to the textile side, not to agricultural tariff-rate quotas.
  73. The correct answer is C (The claim is generally filed within five years of importation, and the export must be timely.): the chapter states that a drawback claim generally must be filed within five years from the date of importation, while the claimant must also comply with any applicable requirement that the export or destruction itself occur within a specified period. The two clocks are separate, so a timely claim does not excuse a late export. The filing window runs from importation rather than export, and the port director does not set the export timing. Destruction is permitted under the drawback categories described in the chapter.
  74. The correct answer is A (Explain that drawback requires proof the duties were paid, so the claim is unsupported.): the chapter states that a drawback claimant must establish the chain connecting the imported merchandise to the later export along with proof that duties were originally paid. Export documents and inventory records alone do not establish the duty payment element. Nothing in the facts shows a nonconformity or a shipment without the consignee's consent, so rejected merchandise procedures are not a substitute route. A missing element is not cured by waiting, because the filing window runs from the date of importation.
  75. The correct answer is C (Proration removes the incentive for importers to wait at the port until midnight.): the chapter explains that the proration rule matters because it removes the incentive to physically camp outside a port at midnight, since everyone who is timely and properly presented on opening day shares proportionally in whatever quantity is available. Proration spreads the available quantity rather than rewarding the fastest filer. It does not change the duty rate and does not generate a penalty. The rule exists to make the opening fair among importers competing for the same limited pool.
  76. The correct answer is A (Substitution manufacturing drawback under section 1313(b), because mixed same subheading yarn was used.): the mill used imported yarn and domestic yarn of the same kind and quality, and both fell under the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading. Because production drew from a shared pool, the specific imported yarn cannot be traced into specific exported rolls, which is the situation section 1313(b) addresses. Direct identification under section 1313(a) requires that traceable link, and the facts rule it out. The yarn was woven into canvas, so the goods were not exported in the same condition and unused merchandise drawback does not apply.
  77. The correct answer is C (It showed the shipment was authorized for export and counted against the country's quota category.): the chapter explains that the exporting country's visa was presented along with the entry documentation and served as evidence that the shipment was authorized for export and counted properly against that country's allotted quota category. It was an endorsement, stamp, or export authorization issued by the exporting country's government. The visa did not replace the entry summary, confirm that CBP had already counted the goods, or act as a customs bond. The historical visa system was phased out with global textile and apparel quotas, though limited requirements can still appear.
  78. The correct answer is D (Entry may proceed now at the higher over-quota rate listed in the HTSUS.): under a tariff-rate quota, merchandise entered after the quota quantity is filled can still be entered for consumption at the higher over-quota rate. The lower in-quota rate applies only while quota room remains. Storage until the next period is a choice associated with an absolute quota, not a requirement of a filled tariff-rate quota. Proration applies to absolute quota entries presented simultaneously while room remains, so it is not the rule for this shipment.
  79. The correct answer is A (Rejected merchandise drawback, because the goods were shipped without the consignee's consent): the chapter states that rejected merchandise drawback applies to imported merchandise that was shipped without the consent of the consignee. The category also covers merchandise that does not conform to sample or specification or that is found defective at the time of importation. The trigger is a nonconformity or an error in the original shipment rather than an uneventful re-export. Unused merchandise drawback and manufacturing drawback categories do not turn on the lack of consent.
  80. The correct answer is B (TFTEA streamlined recordkeeping while claims still require supporting records.): the chapter states that TFTEA streamlined recordkeeping requirements, along with requiring electronic filing, but it did not remove the need to support a drawback claim with records. The claimant still has to establish the import to export link and the substitution relationship where that basis is used. TFTEA did not shift recordkeeping authority to the exporter, since CBP continues to administer drawback. The recordkeeping obligation applies across the drawback categories, not only to rejected merchandise claims.
  81. The correct answer is D (Entry may proceed at the higher over-quota rate because a TRQ does not block entry.): the chapter states that with a tariff-rate quota, entry is never legally blocked by the quota itself, because only the duty rate changes once the quota quantity is filled. A broker who treats a tariff-rate quota like an absolute quota may wrongly tell a client the goods cannot enter at all. Proration applies to absolute quota entries presented simultaneously while quota room remains, not to a filled tariff-rate quota. Placement in a foreign trade zone is one option for merchandise that cannot enter under an absolute quota, not a condition for a tariff-rate quota entry.
  82. The correct answer is A (Textile trade remains subject to detailed rules of origin even where quotas ended): the chapter states that the exam expects a broker to recognize that textile trade is still subject to detailed rules of origin even where broad quantitative quotas no longer apply. Special safeguard mechanisms and country specific restrictions tied to particular trade agreements can still appear. Visa or certification requirements can also still appear for specific product categories or trading partners. The remaining options describe a total absence of textile rules or an unsupported origin test.
  83. The correct answer is C (19 USC 1313, the drawback statute, which covers the refund categories.): the chapter states that the statutory foundation for drawback is 19 USC 1313. The same section contains the unused merchandise, manufacturing, and rejected merchandise categories described in the chapter. Tariff classification rules and quota category listings serve other purposes and are not the drawback foundation. The USMCA certification rules govern preferential origin claims rather than the refund of duties.
  84. The correct answer is D (Classification under the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading as the imported goods.): TFTEA moved the drawback system toward the objective standard that merchandise classified under the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading is eligible for substitution. That replaced much of the fact intensive, sometimes subjective analysis that the older commercially interchangeable and same kind and quality tests required. Supplier certifications do not establish the statutory substitution basis. The broker should compare the tariff classification of the imported and substituted merchandise before filing the claim.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Not every HTSUS provision is eligible, so product eligibility must be checked): the chapter states that merchandise must be classified under a tariff provision that is eligible for GSP treatment and that not every HTSUS provision is GSP eligible. The broker should confirm the country and tariff provision are currently eligible. Country eligibility can also change for reasons such as graduation or competitive need limitations. The general column rate and any foreign ministry list are not the eligibility test the chapter describes.
  2. The correct answer is B (Visa or certification requirements can still appear for specific categories or trading partners.): the chapter states that most textile and apparel quotas were eliminated after the January 1, 2005 phase-out, but special safeguard mechanisms, country specific restrictions tied to particular trade agreements, and visa or certification requirements can still appear for specific product categories or trading partners. A blanket statement that no textile shipment can ever require certification is therefore wrong. The phase-out applied under World Trade Organization commitments rather than only to countries that joined the organization. Textile articles as well as apparel can be covered by the remaining rules.
  3. The correct answer is D (September 12, 2025, five years after the date of importation.): the chapter states that a drawback claim generally must be filed within five years from the date of importation of the merchandise on which drawback is claimed. Five years after September 12, 2020 is September 12, 2025. The export date does not start the filing clock for the claim, although the claimant must also satisfy any separate timing requirement for the export or destruction. The chapter also advises building in a margin of safety rather than filing at the last possible moment.
  4. The correct answer is B (Direct identification manufacturing drawback under section 1313(a), because traced imports became destroyed articles.): manufacturing drawback applies where imported merchandise is used to manufacture an article in the United States and that manufactured article is subsequently exported or destroyed. The imported sheet was formed into a new article, the sinks, and those sinks were destroyed under CBP supervision. Because the importer can trace the specific imported sheet into the destroyed sinks, section 1313(a) direct identification applies. The nonconformity here arose in the manufactured article rather than in the imported merchandise at entry, so rejected merchandise drawback is not the trigger.
  5. The correct answer is C (Rejected merchandise drawback does not apply because the goods conformed to specification.): section 1313(c) is triggered by nonconformity to sample or specification, shipment without the consignee's consent, or merchandise found defective at importation, and none of those facts is present. A cancelled order is an ordinary business reason rather than a rejection trigger. The chapter draws that distinction directly when it separates routine re-exports of acceptable goods from nonconformity cases. Unused merchandise drawback may still be available if the inserts are exported or destroyed unused in essentially the same condition.
  6. The correct answer is A (Whether the reason for export was a defect or ordinary business decision making): the chapter explains that the distinguishing fact for section 1313(c) is the nonconformity and rejection. An ordinary re-export of acceptable goods falls under unused merchandise drawback instead. A broker walking through a fact pattern should identify whether the goods were used or manufactured into something else and whether the export stemmed from a defect or from business decision making. Quota status, the method of duty payment, and the mode of transport are not the controlling facts.
  7. The correct answer is D (Unused merchandise drawback under section 1313(j), because the goods were destroyed unused.): unused merchandise drawback covers merchandise exported or destroyed in essentially the same condition without having been used in the United States. The hoses stayed in their original coils and were destroyed under CBP supervision, which satisfies the destruction option in section 1313(j). Surplus inventory is an ordinary business situation rather than a nonconformity or shipment error, so rejected merchandise drawback does not apply. No manufactured article was produced from the hoses, so the manufacturing provisions are not the right category.
  8. The correct answer is C (TFTEA kept the basic categories and modernized substitution measurement and electronic filing.): the chapter states that TFTEA did not eliminate the underlying drawback categories but modernized how substitution is measured and how claims move through the system. The basic structure of unused merchandise drawback, manufacturing drawback, and rejected merchandise drawback under 19 USC 1313 remains intact. TFTEA pushed substitution toward the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading standard and required electronic filing in the Automated Commercial Environment. CBP continues to administer drawback, so authority did not move to the Department of Commerce.
  9. The correct answer is A (Drawback is a privilege granted by statute and regulation, with strict records required): the chapter states that drawback is a privilege granted by statute and regulation rather than an automatic entitlement. Strict recordkeeping and timely filing are essential to a claim. A broker who mishandles drawback documentation can cost a client a substantial refund. Automatic payment, a post protest judicial remedy, and a quota benefit are not how the chapter describes drawback.
  10. The correct answer is B (Brief processing that does not amount to use does not disqualify unused merchandise drawback.): the chapter explains that unused merchandise drawback can apply where goods sat in inventory or were briefly processed in ways that do not amount to use or manufacture. Inspecting, labeling, and repacking belts for export fit that description when the goods remain in essentially the same condition. The goods were not transformed into a new article, so manufacturing drawback is not the correct category. No nonconformity, shipment error, or defect at importation is present, so rejected merchandise drawback is also unavailable.
  11. The correct answer is C (Presentation requires that the entry actually be filed with CBP with complete documentation.): the chapter states that merchandise is not considered presented merely because a vessel is in port or a conveyance has crossed the border, because the entry must actually be filed with CBP. Presentation generally requires that the merchandise has arrived within the port limits, that the entry summary or request for entry has been filed, and that the required documentation is complete. Arrival of the vessel or unloading of the goods alone does not satisfy the requirement. Filing against the correct quota category is part of presenting the shipment so it counts properly.
  12. The correct answer is A (The HTSUS chapter level, heading level, or subheading level): the chapter states that a tariff shift rule is stated in terms of the HTSUS chapter, heading, or subheading level. A rule might require that non-originating materials be classified in a different chapter than the finished good. Another rule might require a shift from one specific heading to another. Quota categories, statistical suffixes, and country codes are not the classification levels used to express a tariff shift.
  13. The correct answer is C (Filing against the wrong quota category or filing late can remove goods from the period.): the chapter warns that filing against the wrong quota category, or filing late, can cause merchandise to be rejected from the current period even when the physical goods were available and compliant. The broker must know the correct entry type and the precise quota category, whether that is a textile category number or an HTSUS subheading for an agricultural tariff-rate quota. CBP administers quotas rather than the exporting country, although some programs involve foreign government licensing. A missing textile visa is a separate historical concept and not the principle that explains this rejection.
  14. The correct answer is B (The export must have occurred within the required time frame for substituted merchandise.): the chapter states that substituted merchandise may be used for drawback so long as it is exported or destroyed within the required time frame, which is separate from the five year claim filing window. A substitution claim can satisfy the subheading match and still fail if the export occurred outside that period. Sale price above import value, a temporary importation bond, and a textile visa are not conditions the chapter attaches to substitution unused merchandise drawback. The broker should confirm both the substitution basis and the timing before filing.
  15. The correct answer is A (More than one method may be used, each with its own threshold by product): the chapter states that USMCA permits calculation of regional value content using more than one method depending on the product. Those methods include a transaction value method and a net cost method. Each method has its own formula and its own specified threshold percentage that varies by product category. A single ten percent threshold, a retail price basis, and a post liquidation calculation by Customs are not what the chapter describes.
  16. The correct answer is D (Export them, warehouse or zone them, or destroy them under CBP supervision.): when merchandise subject to an absolute quota arrives after the quota is closed, the chapter gives the importer those three choices for the excess goods. The goods may be exported, placed in a bonded warehouse or a foreign trade zone to await the next quota period, or destroyed under CBP supervision. An absolute quota has no over-quota rate, so paying a higher duty rate is not a way to obtain release. Proration applies to entries presented at the same time while quota room still exists, not to a shipment arriving after the quota is closed.
  17. The correct answer is A (It served as evidence that the shipment counted against that country's quota category): the chapter states that the exporting country's visa was presented along with the entry documentation for that purpose. The visa was an endorsement, stamp, or export authorization issued by the government of the exporting country. It certified that the shipment of textiles or apparel was authorized for export to the United States under a bilateral quota agreement. Duty payment receipts, classification rulings, and warehouse receipts are different documents with different functions.
  18. The correct answer is B (The sugar may still enter for consumption at the higher over-quota duty rate.): a tariff-rate quota does not bar merchandise from entering once the quota quantity is reached, because only the duty rate changes. Merchandise entered within the quota quantity pays the lower in-quota rate, and merchandise entered after the quota fills pays the higher over-quota rate. Both rates appear in the Harmonized Tariff Schedule for the same tariff provision. Treating a tariff-rate quota like an absolute quota would wrongly tell the client that entry is impossible.
  19. The correct answer is C (Within five years from the date of importation of the merchandise.): the chapter states that a drawback claim generally must be filed within five years from the date of importation of the merchandise on which drawback is claimed. The clock therefore runs from importation, not from the export date. The claimant must also comply with any separate timing requirement for the export or destruction itself. Missing the filing deadline forfeits the refund even when the underlying transaction is well documented.
  20. The correct answer is A (Sugar, certain dairy products, and some categories of beef): the chapter names sugar, certain dairy products, and some beef categories as commonly cited tariff rate quota examples. The over-quota rate on some of these can be substantially higher than the in-quota rate. That gap gives importers a strong financial incentive to enter early or plan shipments around the opening date. Tuna is described as an absolute quota example, and the remaining commodities are not named in the chapter at all.
  21. The correct answer is B (Rejected merchandise drawback under section 1313(c), because the goods were defective when imported.): the chapter lists merchandise found to be defective at the time of importation as one of the triggers for rejected merchandise drawback. The failed impact standard is a nonconformity that led to the return of the shipment to the supplier. Section 1313(c) addresses that specific situation rather than the routine re-export of acceptable goods. Manufacturing drawback is unavailable because no exported article was produced from the helmets.
  22. The correct answer is D (Unused merchandise drawback under section 1313(j), because the goods were destroyed unused under CBP supervision.): unused merchandise drawback covers imported goods that are exported or destroyed in essentially the same condition without having been used in the United States. Section 1313(j) expressly includes destruction, and the chapter notes that destruction must occur under CBP supervision so the goods cannot slip into domestic commerce. The extract was never transformed into a different article, so manufacturing drawback is not the correct category. The drums were surplus rather than nonconforming, so rejected merchandise drawback is not the trigger.
  23. The correct answer is A (The quota has filled and the described merchandise cannot enter this period): Customs uses the term closed to describe an absolute quota that has already been filled. Once that ceiling is reached, no more of the product may enter the commerce of the United States for the remainder of the period. Duty rate is not the mechanism that controls an absolute quota, so paying more does not solve the problem. The closed status applies to the quota category itself rather than to a single port or to an audit hold.
  24. The correct answer is D (Substitution manufacturing drawback under section 1313(b), because mixed same subheading cells were used.): the imported cells and the domestic cells were drawn from one pool and used to manufacture exported articles, and the specific imported units could not be traced. Section 1313(b) exists for exactly that situation, where the manufacturer uses both imported and other merchandise of the same kind and quality or the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading. Direct identification under section 1313(a) fails because individual imported cells cannot be matched to specific exported packs. The cells were consumed in production, so the transaction does not fit unused merchandise drawback under section 1313(j).
  25. The correct answer is B (Rejected merchandise drawback under section 1313(c), because the goods failed to conform to specification.): the shipment did not match the contracted shade, and the goods were returned to the supplier, which puts the transaction squarely within the rejected merchandise category. Section 1313(c) addresses merchandise that does not conform to sample or specification, that was shipped without the consignee's consent, or that was defective at the time of importation. The key trigger is the nonconformity rather than an ordinary business decision to re-export acceptable goods. Manufacturing drawback is not available because the fabric was never used to produce an exported article.
  26. The correct answer is A (It may still create consumer deception concerns even though technical marking exists elsewhere, since the overall impression conveyed by labeling and branding must not mislead as to true origin): Even where accurate origin marking technically exists somewhere on a product, an overall labeling and branding presentation that creates a false or misleading impression about domestic manufacture can still raise marking law concerns, since marking law is concerned with preventing deception about true origin, not merely satisfying a technical checkbox. Simply having accurate text present somewhere does not automatically neutralize a misleading overall impression created by other prominent labeling elements. Brand name choices can be relevant to whether an overall presentation is misleading, and this scenario is directly connected to customs marking rules rather than being confined to nutrition labeling.
  27. The correct answer is D (The substituted goods must be exported or destroyed within the required time frame): the chapter states that commercially interchangeable or same 8-digit subheading merchandise may be substituted so long as it is exported or destroyed within the required time frame. The substitution rule removes the burden of tracking each individual imported unit through a warehouse to its eventual export. That benefit matters most to companies with high volume, fungible inventory. Domestic sale, domestic production, and insurance coverage are not conditions of substitution unused merchandise drawback.
  28. The correct answer is B (Direct identification manufacturing drawback under section 1313(a), because the traced oil became exported sauce.): the imported oil was used to manufacture a new article in the United States, and that manufactured article was exported. Direct identification applies because the company can trace the specific imported oil into the specific exported jars. Unused merchandise drawback requires the goods to leave in essentially the same condition, and the oil was transformed into sauce instead. Rejected merchandise drawback addresses nonconformity or shipment errors, and no such fact appears here.
  29. The correct answer is C (The statutory and regulatory conditions, including timely notice to Customs): the chapter states that duties paid on the original entry may be recoverable under section 1313(c) provided the statutory and regulatory conditions, including timely notice to Customs and proper handling of the merchandise, are satisfied. A common fact pattern is an importer who orders a specified quantity or quality, receives merchandise failing the contracted specification, and returns the nonconforming shipment. The category is narrower and more specific than unused merchandise drawback. Supplier agreement, carrier damage certification, and a pre-export appraisal are not the conditions the chapter lists.
  30. The correct answer is A (As potential gray-market or unauthorized diversion goods rather than counterfeit, since the mark itself is genuinely applied by the licensed manufacturer): Because the mark was genuinely applied by the actual authorized manufacturer, unauthorized overrun production diverted outside the brand owner's control is generally analyzed as a gray-market or diversion issue rather than counterfeiting, which requires a spurious mark rather than an authentic one applied without full authorization for this particular batch. Lack of consent for this specific sale does not automatically convert genuine goods into counterfeit merchandise. CBP retains jurisdiction to examine such goods, and this scenario relates to trademark and licensing issues rather than country of origin marking duty concerns.
  31. The correct answer is D (The client is wrongly told the goods cannot enter when they can, at a higher rate): the chapter states that a broker who mistakenly treats a tariff rate quota like an absolute quota might tell a client the goods cannot enter at all, when in fact they can enter, just at a higher rate. With a tariff rate quota, entry is never legally blocked by the quota itself. Only the duty rate changes once the in-quota quantity has been filled. A protest, a visa, or waiting for the next period is not the remedy for this situation.
  32. The correct answer is C (The merchandise can be rejected from the current period despite being compliant): the chapter states that filing against the wrong quota category, or filing late, can cause merchandise to be rejected from the current period even though physical goods were available and compliant. A broker handling quota sensitive merchandise needs to know the specific port's procedures, the correct entry type, and the precise quota category. The quota category is often expressed as a specific quota category number for textiles or by HTSUS subheading for agricultural tariff rate quotas. Automatic correction and proration do not cure a filing made against the wrong category.
  33. The correct answer is A (The marking duty is a remedial charge for goods simply not marked as required, while a false marking penalty targets deliberately inaccurate or deceptive marking and can carry more serious consequences): The ten percent ad valorem marking duty addresses the straightforward failure to mark goods as legally required, serving a remedial and largely non-punitive purpose that can often be avoided through corrective marking, while a false or fraudulent marking violation targets intentional deception about origin and can carry separate, more serious civil or criminal consequences. These are legally distinct concepts, not interchangeable terms. The marking duty applies to country of origin marking failures generally, not exclusively to counterfeit trademark violations, and the two consequences are not mutually exclusive in every case.
  34. The correct answer is C (It was issued by the government of the exporting country as an authorization): the chapter describes a textile visa as an endorsement, stamp, or export authorization issued by the government of the exporting country. It certified that a shipment of textiles or apparel was authorized to be exported to the United States under a bilateral quota agreement. The visa was presented along with the entry documentation. Customs, embassies, and carriers are not the issuing parties the chapter identifies.
  35. The correct answer is C (Bilateral agreements with country specific quotas and a visa requirement): the chapter states that textile and apparel trade was historically managed through a system of bilateral agreements, country specific quotas, and a visa requirement. The system arose under agreements that predate the modern World Trade Organization framework. The exporting country's visa was presented with the entry documentation as evidence that the shipment counted against that country's allotted quota category. Unilateral proclamations and regional free trade agreements are not the arrangements the chapter describes.
  36. The correct answer is A (A potential permanence deficiency, since the marking may not survive the storage and handling conditions the article will actually experience before reaching the ultimate purchaser): Permanence under 19 CFR 134.41 is evaluated relative to the realistic handling and storage the article will undergo before reaching the ultimate purchaser, so a light-duty adhesive sticker facing extended uncontrolled outdoor storage risks detaching or degrading before sale, raising a genuine compliance concern. Permanence is not judged solely against an idealized indoor scenario disconnected from actual conditions. This is squarely a customs marking compliance issue, not merely a packaging engineering matter, and sticker-based marking does not automatically trigger the marking duty; the duty applies only when marking actually fails to satisfy the legal standard.
  37. The correct answer is D (Materials produced in the beneficiary country plus direct processing costs there): the chapter states that the value added test typically requires the sum of the cost or value of materials produced in the beneficiary country plus the direct costs of processing operations performed there. That sum must equal at least a specified minimum percentage of the appraised value of the merchandise at the time of entry. Materials from certain other beneficiary countries within the same regional grouping may sometimes be counted under accumulation provisions. Retail price, transport cost, duty paid, and entry fees are not components of the value added test.
  38. The correct answer is D (Destruction under Customs supervision can support drawback for qualifying merchandise): the chapter defines drawback as a refund granted when imported merchandise, or a substitute for it, is subsequently exported or destroyed under Customs supervision. The policy is that the United States generally does not want to keep duty revenue collected on goods that end up leaving the country rather than being consumed domestically. The destruction must be carried out under Customs supervision to satisfy the requirement. The remaining options impose limits on destruction that the chapter does not state.
  39. The correct answer is A (Each article in a set is generally evaluated individually unless it qualifies as part of the immediate container or falls under a specific set marking exception): CBP generally requires each article to bear its own origin marking unless it functions as the immediate container for another marked article or a recognized exception applies, so marking the knife blade alone does not automatically extend to an unmarked wooden block sold as part of the same set unless a specific exception or container relationship applies. There is no blanket rule exempting all set components from marking, and marking obligations are not limited only to the highest-value item in a set.
  40. The correct answer is D (A standardized test based on the 8-digit HTSUS subheading for substitution): the chapter states that TFTEA moved the system toward the objective standard that merchandise classified under the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading is eligible for substitution. Before TFTEA, substitution drawback used a commercially interchangeable standard for unused merchandise and a same kind and quality standard for manufacturing drawback. Those older tests could require fact intensive and sometimes subjective analysis. TFTEA also moved drawback claim filing to the Automated Commercial Environment and required electronic filing.
  41. The correct answer is B (Brief processing that does not amount to use or manufacture still qualifies): the chapter describes unused merchandise drawback as covering goods that sat in inventory or were briefly processed in ways that do not amount to use or manufacture. The goods must be exported or destroyed in essentially the same condition they were in when imported. The importer then never gained the domestic market benefit that the duty was meant to offset. Domestic sale and automatic reclassification to rejected merchandise drawback are not consequences the chapter describes.
  42. The correct answer is A (It likely constitutes unlawful concealment or alteration of required marking and may also raise false marking concerns under federal law): Deliberately covering accurate country of origin marking with a false Made in USA sticker misrepresents the true origin to the ultimate purchaser and implicates both marking concealment and false marking provisions, potentially triggering penalties independent of any separate private disclosure to CBP, which does not cure a deceptive marking directed at consumers. Retailers do not have unrestricted discretion to override accurate federal marking requirements, and the violation exists regardless of whether the goods are later exported.
  43. The correct answer is B (The Automated Commercial Environment, which requires electronic filing of claims): the chapter states that TFTEA transitioned drawback claim filing to the Customs Automated Commercial Environment and required electronic filing. The same changes streamlined recordkeeping requirements. The goals were reducing processing time and increasing consistency in how claims are adjudicated across ports. The other systems named are used for entry transmission, quota status publication, and zone inventory control.
  44. The correct answer is D (It removes the burden of tracking each imported unit to its eventual export): the chapter explains that the substitution version is significant for companies with high volume, fungible inventory. Substitution unused merchandise drawback allows merchandise commercially interchangeable with, or classified under the same 8-digit HTSUS subheading as, the imported goods to be substituted. The substituted merchandise must still be exported or destroyed within the required time frame. The approach does not remove the export requirement, raise the refund rate, or extend the filing window.
  45. The correct answer is A (Detailed production records showing the specific manufacturing operations performed that changed the components' name, character, or use): Substantiating a substantial transformation claim requires concrete evidence of the actual manufacturing processes performed, such as production records, bills of materials, and process descriptions demonstrating the components emerged as a new article with a different name, character, or use. Marketing brochures, shipping route documents, and subjective visual impressions do not establish the legal elements of substantial transformation and would not satisfy CBP's evidentiary expectations in a request for information.
  46.
