  37. The correct answer is C (Transaction value of the imported merchandise): Transaction value, the price actually paid or payable for merchandise when sold for export to the United States, is the statutory primary basis of appraisement and must be used whenever it can be determined and is not otherwise barred by the related party rules. Only when transaction value cannot be applied does CBP move to the alternative methods in their legal order of preference. The other three listed methods are all secondary bases that apply only after transaction value is ruled out. This hierarchy is fixed by statute and cannot be reordered at the importer's discretion.

The die is an assist valued at $2,400.00, and the selling commission of $1,300.00 was paid to an agent representing the seller, not the buyer. $37,200.00 plus $2,400.00 plus $1,300.00 equals $40,900.00.
  62. The correct answer is C ($29,975.00): The value of any part of the proceeds of a subsequent resale that accrues directly or indirectly to the seller is a statutory addition to transaction value under 19 USC 1401a(b)(1)(E). Here $1,975.00 of resale proceeds must flow back to the seller under the sales agreement, so it is added to the base price. $28,000.00 plus $1,975.00 equals $29,975.00.
  63. The correct answer is B ($19,610.00): Selling commissions, paid to an agent who represents the seller's interests in the transaction, are a statutory addition to the price actually paid or payable under 19 USC 1401a(b)(1)(B). This differs from a bona fide buying commission paid to an agent representing the buyer, which is excluded from transaction value entirely. $17,600.00 plus the selling commission of $2,010.00 equals $19,610.00.

Here $1,550.00 in packing costs must be added to the base price of $48,200.00. $48,200.00 plus $1,550.00 equals $49,750.00, the transaction value.
  67.   68. The correct answer is D ($34,450.00): Royalty and license fees are added to the price actually paid or payable under 19 USC 1401a(b)(1)(D) when the buyer must pay them as a condition of the sale of the imported merchandise for export to the United States. Because payment of the $1,250.00 royalty is a condition of this sale, it forms part of dutiable transaction value. $33,200.00 plus $1,250.00 equals $34,450.00.
  69. Because the agent negotiated solely on the buyer's behalf, the $1,725.00 commission stays outside the dutiable value. The transaction value therefore remains the price actually paid or payable of $17,600.00.

Assists are one of the five statutory additions listed in 19 USC 1401a(b)(1) that are added even though the assist itself was never invoiced. $61,000.00 plus $1,500.00 equals $62,500.00.

  75. The correct answer is A (Once classification is established through a ruling, the applicable duty rate generally follows from that determined heading and subheading): because each subheading in the HTSUS carries its own specified rate of duty, a binding ruling that establishes the correct classification effectively also identifies the applicable duty rate tied to that heading and subheading. It is inaccurate to say a binding ruling has no connection at all to the duty rate ultimately assessed, since classification and duty rate are directly linked in the tariff schedule's structure. Duty rate is not determined through an entirely separate and unrelated process disconnected from classification. There is no requirement to file a separate second ruling request specifically for duty rate once the classification itself has been properly established.

It is inaccurate to say Part 177 rulings cover classification exclusively and nothing else. CBP itself, not an entirely separate unrelated agency, administers marking determinations relevant to imported goods. Origin determinations can indeed be the subject of a binding ruling request under the applicable regulatory framework.

Visual prominence of the wicker alone does not outweigh the frame's functional role under this test. Relative sourcing cost of materials is not the legal standard applied under essential character analysis. GRI 4 is reserved as a true last resort, not applied before a genuine essential character determination under GRI 3(b) has been attempted.

GRI 3(b) essential character analysis is unnecessary when the article is made of a single material and a heading directly covers it under GRI 1. GRI 2(b) addresses mixtures of distinct materials, which does not describe a bag made entirely of one uniform fabric. A residual basket provision is used only after more specific classification approaches, including GRI 1, are found inadequate, not skipped as a first resort.
  83. The correct answer is D (An authorized representative, such as a licensed customs broker, may generally submit the request on the importer's behalf): Part 177 procedures generally permit an authorized representative, including a licensed customs broker acting under proper authorization, to submit a binding ruling request on behalf of the actual importer or manufacturer. It is not accurate that only the importer of record personally may ever submit such a request. Submission is not restricted exclusively to attorneys admitted to a federal court. A properly executed power of attorney is a standard and permissible mechanism for authorizing a representative to act on the importer's behalf in this process.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The insulated liner, since it provides the article's core temperature control function): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) considers which component provides the article's fundamental purpose, and an insulated liner responsible for the lunch bag's defining temperature control function likely supplies that essential character over an outer fabric shell that primarily serves an aesthetic and protective covering role. Larger visible surface area alone does not establish essential character under this test. Order of assembly during manufacturing is not a legally relevant factor. GRI 3(c) applies only as a genuine last resort when essential character truly cannot be determined, which is not indicated by this fact pattern given the liner's clear functional role.
  2. The correct answer is A (Classified as footwear under GRI 3(b), since the shoe function supplies essential character): a minor pocket feature integrated into a shoe does not change the article's fundamental identity as footwear, so GRI 3(b) essential character analysis appropriately treats the footwear function as controlling over this small secondary feature. The mere presence of a small storage pocket does not reclassify the entire shoe as a storage article. A single physically integrated shoe is not split into separate entries based on a minor internal feature. GRI 2(a) addresses incomplete or unassembled articles, which does not describe finished footwear with a minor built-in convenience feature.
  3. The correct answer is B (A general purpose article is typically classified by its eo nomine or general heading, while a specialized-use article may fall under a distinct use provision): whether an article falls under a general eo nomine heading or a more specialized use provision often depends on whether it is designed for general purpose use or for a narrow, specific specialized function recognized by a distinct heading. Not every clothing accessory falls under one identical heading whatever its design and intended purpose. Use provisions can and do apply to certain articles of clothing or accessories when a heading specifically requires principal use analysis. Design specificity is directly relevant to determining which type of provision, eo nomine or use, properly governs the article.
  4. The correct answer is D (As a single composite article under GRI 1 or GRI 3(b), based on its overall character as a therapeutic product): a permanently combined ice pack and fabric sleeve sold as one first aid product is a single article, first analyzed under GRI 1 if a heading directly covers such combination therapeutic products, and otherwise under GRI 3(b) essential character analysis if more than one heading could apply. Splitting it into two separate articles ignores that it is one permanently sealed combined product. GRI 5(b) is limited to ordinary packing materials, not a functional sleeve that is integral to how the product is actually used. Chapter 99 addresses temporary legislative and trade remedy provisions, not the ordinary classification of a first aid product.
  5. The correct answer is C (The core Part 177 process applies broadly across product types, though technical content required will vary by commodity): the fundamental binding ruling request process under Part 177 applies generally across commodity types, though the specific technical information needed for an agricultural product will naturally differ from that needed for a textile or industrial article. Agricultural goods are not categorically excluded from the binding ruling program. Textile merchandise ruling requests are not governed by an entirely separate legal statute outside the Part 177 framework. Industrial goods classification rulings are issued by CBP itself under Part 177, not exclusively by a specialized court.
  6. The correct answer is D (Evidence of physical design features, marketing, and trade channels showing principal laboratory use): classification under a use provision depends on established factors such as physical design characteristics, how the goods are marketed, and the channels of trade through which they are sold, all bearing on the principal use of the class or kind of merchandise. A single purchaser's individual intent does not establish the principal use of the broader class of goods under this legal standard. Cosmetic lens tint alone, without functional design evidence, does not resolve a principal use classification question. A brand name alone, without supporting design and marketing evidence, is insufficient to establish principal use.
  7. The correct answer is A (As a single composite helmet article under GRI 1, since the visor is integrally molded): when a visor is permanently and integrally molded as part of the helmet shell forming one continuous physical article, GRI 1 can often resolve classification directly under the heading for helmets, since the article is a single unified good rather than a combination of separable items. Treating it as two separate articles ignores that it is physically one continuous molded piece. GRI 2(b) addresses mixtures and combinations of distinct materials or substances, not an integrally molded single-piece article. Chapter 99 addresses temporary legislative provisions and trade remedies, not the ordinary classification of protective headgear.
  8. The correct answer is C (Confirm whether the article is part of a qualifying retail set before applying the primary chapter heading): a binding exclusion note redirecting set merchandise to different set provisions must be applied under GRI 1, so the broker must first determine whether the article is genuinely part of a qualifying retail set before defaulting to the primary chapter's individual article heading. Ignoring a binding note simply because an individual heading otherwise appears to fit misapplies the required legal analysis. Application of a binding note does not depend on a written request from the importer. There is no indication this kind of note is conditioned on a specific dollar value threshold absent express language to that effect.
  9. The correct answer is D (As a single composite luggage article under GRI 1, since the panel is permanently integrated): a garment bag panel permanently sewn into luggage as an inseparable interior feature makes the item one continuous physical article, allowing GRI 1 to often resolve classification directly under the applicable luggage heading. Treating it as two separate articles ignores that it is physically one unified piece of luggage. GRI 2(b) addresses mixtures and combinations of materials or substances, not a permanently integrated fabric panel within a single luggage article. GRI 3(c) is a tiebreaker used only after GRI 3(a) and 3(b) fail to resolve a genuine conflict between competing headings, which has not been established here.
  10. The correct answer is C (A part is typically necessary for the machine's function, while an accessory improves or extends its use without being strictly essential): a part is generally understood as a component necessary for the machine to function as intended, while an accessory enhances, extends, or facilitates the machine's use without being strictly essential to its basic operation, and relevant notes sometimes distinguish these for classification purposes. This distinction does carry real legal significance in various headings and notes throughout the HTSUS. An accessory is not automatically classified as raw material simply because it is not strictly essential to function. Relative weight between a part and an accessory is not the legal basis for this classification distinction.
  11. The correct answer is B (Obtaining a binding ruling is optional, though it is a recommended tool for reducing classification risk): the Part 177 ruling process is an available tool an importer or broker may choose to use for advance certainty, but it is not a legal prerequisite that must be completed before every new product line can be imported. A binding ruling is not mandatory for all imports as a matter of law. CBP does not automatically generate a binding ruling for every entry filed absent an actual request. There is no statutory dollar threshold that makes a binding ruling mandatory based solely on merchandise value.
  12. The correct answer is C (Trade publications provide no binding legal certainty comparable to a formal Part 177 ruling): only a properly issued binding ruling under Part 177 provides the legal reliability an importer can affirmatively depend on, and general industry trade publications, however informative, do not carry that same binding legal weight. Trade publications are not equal in legal effect to a formal ruling. CBP is not legally bound by classification descriptions appearing in independent trade publications. Simply citing a trade publication in an entry summary does not transform it into a binding legal authority.
  13. The correct answer is A (The metal buckle may supply essential character given its dominant role): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) considers which component dominates in terms of function, visual prominence, and the role it plays in the article's identity, and a large decorative buckle serving as the item's defining feature can supply that essential character over a plain strap. Belts are not classified by material alone without considering the composite article's overall character. Essential character analysis does apply to composite fashion accessories generally. A basket provision is used only when no more specific classification approach applies, not as a default before analysis is attempted.
  14. The correct answer is D (The toy function, since it is the article's dominant commercial purpose and identity): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) considers which feature defines the article's overall purpose and identity to the consumer, and a squeeze toy's play function clearly dominates even though a small embedded speaker adds a sound feature. Greater internal technical complexity of a minor component does not automatically make it the essential character driver. Manufacturing cost per unit is not the legal standard applied under this test. Retail packaging materials are addressed separately under GRI 5 and are not the essential character component of the toy itself.
  15. The correct answer is B (Evidence regarding the actual or principal use of the class or kind of merchandise): classification under a use provision depends on the principal use of the class or kind of goods to which the imported merchandise belongs, examined through factors like physical characteristics, marketing, and channels of trade, rather than any single purchaser's subjective intent. One individual buyer's personal use does not establish the principal use of the broader class of merchandise. Retail price alone, without regard to functional design characteristics, does not resolve a principal use analysis. Packaging color is not a legally significant factor in determining principal use.
  16. The correct answer is D (Headquarters rulings are often appropriate for novel or legally complex issues requiring broader precedential guidance): while both port level offices and CBP headquarters can issue binding rulings under Part 177, particularly novel, legally complex, or precedent setting issues are often more appropriately directed to headquarters for broader, more authoritative guidance. Port offices are not prohibited from issuing binding rulings generally. There is no dollar value threshold that exclusively determines which office may rule on a classification question. The choice of issuing office is not entirely irrelevant, since novel legal issues may benefit from the broader analytical resources available at headquarters.
  17. The correct answer is C (Classified as furniture under GRI 3(b), since the furniture function supplies essential character): where a minor secondary feature like a charging port does not change the article's fundamental identity as furniture, GRI 3(b) essential character analysis appropriately treats the furniture function as controlling. The mere presence of an electronic component does not automatically reclassify the entire article as an electronic device. A single physically integrated article is not split into separate entries based on differing internal features. GRI 2(a) addresses incomplete or unassembled articles, which does not describe finished furniture with a minor added convenience feature.
  18. The correct answer is D (Inconsistent classification without supporting analysis may indicate a lack of reasonable care): CBP expects consistent classification of substantially identical merchandise, and unexplained inconsistency across shipments without a documented analytical basis or binding ruling can suggest a failure to exercise the reasonable care required of brokers and importers. Rough similarity in duty paid does not excuse inconsistent legal classification. CBP does not simply average duty rates across inconsistent entries to resolve a classification discrepancy. Timely filing alone does not cure an underlying classification compliance problem.
  19. The correct answer is A (The watch mechanism, since it provides the article's essential character and function): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) considers which component supplies the item's fundamental identity and function, and a timekeeping mechanism clearly provides that role over interchangeable straps that are secondary accessories. Having multiple strap options included does not shift essential character away from the mechanism itself. Manufacturing date has no bearing on the essential character test. GRI 5(b) applies to ordinary packing materials, not to the primary functional component of the set.
  20. The correct answer is C (The knives, since they supply the essential character of the combined product): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) looks to which component provides the item's primary function and purpose, and kitchen knives, as the tools actually used for their intended purpose, likely supply that essential character over a supporting storage block. Relative material value alone does not determine essential character under this test. Order of appearance in a printed product description carries no legal weight. GRI 3(c) applies only as a last resort when essential character genuinely cannot be determined, which is not the case here given the knives' clear functional dominance.
  21. The correct answer is D (A ruling request can generally be based on sufficiently detailed specifications even before actual production): the binding ruling process is designed to give importers classification certainty in advance, so a request can be based on detailed technical specifications, drawings, or prototypes describing merchandise that has not yet entered full production, provided the description is thorough enough for analysis. Requiring the finished product to already exist would defeat the advance-certainty purpose of the program. There is no requirement that an entry already be liquidated before a ruling request can be submitted. While vague specifications would be inadequate, sufficiently detailed technical design information is not automatically insufficient.
  22. The correct answer is A (CBP may request additional information or decline to rule until detail is provided): under Part 177, CBP needs an adequately detailed merchandise description to issue a reliable ruling, and if a submission lacks necessary information, CBP may request supplementation or decline to rule until the record is complete. CBP has no obligation to guess in the importer's favor when information is missing. An incomplete request does not automatically become an unrelated protest. A single incomplete submission does not permanently bar future ruling requests on other products.
  23. The correct answer is B (It likely does not qualify as a set, since the items lack a shared specific purpose): the GRI 3(b) definition of a set requires components to meet a particular need or specific activity together, and bulk fasteners sold for general resale without any coordinated task in mind do not satisfy this requirement, so each type would likely require its own classification. Simply being packaged together does not automatically create set status. Classifying by the most numerous item ignores the legal requirement to properly analyze each distinct article. Chapter 98 addresses special scenarios unrelated to bulk fastener sales and does not apply here simply because eventual assembly elsewhere is contemplated.
  24. The correct answer is A (The items may fail to qualify as a set because they lack a shared specific need or activity): the GRI 3(b) definition of a set requires the components together meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity, not merely that they share packaging. A random assortment for general gift-giving may not satisfy this requirement, meaning the items could instead require separate classification. Mere retail packaging alone is not sufficient to establish set status. Value of an individual item and the container itself are not controlling factors in this preliminary set-qualification analysis.
  25. The correct answer is C (As a single composite article under GRI 1 or GRI 3(b), based on its overall character): a permanently combined piece of furniture forming one integrated article is generally classified as a single composite good, first attempting GRI 1 if a heading directly covers such combination furniture, and otherwise applying GRI 3(b) essential character analysis if more than one heading could apply. Splitting the item into two separate articles ignores that it is physically one permanently joined unit. GRI 2(a) is inapplicable since neither portion is an unfinished version of the other. A residual provision applies only after more specific classification approaches have been properly considered and found inadequate.
  26. The correct answer is A (A ruling generally applies to the specific facts and merchandise described in it): a Part 177 ruling is issued based on the specific facts and description of the merchandise presented, so it is binding as to that transaction but persuasive rather than automatically controlling for different merchandise, even similar merchandise. An importer with different goods should generally seek its own ruling for certainty. Binding rulings do not carry a blanket ninety day expiration simply from the passage of time. Published rulings from any authorized office can carry legal weight, so the distinction is specificity of merchandise, not the issuing office.
  27. The correct answer is D (GRI 3(b), determining which function gives the article essential character): when GRI 3(a) fails because neither heading is more specific than the other for a composite functional article, GRI 3(b) directs classification according to whichever material or component gives the article its essential character. GRI 1 does not apply because no heading names this hybrid combination article directly. GRI 5(a) is limited to containers specially shaped for a specific article, not a dual-function composite good. GRI 4 is reserved for situations where no earlier rule, including GRI 3(b), can resolve the classification.
  28. The correct answer is C (Requesting reconsideration or seeking further administrative review through CBP procedures): an importer dissatisfied with a ruling generally has administrative avenues, such as requesting reconsideration, before resorting to litigation or simply disregarding the ruling. Filing suit immediately without pursuing available administrative review is not the standard first step. Ignoring a binding ruling and self-classifying contrary to it exposes the importer to compliance risk and potential penalties. Waiting until after liquidation to raise concerns forgoes the advance certainty that the ruling process is designed to provide.
  29. The correct answer is B (Classify as the complete bicycle, since unassembled goods are treated as the finished article): GRI 2(a) expressly extends a heading for a complete article to goods imported unassembled or disassembled, provided the imported components together present the essential character of the finished article. Separate component-by-component classification ignores this specific rule designed for exactly this scenario. A residual provision is inappropriate when GRI 2(a) directly resolves the classification. Relative component value is not the legal standard GRI 2(a) applies.
  30. The correct answer is C (The wood likely supplies essential character since the metal parts are minor accessories): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) weighs the role, bulk, and function each material contributes to the finished article, and minor hardware like hinges and handles typically does not outweigh the wood that forms the article's structure and primary identity. Metal does not automatically control simply due to general durability characteristics. A composite article like this is classified as a whole under GRI 3(b) rather than split across two headings. GRI 4 applies only as a true last resort, not before a reasonable essential character determination has been attempted.
  31. The correct answer is D (Enough detail for CBP to fully understand composition, construction, and function): a reliable ruling request under Part 177 requires a full and accurate technical description so CBP can properly analyze the classification question presented. A marketing name alone does not convey the technical facts needed for a legal classification analysis. A brief one sentence summary is inadequate under the standard CBP applies to ruling requests. The level of detail required is set by CBP's own requirements, not by whatever the overseas supplier happens to be willing to disclose.
  32. The correct answer is B (Chapter 98, containing provisions for American goods returned): Chapter 98 contains special classification provisions including treatment for US products exported and later returned without advancement in value or improvement in condition abroad. Chapter 99 instead addresses temporary modifications such as trade remedy duties, not returned American goods. Chapter 1 is unrelated, covering live animals. Classifying solely by the machine's constituent material bypasses the special provision that exists precisely for this returning goods scenario.
  33. The correct answer is A (GRI 3(b), since the goods are put up together and one likely gives essential character): when distinct articles are packaged together for retail sale and jointly meet a particular need, GRI 3(b) classifies the set by whichever component gives essential character, rather than separately. GRI 2(a) is inapplicable because the accessory is a complete article, not an unfinished version of the main product. GRI 5(a) applies to specially shaped containers for a specific article, such as a camera case, not to a functional accessory performing its own task alongside the main article.
  34. The correct answer is D (The chapter note's definition controls within that chapter over the dictionary meaning): where a Chapter Note supplies a specific legal definition for a term used in that chapter's headings, that definition controls classification, taking precedence over a general dictionary or trade meaning. Ignoring the note in favor of ordinary meaning would misapply the binding legal text required under GRI 1. Duty rate outcome has no bearing on which definition the broker is legally required to apply. Both Section Notes and Chapter Notes carry authority to define terms.
  35. The correct answer is B (The metal frame, since it supplies essential character through structure and function): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) examines factors like structural role and function in relation to use, rather than mere visual prominence or cost. Since the metal frame provides structural strength and functional purpose, it likely supplies essential character. Visual prominence of the wood alone does not outweigh a component's functional significance. GRI 3(c) is a last resort used only when essential character genuinely cannot be determined, which is not indicated here.
  36. A basket provision would apply only if no more specific parts provision or note existed, which is not the case here. The parts are finished, functional components, not raw material stock. Chapter 99 addresses temporary legislative and trade remedy provisions, not the ordinary classification of repair parts.
  37. The correct answer is A (Classify under the heading occurring last in numerical order among those equally deserving consideration): GRI 3(c) is a tiebreaker applied only when goods cannot be resolved under GRI 3(a) or 3(b), pointing to the heading appearing last numerically among equally qualifying headings. This mechanical rule has nothing to do with importer preference or minimizing duty, which carry no legal weight in classification. Referral to headquarters is not a mandatory step under the GRIs themselves. The rule exists specifically for genuine ties where essential character cannot be determined.
  38. The correct answer is D (Classification as goods put up in a set for retail sale under GRI 3(b)): a collection of distinct articles packaged together to meet a particular need or activity, such as home repair, and sold as a unit meets the GRI 3(b) set definition, requiring classification by the essential-character component rather than itemized separate classification. Classifying each tool individually ignores the set provision governing this pattern. Classifying solely by the container would improperly ignore the tools, which are clearly the primary commercial value. GRI 2(a) is irrelevant since this is a complete assortment of finished tools, not an incomplete manufacturing scenario.
  39. The correct answer is C (A legal note establishing a specific rule that must be applied under GRI 1): when a section or chapter note supplies its own classification rule, such as fiber predominance by weight, that note is binding legal text applied under GRI 1 before ever reaching GRI 3. This differs from a general essential character test under GRI 3(b), which applies only when no controlling note resolves the question. GRI 2(b) addresses mixtures more broadly but yields to a specific controlling note when one exists. Explanatory Notes are merely persuasive and are not the same as a binding Section or Chapter Note.
  40. The correct answer is B (A binding ruling request submitted under 19 CFR Part 177): Part 177 establishes the process for obtaining a prospective binding ruling that an importer or broker can rely on before filing entries. A verbal port director opinion carries no binding legal effect. The Explanatory Notes are persuasive interpretive aids, not a substitute for a formal ruling request. A protest addresses a dispute after liquidation and cannot provide advance certainty.
  41. The correct answer is D (The requester may ask CBP to protect specified confidential business information): Part 177 procedures allow a requester to identify information believed confidential, and CBP generally protects properly identified confidential business information when publishing the ruling. A confidentiality request does not void or weaken the ruling's binding legal effect. CBP routinely publishes rulings, often in redacted form, rather than withholding them entirely. Confidential information is addressed within the Part 177 request process itself, not through an unrelated protest.
  42. The correct answer is C (A complete merchandise description, the requester's identity, and a proposed classification): a Part 177 ruling request must provide a full and accurate description of the article, including technical detail, the requester's identity, and typically a suggested classification with supporting rationale. Bond information and port of entry are not the core elements CBP needs for classification analysis. An invoice showing value relates to appraisement rather than the classification question at issue. Without adequate merchandise description, CBP cannot issue a reliable ruling.
  43. The correct answer is A (GRI 3(b), classifying the set according to the pitcher as the component imparting essential character): when one component of a recognized set, here the pitcher, clearly predominates in bulk and value relative to the serving function of the whole set, GRI 3(b) properly resolves the classification by identifying that predominating component as imparting the essential character, without needing to fall back to the GRI 3(c) tie-breaker. GRI 3(c) is reached only when GRI 3(b) genuinely cannot identify a predominating component, which is not the case in this clear scenario. GRI 4 is reserved for goods that elude classification under GRI 1 through 3 entirely, not relevant once GRI 3(b) resolves the matter. The crystal bowl is a genuine, distinct article of the set rather than mere packing material under GRI 5(b).
  44. The correct answer is C (Apply GRI 2 and then GRI 3, in that numerical order): the General Rules of Interpretation apply sequentially, so when GRI 1 fails to resolve a classification, the broker moves to GRI 2 and then GRI 3 before any later rule. Jumping to GRI 6, which governs subheading comparison, is improper before the heading level is settled. Picking the first heading numerically skips the required legal analysis entirely. The Explanatory Notes remain persuasive tools, not a substitute for working through the GRIs in order.
  45. The correct answer is B (The stool, being a separate, functionally unrelated article rather than a component of the cabinet itself, is properly classified on its own merits, separate from the GRI 2(a) unassembled-article analysis applicable to the cabinet components): GRI 2(a) applies to components that together form a single, recognizable unassembled article, and a freestanding stool with no functional relationship to the cabinet's structure is a genuinely separate, independently classifiable article rather than a cabinet component, so it must be analyzed on its own under GRI 1 or the appropriate applicable rule. Simply sharing a wood material and a shipping container does not merge two functionally unrelated furniture pieces into one GRI 2(a) unassembled article. Shared container shipment does not prevent either article from being properly classified; each is still classifiable under the ordinary GRI framework. A functional piece of furniture like a stool does not become mere packing material under GRI 5(b) simply because it was packed alongside other goods.
  46. The correct answer is D (Parts provisions generally follow the classification of the principal article served): tariff treatment of parts is shaped heavily by the article's relationship to the machine it serves, and many headings and notes direct dedicated parts toward the principal article's classification. Classifying by material alone ignores that relationship and any applicable legal note. There is no rule automatically excluding a single-use part from a parts provision. A residual chapter applies only when no more specific parts provision or note exists.
  47. The correct answer is A (The recipe card, since printed informational material of this kind, while classifiable elsewhere in the schedule, plays a role distinct from meeting the set's core culinary gift-giving need and is often treated separately): a printed recipe card included purely for informational value is classifiable under a printed matter heading, but it plays a role distinct from the culinary gift-giving purpose that ties the cookies and tin together, so it is often analyzed separately rather than folded into the GRI 3(b) set. The decorative tin genuinely serves a storage and presentation function directly tied to the cookies and is properly part of the set. The cookies are a genuine, useful article of the set regardless of being consumable. This basket does present a real classification question about how to treat the recipe card relative to the rest of the items.
  48. The correct answer is B (The relative bulk, value, and functional role each material plays in providing the bag's overall form, structural integrity, and primary carrying function): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) requires weighing bulk, quantity, weight, value, and the functional role of each material relative to the finished article's use, and for a backpack the outer nylon shell providing the bag's primary shape and carrying structure typically plays a dominant role in that overall weighing. Focusing only on metal content by weight ignores the far more significant fabric components defining the bag's basic function. Zipper pull color has no bearing on essential character analysis. Whether assembly was performed by hand or by machine is a manufacturing method detail irrelevant to essential character determination.
  49. The correct answer is D (As binding legal text that must be applied before choosing a heading): Section and Chapter Notes carry the same binding force as heading text under GRI 1, and an exclusion note can redirect an article to another chapter entirely. Notes cannot be disregarded merely because a heading's wording seems to fit at first glance. They govern classification itself, not merely the duty rate ultimately applied. CBP has no mechanism to waive binding legal notes on request.
  50. The correct answer is B (Likely the gemstone ring, given the prominent decorative and higher relative value role the gemstone typically plays in a bridal ring set, though bulk, value, and marketing role are all weighed together): essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) genuinely weighs value, bulk, and the functional or marketing role each component plays, and for a bridal ring set the gemstone ring often carries greater relative value and plays the more prominent role that customers focus on, though a full factual analysis considering all relevant factors together is still required. Basing the determination purely on total metal weight, ignoring value and marketing role, is an incomplete analytical approach. Sets can and often do have an identifiable essential character once the relevant factors are properly weighed, so ruling it out entirely is incorrect. The presentation box is packing material under GRI 5(b), not a component relevant to identifying essential character among the jewelry pieces themselves.
  51. The correct answer is A (It provides finer statistical reporting detail for trade data purposes while generally sharing the same duty rate as its parent eight-digit rate line): the ten-digit statistical suffix exists primarily to capture more granular trade statistics for government reporting purposes, while the applicable duty rate is generally established at the eight-digit rate line level above it. Claiming it sets an entirely separate, independent duty rate misunderstands how the statistical suffix typically functions within the schedule's structure. The statistical suffix is not legally meaningless; accurate reporting at this level is often required on entry documentation. The ten-digit suffix supplements, rather than replaces, the internationally harmonized six-digit subheading that remains foundational to the classification.
  52. The correct answer is D (As an unfinished article having the essential character of the complete glove, classified under the heading for finished baseball gloves): GRI 2(a) extends the finished-article heading to an unfinished version of that article whenever the essential character of the complete good is already present, and a fully shaped, stitched leather glove missing only wrist lacing clearly meets that threshold. Treating a substantially completed, stitched leather glove as mere raw material would ignore the extensive manufacturing already performed. Chapter 99 addresses additional temporary trade remedy duties layered on top of an underlying classification, not the base classification analysis itself. GRI 5(a) governs fitted cases presented with goods, an unrelated concept to an unfinished sporting goods article like this glove.
  53. The Section 232 duty determination does not itself govern or replace the base heading selection. Both the base classification and the additional duty determination remain legally required once a Section 232 action exists. Country of origin is relevant to whether an additional duty applies at all, but it does not itself replace the GRI-based process for selecting the fitting's base tariff heading.
  54. The correct answer is A (Within Chapter 99, which houses temporary duty modifications such as Section 301 actions applied alongside the underlying merchandise classification): Chapter 99 is specifically where temporary legislative and administrative trade actions, including Section 301 duties, Section 232 duties, and safeguard measures, are set out, layered on top of the base classification found elsewhere in the schedule rather than replacing it. The underlying household appliance heading itself remains unchanged; only an additional Chapter 99 provision applies alongside it. Section 301 duties are in fact published and codified within the tariff schedule once imposed, contrary to any suggestion they are never reflected there. The General Notes address broader interpretive and preference program matters rather than specific country-targeted temporary duty actions.
  55. The correct answer is B (Check whether the ruling has since been modified, revoked, or superseded, since CBP retains authority to update prior rulings through required procedures as facts, law, or CBP's understanding evolve): CBP retains ongoing authority to modify or revoke a prior classification ruling through required notice and comment procedures, so a broker must confirm a ruling's current status rather than assume it remains permanently unchanged. Assuming permanent, unchangeable validity ignores this real possibility of later modification. Relying solely on the original issuance date without checking for subsequent action is an incomplete verification approach. A published ruling generally does not require permission from the original requester for a different importer with substantially identical facts to rely upon it.
  56. The correct answer is D (The GRI 3(b) composite goods essential character test, weighing which material most defines the article's use and character): a comb combining plastic and metal into one inseparable grooming object is a composite good rather than a set of distinct articles, so the applicable analysis is the GRI 3(b) composite goods essential character test, weighing bulk, value, quantity, and the functional role each material plays in the finished article's grooming use. The three-part retail set test is reserved for combinations of genuinely separate, individually packaged articles, not a single fused object like this comb. GRI 5(a) addresses fitted cases presented with goods, an unrelated concept to a grooming tool's internal material composition. GRI 4 is reached only when GRI 1 through 3 fail entirely, which is not the case for a well-understood composite article like a comb.
  57. The olive oil, vinegar, and dipping bowl are each genuinely useful, separately classifiable articles that together fulfill the gourmet gift-giving purpose. Being liquid or heavy does not itself determine whether an item is packing material or a set article. At least one element here, the bubble wrap, clearly does qualify as mere packing rather than a set article.
  58. The correct answer is B (A use provision, requiring analysis of principal use of the class or kind of merchandise under Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a), rather than the intent of any single importer): because the heading ties classification to the article's use rather than naming it by a specific product name, it constitutes a use provision, and Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a) directs that principal use be assessed for the class or kind of merchandise as a whole, considering factors like physical characteristics, channels of trade, and marketing, rather than any single importer's individual stated purpose. An eo nomine provision instead names the article specifically without regard to use, which is not the case here given the use-based heading language. GRI 5(b) governs packing materials and containers, an unrelated concept to a use-based classification heading. A genuine use provision still requires supporting evidence of principal use and is never established without any evidentiary basis.
  59. The correct answer is A (GRI 3(a), in favor of the more specific heading naming chairs): GRI 3(a) resolves conflicts between a specific and a general heading by preferring whichever heading provides the more specific description, and a heading directly naming chairs is more specific than a general furniture parts catch-all provision. Choosing the general heading over the specific one would directly contradict the plain text and purpose of GRI 3(a). GRI 5(a) is limited to fitted cases and containers, an unrelated concept to a wheeled office chair. GRI 2(a) applies to unfinished or unassembled articles, not a fully finished, complete chair.
  60. The correct answer is C (A Section is a broad grouping of related Chapters covering similar categories of goods, such as textiles or machinery, with each Section then subdivided into individual numbered Chapters): the HTSUS organizes its ninety-nine chapters into a smaller number of broader Sections that group related categories of merchandise together, such as an entire Section devoted to textiles and textile articles spanning several chapters. Sections and Chapters are distinct organizational levels with different numbering, not simply alternate names for the same grouping. There is no fixed universal ratio of chapters to sections across the schedule. Sections cover the full range of merchandise categories in the schedule, not agricultural goods exclusively.
  61. Assessing duty on the full watch value would defeat the purpose of this narrowly tailored provision. The American goods returned provision requires no advancement in value or improvement in condition at all, and a repair that restores or improves the watch's function does not meet that strict no-change standard. Chapter 99 addresses separate temporary trade remedy duties and does not replace the specific Chapter 98 repairs framework applicable here.
  62. The repairs and alterations subheading requires that actual repair or alteration work occurred abroad, which is expressly not present here. Chapter 99 concerns unrelated temporary trade remedy duties layered on an underlying classification. A generic eo nomine provision naming the equipment type does not itself provide the specific duty-free treatment tied to prior US origin and unchanged condition.

GRI 4 is reserved for goods that elude classification under GRI 1 through 3 entirely, which is not the case for a well-defined crystal set. That stands in contrast to a genuine use provision, which would require a class or kind of use analysis under Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a), not applicable here since this heading is not use-based. Documentation requirements are a real and often significant part of substantiating such claims, contrary to any suggestion that none is needed. The claim does not apply automatically without a factual showing, since CBP may request proof. A foreign government certificate is not the typical or required form of proof for this US domestic origin claim.
  71. The correct answer is B (The six-digit subheading level is internationally harmonized across countries using the Harmonized System, while the eight- and ten-digit levels reflect United States-specific statistical breakouts and duty rate lines): the Harmonized System is internationally standardized through the six-digit level, meaning many countries share identical six-digit subheadings, while each country then adds its own further breakouts, with the United States extending to eight-digit rate lines and ten-digit statistical suffixes for its own tariff and trade data purposes. Deeper digit levels are legally significant, not merely decorative, since they can carry different duty rates and statistical reporting requirements. National tariff schedules diverge below the six-digit level precisely because countries add their own further distinctions. Tariff schedules are established at the federal level, not by individual state governments.

The bolts are minor fasteners and do not control classification of the entire finished article. GRI 2(a) applies broadly across article types, including furniture, whenever the unassembled-goods test is met.

The Federal Maritime Commission regulates ocean shipping matters unrelated to tariff classification rulings. The Department of Commerce plays a role in other trade remedy proceedings such as antidumping investigations, but it does not issue binding HTSUS classification rulings. A private attorney can assist in preparing a ruling request but has no authority to issue the binding ruling itself.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Chapter and Section Notes carry binding legal force and must be consulted to determine whether printing affects classification of the finished garment, but ordinary decorative printing typically does not change the garment's chapter): Section and Chapter Notes are legally binding and must always be reviewed alongside heading text under GRI 1, and for apparel, decorative surface printing generally does not remove the garment from its normal textile apparel heading. Claiming printing always shifts the article into a wholly different printed-materials chapter misstates how these Notes typically function for finished wearing apparel. Notes cannot be disregarded, since GRI 1 expressly requires their consideration. The identity of the printer's country has no bearing on tariff classification, which concerns the nature of the article itself.
  2. The correct answer is D (GRI 5(b) generally classifies such containers with the goods they contain when they are of a kind normally used for packing those particular goods, unless the container is clearly suitable for repetitive use): GRI 5(b) directs that packing materials and containers presented with goods be classified with those goods when they are of the customary kind used to package that merchandise, with an exception carved out for containers that are clearly designed for repetitive use, such as durable, reusable industrial containers. A perfume bottle of ordinary single-brand, single-use design fits the general rule rather than the repetitive-use exception. Chapter 98 concerns previously exported American goods returning unchanged and has no application to a newly imported filled bottle. GRI 3(c) is a last-resort tie-breaker unrelated to packing container analysis.
  3. The correct answer is A (GRI 3(a), preferring the more specific description over the general one): GRI 3(a) directs that when goods appear classifiable under two or more headings, the heading providing the most specific description is preferred over one that is more general. A heading naming the article by name or function outranks one that merely describes a class it belongs to. GRI 3(c) is only reached as a last resort when 3(a) and 3(b) both fail to resolve the classification. Neither GRI 5(a) nor GRI 2(a) applies to a finished, fully assembled chair.
  4. The correct answer is C (The items must be classifiable under different headings, put up together to meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity, and packaged for retail sale without repacking): these are the three well-established conditions for a retail set under GRI 3(b), and once satisfied, the whole set is classified according to whichever component gives it its essential character. Fabric composition is not itself a qualifying factor for set status. Neither a minimum dollar value threshold nor a common country of manufacture is part of the recognized set test. The necktie, pocket square, and tie clip together satisfy the shared purpose requirement of complementing a man's formal attire.
  5. The correct answer is A (According to the heading whose terms and applicable legal notes describe the frame): GRI 1 is the controlling rule and directs the classifier to the heading language and any Section or Chapter Notes first. Only when the heading terms and notes do not resolve the classification does a classifier move on to GRI 2 through 6. Duty rate is never a factor in choosing a heading. This frame is fully described by heading terms, so no other GRI is needed.
  6. The correct answer is B (A use provision, generally governed by principal use of the class or kind of merchandise under Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a)): because the heading ties classification to the class or kind of goods used for packing rather than naming the article by its specific name, it functions as a use provision, and Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a) directs that principal use be assessed for the class or kind of merchandise as a whole, not any single importer's subjective intent. An eo nomine provision instead names the article specifically without regard to use, which does not describe this use-based heading. GRI 5(a) governs fitted cases presented with goods, an unrelated concept to a use-based classification analysis. A genuine use provision always requires factual support such as channels of trade and physical characteristics, never a determination made with no analysis at all.
  7. The correct answer is C (In Chapter 99, which lists temporary modifications and additional duties applied in conjunction with the underlying Chapter 1 through 97 classification): Chapter 99 is specifically designated within the HTSUS for temporary legislative and administrative trade actions, such as national security duties, layered on top of the merchandise's regular classification found elsewhere in the schedule. The Chapter Notes to Chapter 72 address steel-specific classification issues within that chapter, not separate trade remedy duty programs. The General Rules of Interpretation govern the analytical process for determining the base classification, not additional temporary duty rates. The alphabetical index is a non-binding research aid and carries no independent legal or rate information.
  8. The correct answer is A (It is a single composite article rather than a set of separate articles, and GRI 3(b) still applies by classifying it according to the component giving it essential character): GRI 3(b) applies to composite goods made up of different components permanently combined into a single physical article, as well as to goods put up in sets for retail sale, and in both situations the classification follows the component or material that gives the whole article its essential character. A composite good like a combined tool is not left unclassifiable simply because its components are fused into one unit rather than packaged separately, since GRI 3(b) explicitly covers composite goods of this kind. A single imported article is classified once under one heading, not divided into separate dutiable components based on each function it performs. GRI 5's packing rule has no application to an article that is not itself a container or packing material for other goods.
  9. The correct answer is C (A prospective transaction involving a specific, actual product with sufficient detail for CBP to classify it): 19 CFR Part 177 binding rulings are issued for prospective transactions, meaning the merchandise has not yet been imported, and the request must include enough factual and technical detail, often including samples or specifications, for CBP to make a definitive classification. CBP does not issue rulings based purely on a desired duty outcome, since the classification must follow the GRIs and legal notes. Hypothetical or purely speculative products generally cannot receive a binding ruling. Rulings on already-liquidated past entries fall outside the binding ruling process and belong to protest or other post-entry procedures.

The correct answer is C (GRI 2(a), which extends a heading for a complete article to the same article unassembled): GRI 2(a) directly addresses this scenario, extending the heading for a complete article to cover that same article presented unassembled or disassembled, so long as the essential character of the complete article is already present. The shaped tabletop and legs together with the assembly hardware already form a recognizable table requiring only routine assembly. GRI 4 is a rule of last resort used only when GRI 1 through 3, including the GRI 2(a) extension, cannot resolve the classification, which is not the case here. GRI 5(a) applies to fitted cases specifically designed to hold particular goods, not ordinary assembly hardware.
  12. The correct answer is D (An eo nomine provision names an article by its specific name and, absent contrary limiting language, generally covers all forms of that article regardless of the particular use to which any individual unit is put): a heading that simply names an article, such as umbrellas, without qualifying use-based language, generally covers the article in all its forms unless the heading text or applicable notes expressly limit it. The garden ornament umbrellas therefore still fall within the eo nomine heading despite their decorative rather than functional use. Domestic manufacture has no bearing on an eo nomine classification, which turns on the article's identity, not its origin. A use-based principal use finding under Additional US Rule of Interpretation 1(a) is required only for genuine use provisions, not for a straightforward eo nomine heading like this one.

The correct answer is D (9802.00, covering articles exported for repairs or alterations, generally allowing duty only on the value of the repairs): the Chapter 98 repairs and alterations provisions allow duty to be assessed only on the value added by the repair or alteration performed abroad, rather than on the full value of the returned article. This differs from the American goods returned provision, which applies when there has been no advancement in value or change in condition at all. Chapter 99 governs separate, additional trade remedy duties layered onto the underlying Chapter 1 through 97 classification. An eo nomine provision simply names an article by name and does not itself address repair value treatment.
  15. The correct answer is B (The broker failed to apply GRI 1's requirement to consider relevant section and chapter notes, which legally exclude the article from that general chapter): GRI 1 requires classification according to the terms of the headings and any relative section or chapter notes, and those notes carry binding legal effect rather than serving as optional guidance a broker may disregard. Failing to check for an applicable exclusionary note before settling on a general chapter is exactly the kind of error GRI 1's note requirement is designed to prevent. GRI 4 addresses goods that cannot be classified under any preceding rule at all, which is not the situation here since a specific note and a correct alternate chapter both exist. GRI 5's packing rule has no bearing on a substantive exclusionary note governing which chapter properly covers the article itself.
  16. The correct answer is D (HQ can review, modify, or revoke a previously issued NY ruling through the notice and comment procedures required by law): CBP's Office of Regulations and Rulings at Headquarters has authority to review and, where warranted, modify or revoke prior ruling letters, including NY rulings, but must generally follow public notice and comment procedures before a modification takes effect. NY rulings are not permanent and can be superseded when CBP determines the original classification was incorrect. NY and HQ rulings do not carry identical review authority, since HQ sits above the district-level NY rulings in the ruling hierarchy. Any interested party, not only the original requester, may trigger review through protest, further ruling requests, or CBP's own initiative.

  18. The correct answer is A (GRI 6, which applies GRI 1 through 5 by analogy at the subheading level, comparing only subheadings at the same level of the same heading): GRI 6 provides that classification of goods within the subheadings of a heading is determined according to the terms of those subheadings and any related subheading notes, applying GRI 1 through 5 by analogy, with only subheadings at the same level being comparable to one another. Once the correct heading has already been identified, this is precisely the rule that governs the remaining choice among its competing subheadings. GRI 3(c) can still operate at the subheading level by analogy through GRI 6 if needed, but the general 3(c) rule itself is a heading-level provision, not the source of authority for the subheading comparison. GRI 2(a) and GRI 4 address entirely different situations, incomplete articles and unclassifiable novel goods respectively, neither of which describes a routine dispute between two subheadings of an already-selected heading.
  19. The correct answer is C (Sections group related chapters, chapters are identified by two-digit numbers, headings are four-digit provisions within chapters, and subheadings further subdivide headings): The HTSUS is organized hierarchically, with Sections grouping related Chapters by broad subject matter, Chapters identified by two-digit numbers covering a category of goods, four-digit headings within each chapter providing more specific descriptions, and subheadings at the six, eight, and ten-digit levels further subdividing those headings with increasing specificity. Reversing this hierarchy, treating subheadings as broader than sections, misstates the entire structure of the schedule. Chapters and sections are distinct organizational levels, not interchangeable terms, with each section typically containing several related chapters. Headings and subheadings are identified by their numeric digit strings, not by letters or roman numerals, throughout the HTSUS.

The correct answer is B (No, because the items do not together meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity as GRI 3(b) requires, so each is classified separately): GRI 3(b) applies only to goods put up together to meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity, and mere co-packing for shipping convenience does not satisfy that requirement, so this shipment falls back to classifying each item separately in its own appropriate heading under GRI 1. Simply placing different articles in one container does not automatically create a qualifying set, since the rule requires a genuine functional relationship among the components toward one shared purpose. GRI 3(b) is not limited to exactly two articles and can apply to sets with more components, as long as the particular-need requirement is otherwise satisfied. Because that functional relationship is absent here, the essential character analysis under GRI 3(b) is never reached.

  24. The correct answer is A (Publish notice and provide an opportunity for public comment before modifying or revoking the ruling, as required by the procedures in 19 CFR Part 177): 19 CFR Part 177 requires CBP to follow specific procedures, generally including published notice and an opportunity for interested parties to comment, before modifying or revoking a ruling that has been in effect, reflecting the reliance interests importers have in binding rulings. CBP cannot simply disregard a ruling internally without following this process, since doing so would undermine the certainty the binding ruling system is designed to provide. No court order is required, since modification and revocation are administrative procedures conducted by CBP itself under its own regulations. Importer consent is not a legal prerequisite for CBP to revoke or modify a ruling, though the importer is given the opportunity to comment during the process.
  25. The correct answer is B (Routine matters are typically handled by the National Commodity Specialist Division in New York, while complex or novel legal issues are more often addressed by headquarters): The National Commodity Specialist Division in New York typically issues rulings on routine classification and marking questions, given its staff of commodity specialists organized by product area, while the Office of Regulations and Rulings at CBP headquarters more often addresses complex, novel, or precedential legal issues, sometimes after a port or the New York office refers a difficult question upward. Both offices issue genuinely binding rulings under 19 CFR Part 177, so classification authority is not exclusive to either one. Ruling requests are submitted to CBP's ruling offices rather than directly to the port where goods will physically arrive. Headquarters handles far more than enforcement matters, since ruling issuance is a core part of its function alongside internal advice and protest review.

  28. The correct answer is A (The part is classified together with the machine it is suitable for use with, following the specific parts note rather than a general basket provision): When a legally binding section or chapter note directs that parts suitable for use solely or principally with a particular kind of machine are classified with machines of that kind, that specific parts note controls the classification of the part under GRI 1, rather than a general residual parts basket provision. This rule reflects the tariff schedule's general preference for aligning dedicated parts with the machinery they serve when a note or heading specifically provides for that treatment. GRI 5 concerns packing materials and containers, not the classification of a functional machine part imported on its own. A part is fully classifiable when imported alone and does not need to be physically joined with the machine at the time of entry.

  30. The correct answer is B (CBP is bound by the ruling and must apply the classification stated in it to entries of the merchandise as described, until the ruling is modified or revoked): A binding ruling issued under 19 CFR Part 177 binds CBP nationwide with respect to the transactions or merchandise described in the ruling, and CBP must apply that classification consistently until the ruling is properly modified or revoked through the procedures Part 177 provides. The ruling is not limited to the individual officer who signed it, since its binding effect runs against the agency as a whole for the described merchandise. There is no automatic ninety-day expiration built into the ruling process, and a ruling remains effective indefinitely absent a change in facts or a formal modification. The ruling binds CBP to the classification itself, which in turn determines the applicable duty rate, rather than binding only the importer while leaving CBP free to classify differently.
  31. The correct answer is A (Under the eo nomine heading naming the article by its own specific name, since that classification does not depend on any individual importer's actual use): An eo nomine provision classifies merchandise according to the specific name by which it is commercially known, without regard to the actual use any individual importer makes of a particular shipment. This differs fundamentally from a principal use provision, which turns on the use of the class or kind of goods to which the imported article belongs as a whole, evaluated by criteria courts have developed such as the general physical characteristics, the channels of trade, and the expectation of the ultimate purchasers, not one importer's personal plan. Because the article is named directly by its own specific term, the eo nomine heading applies regardless of what this particular importer intends to do with it. An importer is never permitted to select a classification simply because it produces a lower duty rate.

  36. The correct answer is A (The note has binding legal effect under GRI 1 and must be followed even though the article seems to fit the general heading): GRI 1 makes clear that classification is determined according to the terms of the headings and any relative section or chapter notes, giving those notes binding legal effect rather than mere advisory status. When a note excludes a category of goods from a chapter or redirects them elsewhere, that exclusion must be followed even if the heading's own wording might otherwise seem to cover the article. Notes do not require a separate binding ruling to take legal effect, since they are part of the legal text of the HTSUS itself, effective upon publication. A broad general heading never overrides a specific, validly enacted note that excludes or redirects particular goods.

  42. The correct answer is B (GRI 4, which classifies the goods under the heading for the article to which they are most akin): GRI 4 provides that goods which cannot be classified in accordance with the preceding rules are classified under the heading appropriate to the goods to which they are most akin, considering factors such as description, character, and purpose. This rule is reached only after GRI 1, 2, and 3 have genuinely failed to produce a classification, which is the situation with a truly novel article unaddressed by any existing heading or note. GRI 6 governs comparison of subheadings within an already-identified heading and presumes a heading has already been selected, so it cannot apply first. Neither GRI 5's packing rule nor GRI 2(b)'s mixture rule is designed to resolve the classification of an entirely novel article lacking any comparable heading.
  43. The correct answer is A (The container is classified together with the goods it holds, since it is of a kind normally used for packing that type of merchandise): GRI 5(b) provides that packing materials and packing containers presented with the goods they contain are classified with those goods when they are of a kind normally used for packing such goods, unless the container is clearly suitable for repetitive use as independent, separately marketable merchandise. A fitted, ordinary carrying case or crate of the kind normally used to pack that category of goods falls within this rule and is not broken out separately. GRI 5 is not limited to textile materials and applies broadly to boxes, crates, cases, and similar packing regardless of the material from which they are made. The container does not drive classification of the goods themselves, since GRI 5 works in the opposite direction, from the goods to the packing.
  44. The correct answer is B (GRI 3(c) applies, and the goods are classified under the heading that occurs last in numerical order among the equally valid headings): GRI 3(c) is a last-resort tiebreaker that applies only when goods cannot be classified under GRI 3(a) or GRI 3(b), directing that the goods be classified under the heading that occurs last in numerical order among those equally meriting consideration. This rule exists precisely for situations like a genuinely balanced composite article where no material or component can be identified as giving essential character. GRI 4 is reached only when no heading can be found through GRI 1 through 3 at all, which is different from having multiple equally specific headings that simply cannot be ranked. Goods are never left unclassified pending a ruling, and the importer has no discretion to choose among competing headings once GRI 3(c) applies.
  45. The correct answer is A (As if it consisted of the material or component that gives the set its essential character): GRI 3(b) provides that goods put up in sets for retail sale, consisting of different articles that meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity, are classified as if they consisted of the component that gives the set its essential character. Essential character is determined by weighing factors such as the bulk, quantity, weight, value, and role each component plays in relation to the overall use of the set, not simply by which item weighs the most. There is no separate residual heading created just for retail sets, since GRI 3(b) instead directs classification by the essential-character component. This approach keeps the set together under one classification rather than dividing it among several headings.

  50. The correct answer is B (The heading providing the most specific description of the goods is preferred over a heading providing a more general description): GRI 3(a) directs that when goods are prima facie classifiable under two or more headings, the heading providing the most specific description is preferred over a heading providing a more general description. This rule is applied before considering essential character under GRI 3(b) or the last-in-order tiebreaker under GRI 3(c). Numerical order under GRI 3(a) itself is irrelevant, since specificity of description, not sequence in the schedule, is the controlling factor. An importer does not have discretion to select a classification based on duty rate, and competing headings are never combined or stacked together.
  51. The correct answer is A (GRI 1, because classification is governed first by the terms of the headings and any relevant section or chapter notes): GRI 1 requires that classification be determined according to the terms of the headings and any relevant section or chapter notes before turning to any other rule. When a heading names the specific article, that specific heading is applied under GRI 1's plain terms rather than jumping to a residual or comparative rule. GRI 4 and GRI 3(c) are reached only if GRI 1 fails to resolve the classification, which is not the case here since the tariff text itself describes the goods. Packaging under GRI 5 has no bearing on classifying the article named in the heading.
  52. The correct answer is C (GRI 4, which classifies the goods under the heading for the article to which they are most akin): GRI 4 provides that goods which cannot be classified in accordance with the preceding rules are classified under the heading appropriate to the goods to which they are most akin, considering factors such as description, character, and purpose. This rule is reached only after GRI 1, 2, and 3 have genuinely failed to produce a classification, which is the situation with a truly novel article unaddressed by any existing heading or note. GRI 6 governs comparison of subheadings within an already-identified heading and presumes a heading has already been selected, so it cannot apply first. Neither GRI 5's packing rule nor GRI 2(b)'s mixture rule is designed to resolve the classification of an entirely novel article lacking any comparable heading.

  56. The correct answer is D (As if it consisted of the material or component that gives the set its essential character): GRI 3(b) provides that goods put up in sets for retail sale, consisting of different articles that meet a particular need or carry out a specific activity, are classified as if they consisted of the component that gives the set its essential character. Essential character is determined by weighing factors such as the bulk, quantity, weight, value, and role each component plays in relation to the overall use of the set, not simply by which item weighs the most. There is no separate residual heading created just for retail sets, since GRI 3(b) instead directs classification by the essential-character component. This approach keeps the set together under one classification rather than dividing it among several headings.
  57. The correct answer is A (They are the legal roadmap and apply in strict numerical order): the General Rules of Interpretation are printed at the front of the HTSUS and are the legal roadmap for classifying any article. They are not optional guidance, and a classifier does not move to the next rule until the current one fails to produce a clear answer. GRI 1 begins with the heading terms and the relevant notes, and later rules apply only as needed. The broker applies the GRIs in order and does not treat them as advisory examples.
  58. The correct answer is D (The heading providing the most specific description of the goods is preferred over a heading providing a more general description): GRI 3(a) directs that when goods are prima facie classifiable under two or more headings, the heading providing the most specific description is preferred over a heading providing a more general description. This rule is applied before considering essential character under GRI 3(b) or the last-in-order tiebreaker under GRI 3(c). Numerical order under GRI 3(a) itself is irrelevant, since specificity of description, not sequence in the schedule, is the controlling factor. It grants duty free or reduced treatment tied to documented conditions rather than replacing the article's identity.
