  62. The correct answer is C (GRI 2(b), which extends any heading referring to a material to mixtures or combinations of that material with others): GRI 2(b) provides that any reference in a heading to a material or substance includes mixtures or combinations of that material with other materials, and any reference to goods of a given material includes goods consisting wholly or partly of that material. This rule is what pulls a blended product like this one into the scope of headings written for its component materials. Once GRI 2(b) has expanded the field of potentially applicable headings, GRI 3 is then used to choose among them if more than one heading appears to apply. The most-akin-article rule describes a rule reached only as a last resort after GRI 1 through 3 fail, which is premature at this stage.
  63. The correct answer is A (The ruling can later be revoked or modified, so the importer should monitor changes): a binding ruling is legally binding on CBP for future entries of the identical merchandise described, provided the facts stay accurate. It can later be revoked or modified, and CBP generally follows notice and a comment period before changing it. The importer should therefore monitor CBP actions that affect the ruling and keep the described facts current. The broker treats the ruling as reliable but not permanent when advising the importer.

  70. A published ruling on materially identical goods is strong persuasive authority even for a party that did not request it. The HTSUS is the tariff schedule itself, and the World Customs Organization maintains the international Harmonized System. The broker uses CROSS as a research tool before deciding whether to request a new ruling.

  72. The correct answer is D (Classify the article under the heading for the goods it is most akin to): GRI 4 applies only in rare cases when goods cannot be classified under any preceding rule. The use must be intended at importation, the goods must actually be so used, and proof must be established within three years of entry. These documentary requirements are set out under Rule 1(b). The broker supports the entry with end use certificates rather than relying on the importer's stated intention alone.

Rule 1(b) is the separate authority that adds documentary requirements to an actual use provision. The broker applies the correct rule of interpretation before evaluating the evidence for the brushes.

A bracket used with several kinds of machine tools is not dedicated to one, so it does not follow any single machine. Such parts typically fall to a residual parts heading under the Section XVI rules. The broker classifies the bracket there rather than assigning it to one machine type.

The guard protects the operator but does not make the machine operate, so it is an accessory rather than a part. Accessories are often classified with the article they are designed for when principally used with it. The broker classifies the guard with the machine on that basis.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (GRI 1, because the heading names lithium-ion accumulators): a heading in the schedule names the article directly, so GRI 1 supplies the classification. The cells and plastic housing are components of a finished article that the heading already covers, so no mixture analysis is needed. The retail box is ordinary packing and follows the battery under GRI 5(b), which does not change the heading for the battery itself. The broker classifies the packs at GRI 1.
  2. The correct answer is A (GRI 3(b), classifying by the material giving essential character): the laminated foil is a composite of aluminum and paper, and two headings each cover only part of the article. GRI 2(b) extends each material heading to the composite and sends the classifier on to GRI 3. Because the aluminum supplies the barrier function and most of the value, it gives the article its essential character under GRI 3(b). GRI 3(c) applies only if essential character cannot be determined, so the broker does not need the numerical order tiebreaker.
  3. The correct answer is B (With the class of mowing machines, because their principal use is with that class): the parts rule covers a part suitable for use solely or principally with a particular kind of machine. Principal use looks at the class or kind of goods as a whole, not whether one blade fits a single model. These blades are used chiefly with one class of mowing machines, so they follow that class under the parts provision. A residual parts heading would apply only if the blades were used with multiple kinds of machines and were not principally used with one class.
  4. The correct answer is D (As a part of the motorcycle it is designed to serve): a part suitable for use solely or principally with a particular kind of machine is classified with that machine under a heading or note that provides for parts. The frame is designed for one motorcycle model and is not named in a heading of its own. GRI 2(a) does not make it a complete motorcycle, because a frame alone does not have the essential character of the finished vehicle. The broker classifies the frame as a part of the motorcycle.
  5. The correct answer is A (GRI 2(a) does not apply because the parts lack the essential character of a bicycle): GRI 2(a) extends a heading to unassembled articles only when they have the essential character of the complete article when presented. This shipment lacks wheels, drivetrain and frame, so it cannot be classified as complete bicycles. The goods are therefore classified as the parts they actually are, each under its own heading. The broker does not use GRI 3(b) or GRI 5(b) to force a complete bicycle classification.
  6. The correct answer is B (The drums may be classified separately because they are clearly reusable): GRI 5(b) classifies ordinary packing materials and containers with the goods they contain. It makes an exception when the packing is clearly reusable, such as a sturdy metal drum with value beyond a single shipment. These drums are designed for repeated return and refill, so they are not ordinary one-trip packing. The broker may therefore classify the drums separately from the adhesive chemicals.
  7. The correct answer is C (As an eo nomine provision naming the article without regard to use): an eo nomine provision names an article by its specific name and does not depend on where the buyer will install it. The heading names the lamps directly, so the studio setting does not move the goods to another heading. A principal use or actual use test would apply only if the competing heading were written as a use provision. The broker classifies the lamps by the heading that names them.
  8. The correct answer is D (As an accessory classified with the loom, because it is principally used with it): an accessory enhances the usefulness of an article without being essential to its function. Such goods are often classified with the article they are designed for when they are principally used with it. The attachment is not required for the loom to operate, so it is an accessory rather than a part in the strict sense. The broker follows the principal use of the attachment and classifies it with the loom.
  9. The correct answer is B (The unwrought nickel heading, because the plates are cast and not further worked): GRI 1 looks to the heading terms, and the schedule separates unwrought nickel from worked nickel articles. Casting a rectangular form does not amount to working the metal beyond the unwrought stage described in the schedule. Because the plates have not been further worked, the unwrought nickel heading describes them. The end use in plating baths does not control when the heading names the article by its condition.
  10. The correct answer is D (The nozzle's own heading, because a part named there is classified there): Section XVI explains that a part itself specifically covered by its own heading, such as a pump or valve, is classified there rather than as a generic part of machinery. The specific heading naming the nozzle takes priority over the general parts rule that would otherwise classify it with the engine. The residual parts heading applies to parts not specifically covered elsewhere and to parts used with multiple kinds of machines. The broker classifies the nozzle under its own heading.
  11. The correct answer is C (GRI 1, because the heading names hand saws directly): the heading names hand saws, which describes the article as presented. GRI 1 resolves the classification from the heading and the relevant Notes, and no Note removes these goods from that coverage. The blade and handle are parts of a finished article that the heading already names, so no composite analysis is needed. The broker stops at GRI 1 and does not move to GRI 3(b) or GRI 5(b).
  12. The correct answer is B (The United States International Trade Commission, which publishes the HTSUS): the HTSUS is published by the United States International Trade Commission and is the legal document CBP uses to assign duty rates. The World Customs Organization maintains the international Harmonized System on which the HTSUS is built, but it does not publish the US schedule. CROSS is CBP's public ruling database, and the Office of Regulations and Rulings issues HQ rulings. The broker identifies the International Trade Commission as the publisher of the schedule.
  13. The correct answer is D (The case is classified with the saxophone because it is fitted to that article): GRI 5(a) covers cases specially shaped or fitted to contain a specific article, suitable for long term use and presented with that article. The molded, lined case is of a kind normally sold with the instrument, so it follows the saxophone's classification. GRI 5(b) governs ordinary packing materials and containers, not a fitted case of this kind. The broker classifies the case together with the saxophone rather than as a separate article.
  14. The correct answer is C (The Section Note, because binding notes can exclude goods from a heading): Section Notes are legally binding and can exclude goods from a chapter even when the heading language appears to cover them. GRI 1 requires the classifier to read the heading together with the applicable notes before settling the classification. Because the Note directs insulated electrical conductors elsewhere, the plain copper wire heading does not control this article. The broker follows the Note and classifies the insulated wire in the chapter the Note identifies.
  15. The correct answer is B (The heading text and the Chapter Notes read together under GRI 1): GRI 1 states that classification is determined by the terms of the headings and any relevant Section or Chapter Notes. The heading covers printed books and similar printed articles, and the Notes may define or limit that coverage. The identity of the intended reader does not become a use test when the heading describes the printed article itself. The broker therefore classifies the manuals from the heading and the notes rather than from the marketing audience.
  16. The correct answer is C (Compare the subheadings using GRI 1 through GRI 5 in order): GRI 6 provides that subheadings within a heading are compared using GRI 1 through GRI 5, substituting subheading for heading. Section and Chapter Notes still apply unless the context requires otherwise. The broker therefore repeats the same disciplined analysis at the six-digit level rather than inventing a different test. Duty rates and printed order are not the tiebreakers that GRI 6 supplies.
  17. The correct answer is D (The material that gives the carpet its visible character and value): GRI 3(b) determines essential character from the whole picture, including bulk, quantity, weight, value and the role each material plays. The wool face dominates the visible surface, the look and feel, and the value, so it supplies the essential character of the carpet. There is no fixed rule that the heavier material wins, and the analysis is factual rather than mechanical. The broker classifies the carpet by the wool that defines it rather than by the jute backing.
  18. The correct answer is B (The broker moves to GRI 3 to resolve the competing headings): GRI 2(b) is a bridge rule that brings a material heading into play when a material is mixed or combined with others. It expressly sends the classifier on to GRI 3 when two or more headings appear to apply equally. GRI 3 then works through most specific description, essential character and last in numerical order in that sequence. Applying GRI 4 before GRI 3 would skip a required step in the analysis.
  19. The correct answer is A (The heading text and the notes are binding, while the Section title is not): GRI 1 states that Section, Chapter and Subchapter titles are for ease of reference only and carry no legal weight. Only the terms of the headings and any relevant Section or Chapter Notes are binding on the classifier. The Section title mentioning animal fodder therefore cannot override the more precise heading language for the dog food. The broker builds the classification from the heading and the notes, using the title only as a reading aid.
  20. The correct answer is B (GRI 2(b), which extends a material heading to mixtures of that material): GRI 2(b) extends any heading naming a material to that material mixed or combined with others, and it is a bridge rule. It does not choose a winner itself, but sends the classifier on to GRI 3 when a mixture appears to fall under two or more headings. GRI 3(b) then decides the outcome through the essential character of the product. The broker therefore uses GRI 2(b) to bring the mixture within the material headings before applying the tiebreakers in GRI 3.
  21. The correct answer is C (GRI 3(a), because the heading with the most specific description is preferred): GRI 3(a) gives preference to the heading that provides the most specific description over a more general one. The silk yarn heading is more specific than the general textile yarn heading because it names the article and its material. GRI 3(c) applies only after GRI 3(a) and GRI 3(b) fail to resolve the choice, and it does not operate as a general preference. The broker applies GRI 3(a) and selects the specific silk yarn heading.
  22. The correct answer is D (GRI 1, because the heading describes the tires directly): GRI 1 resolves classification from the heading terms and the relevant notes, and the heading names new pneumatic rubber tires. The reinforcing cord inside the tire does not create a competing material heading, because the heading already describes the finished product. GRI 2(b) applies when a material heading must be stretched to reach a mixture, not when a heading names the goods themselves. The broker stops at GRI 1 and does not reach the later rules.
  23. The correct answer is C (The stoppers are classified under the cork heading by their own material): the heading covers articles of natural cork, which describes the stoppers directly. GRI 5(b) applies to packing materials and containers presented with the goods they contain, and the stoppers are entered as merchandise in their own right. No use provision competes with the material heading, so a principal use or actual use analysis is not required. The broker classifies the stoppers by the heading that names their material.
  24. The correct answer is D (GRI 1, because the heading and notes describe the preparation directly): when the heading text covers the preparation as presented, GRI 1 supplies the classification and no further rule is needed. The heading names modeling pastes and similar preparations, which describes this product more completely than the headings for the separate raw materials. GRI 2(b) would be relevant only if the mixture required a material heading to reach it before a tie developed. The broker first applies GRI 1 and stops there because the description is already complete.
  25. The correct answer is C (The Chapter Note, because Notes are binding and can route goods elsewhere): Section and Chapter Notes are legally binding text that GRI 1 directs the classifier to read together with the headings. A Note can exclude goods from a chapter or direct them to a different chapter even when the heading language seems to cover them. The broker must therefore check the notes before finalizing the heading for this windshield. Ignoring the Note and relying on the general safety glass heading is a common cause of a wrong classification.
  26. The correct answer is A (The rate, value, and amount originally asserted by the importer at entry): Deemed liquidation under 19 U.S.C. 1504 locks in the rate, value, and amount of duty originally asserted by the importer at the time of entry, since CBP's own failure to act within the statutory period effectively forfeits its ability to make some different determination later. CBP cannot retroactively substitute a different, less favorable rate once deemed liquidation has already legally occurred, which is precisely the protection the statute was designed to provide importers. A default zero percent rate is certainly not the applicable standard here, since the originally entered rate simply controls the outcome. Applying the single highest possible HTSUS rate would directly contradict the entire purpose behind the deemed liquidation finality rule.
  27. The correct answer is D (The internationally harmonized HS code shared by many countries): the first six digits are formed by the chapter, heading and subheading, and together they make up the HS code. The Harmonized System is maintained by the World Customs Organization and is used by more than 200 countries and territories. Because the six digits are internationally harmonized, a US broker can often match a foreign customs document at that level. The final four digits remain a US statistical suffix and are not part of the international agreement.
  28. The correct answer is B (The deadline generally moves forward to the next available business day): When a regulatory filing deadline happens to fall on a weekend or a federal holiday, it is generally treated as falling on the next available business day, consistent with standard CBP practice for computing these statutory filing periods. The deadline certainly does not simply become void or disappear, nor does it move backward to some earlier calendar date. A full thirty-day extension is not automatically granted merely because the calculated deadline happens to fall on a non-business day. Brokers should still plan to file well before the weekend whenever practical, to avoid unnecessary last-minute processing complications.
  29. The correct answer is D (Apply GRI 6 to select the proper six-digit subheading within that heading): GRI 6 provides that subheadings are compared using GRI 1 through GRI 5, substituting subheading for heading. Once the four-digit heading is settled, the broker works through the subheading level to reach the six-digit code. The sugar and fruit in marmalade are not competing materials that require GRI 2(b), because the heading names the finished preparation. The final step is the statistical suffix within the chosen subheading, which the broker completes after the GRI 6 analysis.
  30. The correct answer is B (The port of entry along with the entry type and merchandise description): Under 19 CFR 142.3, the entry/immediate delivery filing must show the port of entry, the entry type, and a description of the merchandise sufficient for CBP to determine admissibility. Retail markup percentages and hull insurance policy details are not required entry data elements at all. The importer's corporate tax filing number is likewise unrelated to CBP's entry release requirements. Accurate, complete entry-level data allows CBP officers to make a timely and informed release decision.
  31. The correct answer is C (By locating the two-digit chapter that groups vinegar and related products): the chapter number forms the first two digits of every classification placed within it, so the chapter is the entry point. Within that chapter the broker then identifies the four-digit heading, the six-digit subheading and the US statistical suffix. GRI 3(c) is a last resort tiebreaker and has no role when a chapter plainly groups the product. Starting at the chapter level keeps the analysis aligned with the hierarchy of the schedule.
  32. The correct answer is C (Resolve the four-digit heading first, then work to the subheading level): classification narrows from general to specific, so the four-digit heading is settled before the six-digit subheading is chosen. GRI 6 applies the GRI 1 through GRI 5 logic at the subheading level only after the heading and its notes are resolved. Because the heading plainly covers green coffee beans, the heading question ends at GRI 1. The broker then repeats the analysis at the subheading level to reach the correct six-digit code.
  33. The correct answer is B (ACE supports the International Trade Data System, sharing data with participating agencies): ACE underpins the broader International Trade Data System, allowing relevant import data to be shared efficiently with other participating government agencies that have a genuine regulatory interest in specific shipments, such as the FDA or USDA on certain products. It would be incorrect to claim the data is never shared beyond CBP itself, since interagency data sharing is actually a core, intended function of the ACE system. ACE is certainly not a state-level sales tax reporting tool of any kind, nor is it limited narrowly to Department of Defense procurement matters. This single-window approach meaningfully reduces duplicate reporting burdens placed on the importer across multiple agencies.
  34. The correct answer is B (No later than twenty-four hours before the cargo is laden aboard the vessel): Under 19 CFR 149.2, the Importer Security Filing must generally be transmitted at least twenty-four hours before the cargo is laden aboard the vessel that is destined for the United States. This timing is measured from the moment of lading, not from the vessel's departure or its eventual arrival, which is a very common point of confusion among newer filers. Filing late, or filing with inaccurate required data, can subject the importer to a liquidated damages claim under the ISF bond. The ISF filing is entirely separate from, and precedes, the later entry filing itself.
  35. The correct answer is D (One full year from the actual date of entry, absent any extension or suspension): Absent any suspension or a validly granted extension, CBP generally must liquidate an entry within one full year from the actual date of entry under 19 U.S.C. 1504(a), or else the entry deems liquidated as originally entered by operation of law. The three hundred fourteen day figure instead applies specifically to entries following the removal of a suspension, which is an entirely different scenario than the standard, non-suspended timeline. Ten working days corresponds instead to the earlier entry summary filing deadline, a wholly separate and unrelated requirement in the process. Five years is not the applicable statutory liquidation period, though CBP may still extend the standard one-year period under certain specified circumstances, such as a pending value or classification determination.
  36. The correct answer is A (It may be sold sooner than the standard general order period allows): Perishable merchandise placed into general order may be disposed of, typically through an accelerated sale, sooner than the standard storage period because of the very real risk of spoilage and a corresponding loss of commercial value over time. Holding perishable goods for the entire full standard storage period would often destroy their value completely, defeating the whole practical purpose of an accelerated disposal procedure in the first place. Automatic charity donation and a mandatory twenty-four hour export-only requirement do not accurately reflect how the general order disposal framework actually operates for these goods. This accelerated handling ultimately protects both government revenue interests and the practical realities that perishable cargo genuinely presents.
  37. The correct answer is C (Entry type 06, the foreign trade zone consumption entry filed on withdrawal): Entry type 06 is the foreign trade zone consumption entry filed when merchandise is finally withdrawn from a zone for entry into domestic commerce after storage or processing. Type 01 does not reflect or account for the merchandise's special foreign trade zone status during its time in the zone. Type 21 instead applies to bonded warehouse withdrawals, which is a distinct legal status from an FTZ admission. Correctly coding the FTZ withdrawal preserves the duty deferral and inverted tariff benefits that the zone status had provided while goods sat in storage.
  38. The correct answer is A (CBP may hold or delay release until the required information is provided): CBP may withhold release under 19 CFR 142.3 whenever the entry document lacks information needed to determine admissibility, and this includes case-specific antidumping case numbers and cash deposit rates. Antidumping requirements are never simply waived because supporting paperwork happens to be incomplete at the time of filing. Release cannot lawfully occur before admissibility is properly established through the required documentation. Liquidation only follows much later, after a completed and accurate entry summary has been filed and finalized.
  39. The correct answer is D (Six months from the actual date of importation of the merchandise): Under 19 CFR 127.14, merchandise that remains unclaimed in general order storage generally may be sold or otherwise disposed of once it has remained in continuous CBP custody for six months from the actual date of importation. Ten working days and twenty-four hours are both far too short a period and instead correspond to entirely different, unrelated deadlines elsewhere in the entry process. Five years vastly overstates the real storage window that precedes the start of any disposal proceedings under these regulations. The importer nonetheless retains the right to still enter and formally claim the merchandise before disposal actually occurs, upon paying all applicable duties, taxes, and accrued storage charges.
  40. The correct answer is B (Three hundred fourteen days from the date the suspension was actually removed): Under 19 U.S.C. 1504(d), once a suspension of liquidation is finally removed, CBP generally has three hundred fourteen days, roughly six months plus about four additional months, to liquidate the entry, after which it is deemed liquidated by operation of law at the rate, value, and amount originally asserted by the importer at entry. Ninety days is far too short a period for this particular statutory deemed liquidation clock to run. One hundred eighty days and a full calendar year likewise do not match the specific three hundred fourteen day figure used specifically for previously suspended entries. Deemed liquidation ultimately provides finality and protects importers against indefinite, open-ended liability lingering on old entries.
  41. The correct answer is A (Ten working days after the date of entry as defined by the release): Under 19 CFR 142.12, the entry summary along with estimated duties attached must be filed within ten working days of the date of entry, which for a release obtained under immediate delivery is generally the actual date of release. Working days specifically exclude weekends and federal holidays, which meaningfully distinguishes this deadline from a simple calendar-day count elsewhere in the regulations. Fifteen and twenty working days simply do not reflect the actual regulatory standard set out here. Timely entry summary filing within this window helps the importer avoid a liquidated damages claim being filed against its underlying bond.
  42. The correct answer is B (CBP Form 3461, the Entry/Immediate Delivery document filed for release): CBP Form 3461, or its electronic ACE equivalent, is filed to request release of goods before the formal entry summary and estimated duties are submitted. It functions as the entry document under 19 CFR 142.3, allowing cargo to move while the entry summary is prepared separately. CBP Form 7501 is filed later to calculate and pay duties owed on the shipment. Using the correct release document keeps the shipment moving smoothly without delaying compliance with the later entry summary deadline.
  43. The correct answer is C (CBP Form 7501, the Entry Summary reporting classification and duty owed): CBP Form 7501 is the entry summary used to report the HTSUS classification, appraised value, and duties, taxes, and fees owed on each entry, filed under 19 CFR 142.11 after release has already occurred. Form 3461 only requests release itself, while Form 5106 is used to register an importer's identification number with CBP. The entry summary is the specific document CBP relies on to assess duties owed and, eventually, to liquidate the entry.
  44. The correct answer is B (The deadline generally moves forward to the next available business day): When a regulatory filing deadline happens to fall on a weekend or a federal holiday, it is generally treated as falling on the next available business day, consistent with standard CBP practice for computing these statutory filing periods. The deadline certainly does not simply become void or disappear, nor does it move backward to some earlier calendar date. A full thirty-day extension is not automatically granted merely because the calculated deadline happens to fall on a non-business day. Brokers should still plan to file well before the weekend whenever practical, to avoid unnecessary last-minute processing complications.
  45. The correct answer is C (The ocean carrier bears responsibility for these two additional elements): The two carrier-side elements, the vessel stow plan and the container status messages, are the ocean carrier's responsibility under 19 CFR 149.4, distinguishing them clearly from the ten importer-provided ISF-10 elements. The importer of record is responsible for its own set of ISF-10 data, not for these separate carrier-side elements described here. A customs broker may assist in filing the importer's ISF-10 data but does not independently generate the carrier's stow plan information. Recognizing this clear division of responsibility helps clarify exactly where each party's compliance obligation actually lies within the overall ISF program.
  46. The correct answer is D (The merchandise may be sent to general order, stored at the importer's own risk): Merchandise not entered within the statutory period may be sent to a general order warehouse under 19 CFR Part 4 and Part 127, held there at the risk and expense of the importer, rather than being automatically forfeited outright. Liquidation cannot even occur before an entry summary formally exists, so an automatic penalty-rate liquidation is simply not a legally available outcome here. Permanent bond revocation is a far more severe and entirely unrelated remedy reserved for very different circumstances. General order storage instead gives the importer a further opportunity to still enter the merchandise before it becomes subject to eventual disposal proceedings.
  47. The correct answer is C (CBP may issue a rate advance and bill for the additional duties owed): When CBP determines that the entered value was genuinely understated, it can issue a rate advance during liquidation review and bill the importer for the additional duties owed, generally applying the valuation principles in 19 CFR Part 152. Entries are not automatically voided outright simply because of a routine value discrepancy discovered during review. Criminal grand jury referral is reserved for cases suggesting actual fraud, not an ordinary valuation correction handled administratively. The importer typically retains appeal rights through the protest process if it disagrees with the rate advance CBP ultimately assesses.
  48. The correct answer is D (Liquidated damages may be assessed by CBP against the ISF bond): A late or otherwise inaccurate ISF filing exposes the importer to a liquidated damages claim under the ISF bond, since CBP actively enforces the twenty-four hour advance filing requirement set out in 19 CFR 149.2. Bond revocation, cargo seizure, and criminal prosecution are all far more severe remedies reserved for egregious or repeated violations, not an isolated routine late filing. CBP typically issues a standard claim for liquidated damages, which the importer may then petition for mitigation through the normal administrative process. Timely and accurate ISF filing avoids this entire exposure altogether from the outset.
  49. The correct answer is B (CBP may issue a liquidated damages claim against the importer's underlying bond): A failure to timely file the entry summary and pay estimated duties within the applicable working-day deadline under 19 CFR 142.12 can result in a liquidated damages claim being filed against the bond that covers the particular entry. Automatic re-exportation at CBP's expense and permanent duty-free treatment simply are not consequences that flow from a late entry summary filing under these regulations. Simply canceling the entry with absolutely no penalty at all ignores the genuine bond obligations the importer voluntarily accepted when the bond was executed. Prompt filing within the working-day window protects the importer from this entirely unnecessary financial exposure.
  50. The correct answer is C (Reduced penalty exposure, generally limited to interest on unpaid duties in a non-fraud case): A valid prior disclosure under 19 U.S.C. 1592(c)(4) generally limits an importer's penalty exposure, often to only interest on the unpaid duties in an ordinary negligence or non-fraud case, rather than the much higher penalties that could otherwise apply. It certainly does not eliminate the underlying duty liability itself, since the unpaid duties, taxes, and fees must still ultimately be tendered to CBP in full. Prior disclosure does not grant blanket immunity from all future CBP audits that happen to be entirely unrelated to the specific disclosed issue. There is no cash reward of any kind paid to importers for making a prior disclosure; the real benefit is mitigated penalty exposure, not any form of direct compensation.
  51. The correct answer is D (Entry type 01, the standard formal or informal consumption entry): Entry type 01 is the standard consumption entry used for ordinary imported merchandise released for use in domestic commerce with no special program claimed. Type 06 designates a foreign trade zone consumption entry, type 22 is the re-warehouse entry used to move goods between bonded warehouses, and type 23 is the temporary importation under bond entry, so none of the other three applies to an ordinary consumption shipment. Choosing the right entry type code keeps CBP processing the shipment under the correct duty and statistical reporting rules. A wrong code can cause processing delays or an incorrect duty calculation.
  52. The correct answer is B (A Transportation and Exportation entry, abbreviated as a T&E entry): A Transportation and Exportation entry, provided for under 19 CFR Part 18, allows merchandise to move in-bond through the United States toward its ultimate export destination without ever being entered for domestic consumption along the way. An Immediate Transportation entry instead moves goods to a different U.S. port specifically for entry within the United States itself, not toward an eventual foreign export destination. A consumption entry or an informal entry would improperly treat this merely transiting merchandise as though it were actually intended for the domestic U.S. market. Using the correct T&E entry preserves the duty-free in-transit status of goods that are simply passing through U.S. territory.
  53. The correct answer is A (It may be sold sooner than the standard general order period allows): Perishable merchandise placed into general order may be disposed of, typically through an accelerated sale, sooner than the standard storage period because of the very real risk of spoilage and a corresponding loss of commercial value over time.   54. The correct answer is D (Pay all applicable duties, taxes, and accrued storage charges and complete entry): To reclaim merchandise currently being held in general order, the importer generally must complete the entry process and pay all applicable duties, taxes, and any accrued storage or handling charges before the goods are finally disposed of by the warehouse operator. Automatic release after only twenty-four hours is simply not how general order storage actually works, since the entire point of the process was that entry was never timely made in the first place. Drawback instead applies to duty refunds on exported or destroyed goods, and it is not the correct mechanism for reclaiming unentered merchandise sitting in general order. A formal federal court injunction would be an extreme and wholly unnecessary remedy for what is really a routine late-entry situation.
  55. The correct answer is C (The rate, value, and amount originally asserted by the importer at entry): Deemed liquidation under 19 U.S.C. 1504 locks in the rate, value, and amount of duty originally asserted by the importer at the time of entry, since CBP's own failure to act within the statutory period effectively forfeits its ability to make some different determination later. CBP cannot retroactively substitute a different, less favorable rate once deemed liquidation has already legally occurred, which is precisely the protection the statute was designed to provide importers. A default zero percent rate is certainly not the applicable standard here, since the originally entered rate simply controls the outcome. Applying the single highest possible HTSUS rate would directly contradict the entire purpose behind the deemed liquidation finality rule.
  56. The correct answer is D (Entry type 21, the warehouse entry storing goods under bond until a later consumption entry): When an absolute quota category is already filled, the merchandise cannot lawfully be entered for domestic consumption, so it is entered into a bonded warehouse under a warehouse entry and held until the next quota period opens. Entry type 01 would improperly release the goods into domestic commerce while the quota remains closed, and the informal and foreign trade zone entries do not fit this situation. Warehousing the goods keeps the cargo under CBP control and avoids an entry that the filled quota cannot support.
  57. The correct answer is C (June 16, which is fifteen calendar days after the date of arrival): Entry generally must be made within fifteen calendar days after the date of arrival of the importing carrier, consistent with 19 U.S.C. 1484 and the implementing regulations in 19 CFR Part 141, so an arrival on June 1 sets a deadline of June 16. Thirty-day and ninety-day windows simply do not reflect the actual statutory entry-filing period that applies here. Requiring entry on the very same day as arrival ignores the statutory grace period Congress specifically gave to importers. Failure to timely enter the merchandise within this window can eventually result in the goods being sent to general order storage.
  58. The correct answer is D (The bond principal may face liability for liquidated damages for the late reporting): A carrier or other bond principal that fails to timely report arrival of an in-bond shipment at its intended destination can be held liable for liquidated damages under the applicable transportation bond, consistent with the requirements set out in 19 CFR Part 18. CBP certainly does not automatically forgive a missed in-bond reporting deadline without any consequence whatsoever attaching to the responsible party. The merchandise is not instantly deemed abandoned property simply because an in-bond movement happened to run late in this instance. Liability under an in-bond movement generally rests with the bond principal who actually executed the underlying transportation bond obligation, which is very often the carrier rather than the importer of record itself.

  62. The correct answer is C (Tendering the actual loss of revenue, meaning the unpaid duties, within the period CBP specifies): To fully perfect a prior disclosure, the disclosing party generally must also tender the actual loss of revenue, meaning the unpaid duties, taxes, and fees, within the specific period that CBP itself specifies after the disclosure has been formally accepted. Filing a protest instead addresses disputes over an already-liquidated entry, and it is simply not the correct mechanism for perfecting a voluntary prior disclosure under this statute. There is no requirement whatsoever to publish the disclosure publicly in a newspaper or through any other public channel. A prior disclosure remains an entirely administrative process conducted directly with CBP, and it does not require any separate court order for its approval.

The correct answer is C (CBP had already commenced a formal investigation of the specific violation beforehand): A prior disclosure loses its intended protective benefit entirely if CBP had already commenced a formal investigation of that specific violation before the importer ever came forward, since a valid disclosure must genuinely precede CBP's own discovery or its formal investigation to properly qualify. Submitting the disclosure in writing, clearly identifying the class or kind of merchandise involved, and disclosing before CBP's own discovery are all fully consistent with, rather than contrary to, a genuinely valid disclosure. This strict timing requirement exists specifically to reward importers who honestly self-report before any enforcement action begins, rather than only after the government has already independently identified the underlying issue. Understanding this critical timing rule is essential before ever advising a client to actually pursue a prior disclosure strategy.

Carrier revenue figures, banking routing details, and individual CBP personnel identifiers are not part of the required ISF data elements at all. Complete and accurate ISF data genuinely helps CBP conduct advance targeting for potential security risks before the vessel even sails. Missing any required element can trigger a liquidated damages claim later under the applicable ISF bond.

Filing a second protest on the identical issue for the same already-decided entry is not permitted once a protest has already been finally decided by CBP. The World Trade Organization instead addresses government-to-government trade disputes, and it has no role whatsoever in an individual importer's protest of a particular liquidation.

This window gives the broker adequate time to compile classification, valuation, and duty calculation data. Missing this working-day deadline can trigger a liquidated damages claim under the importer's continuous bond. Filing only the entry document does not, by itself, satisfy the separate entry summary filing requirement.
  83. The correct answer is B (The port of entry along with the entry type and merchandise description): Under 19 CFR 142.3, the entry/immediate delivery filing must show the port of entry, the entry type, and a description of the merchandise sufficient for CBP to determine admissibility. Retail markup percentages and hull insurance policy details are not required entry data elements at all. The importer's corporate tax filing number is likewise unrelated to CBP's entry release requirements. Accurate, complete entry-level data allows CBP officers to make a timely and informed release decision.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The ocean carrier files the two additional elements under the program): The Importer Security Filing program is called 10+2 because the importer files 10 elements and the ocean carrier files 2 more. Together those form the 12-element advance filing. A broker should coordinate with the carrier so all elements reach CBP in time. The broker should confirm that the carrier has transmitted its two elements.
  2. The correct answer is D (Six months from the actual date of importation of the merchandise): Under 19 CFR 127.14, merchandise that remains unclaimed in general order storage generally may be sold or otherwise disposed of once it has remained in continuous CBP custody for six months from the actual date of importation. Ten working days and twenty-four hours are both far too short a period and instead correspond to entirely different, unrelated deadlines elsewhere in the entry process. Five years vastly overstates the real storage window that precedes the start of any disposal proceedings under these regulations. The importer nonetheless retains the right to still enter and formally claim the merchandise before disposal actually occurs, upon paying all applicable duties, taxes, and accrued storage charges.
  3. The correct answer is A (Entry status, liquidation status, and bond sufficiency for the importer): Brokers use ACE both to file documents and to monitor entry status. They can also monitor liquidation status and bond sufficiency. That visibility helps a broker catch a problem with a client's bond before it delays a shipment. That oversight is one reason ACE matters to a broker's daily practice.
  4. The correct answer is B (Liquidation confirms the estimate or adjusts it, producing a bill or refund): Liquidation is when CBP reviews the entry to whatever extent it chooses. It either confirms the estimate as final or adjusts it. The adjustment produces either a bill for additional duty or a refund to the importer. A broker should compare the liquidation result with the amount originally deposited.
  5. The correct answer is A (ISF, entry, entry summary, liquidation, then protest if needed): The Importer Security Filing comes first, before the cargo is laden abroad. Entry follows arrival, then the entry summary and estimated duties, then liquidation. If the importer disagrees with the liquidation, a protest follows within the allowed period. Each of those events carries its own deadline that a broker must track.
  6. The correct answer is B (19 USC 1484 places the duty to make entry on the importer of record): The statutory authority for CBP to require a complete entry before releasing merchandise comes from 19 USC 1484. That statute places the legal duty to make entry, using reasonable care, on the importer of record. A broker acting for the importer is expected to meet the same reasonable care standard. The chapter treats that reasonable care duty as a foundation of the entry process.
  7. The correct answer is A (A broker often finds them during a routine post-entry audit or review): Brokers frequently discover these errors during a routine post-entry audit or classification review. Examples include an incorrect tariff classification or an undeclared assist or royalty includible in dutiable value. Advising the client promptly so a prior disclosure can be filed before CBP begins asking questions is valuable work. That review is often the moment when a broker earns its keep for a client.
  8. The correct answer is C (The entry number, the port of entry, and the estimated value): CBP Form 3461 carries the importer of record, the entry number, the port of entry, a description of the merchandise, and the estimated value. It also carries the bill of lading or airway bill number tying the entry to the cargo. Filing the 3461, or its ACE equivalent, is what allows the goods to leave the port. The 3461 is often filed in ACE as the electronic equivalent of the paper form.
  9. The correct answer is D (It gives importers certainty that a transaction will not stay open indefinitely): The deemed liquidation rule functions almost as an automatic finalization if CBP does nothing. It gives importers legal certainty that a transaction will not remain open forever. Generally one year after entry, with no affirmative action and no valid extension, the entry is deemed liquidated at the rate the importer asserted under 19 USC 1504. The rule also limits how long an importer must wait for a final duty figure.
  10. The correct answer is A (It must identify the class or kind of merchandise involved): A prior disclosure must identify the class or kind of merchandise involved. It must also identify the specific entries affected or a time period and manner allowing them to be identified. Those content requirements let CBP evaluate the scope of the violation. Those details allow CBP to identify the entries and respond appropriately.
  11. The correct answer is D (The moment the conveyance physically reaches the United States and reports): Arrival happens when the vessel, aircraft, truck, or train physically reaches the United States and reports to CBP. Entry is a separate legal act, the filing of documentation that declares the merchandise and the duty rate that applies. Merchandise is not legally released until CBP authorizes that release. That arrival event is what starts the clock on the entry deadline.
  12. The correct answer is C (The manufacturer or supplier that produced the imported merchandise): The importer-side elements generally include the manufacturer or supplier, the seller, the buyer, and the ship-to party. They also include the container stuffing location, the consolidator, the importer of record number, the consignee number, the country of origin, and the six-digit tariff number. The carrier files the stow plan and container status messages as its two elements. Those ten elements must reach CBP before the vessel is loaded overseas.
  13. The correct answer is D (By noon on Friday, June 19, which is 24 hours before the scheduled loading): The Importer Security Filing is due no later than 24 hours before the cargo is laden aboard the vessel at the foreign port. Counting back one full day from a Saturday noon loading gives Friday noon. The deadline is tied to the overseas loading event, not to the vessel's arrival in the United States. A broker should calendar that deadline as soon as the loading schedule is known.
  14. The correct answer is B (A time period and manner allowing identification of the entries suffices): To be effective, a prior disclosure must identify the specific entries affected or a time period and manner allowing them to be identified. That alternative recognizes that importers may not have every entry number at hand. The disclosure still must be in writing and include the other required elements. A broker should keep the records that let CBP match the disclosure to the entries.
  15. The correct answer is C (An undeclared assist or royalty that is includible in dutiable value): The chapter gives an undeclared assist or royalty includible in dutiable value as an example of an error a broker may find during a post-entry audit. Such a discovery can lead to a prior disclosure that limits penalty exposure. Incorrect tariff classification is another common example. Such errors can be corrected through a prior disclosure before CBP finds them.
  16. The correct answer is A (Within 180 days after the date of liquidation): A protest under 19 USC 1514 is generally due within 180 days after the date of liquidation. The clock runs from the liquidation date, not from entry or from the entry summary filing. A broker evaluating timeliness should first pin down the actual liquidation date. Filing after that window generally forfeits the administrative protest remedy.
  17. The correct answer is C (The tender means the unpaid duties plus interest owed on those entries): The tender required with a prior disclosure is the actual loss of revenue, meaning unpaid duties plus interest. It must accompany the disclosure or promptly follow it. That payment requirement separates a valid prior disclosure from a mere admission of error. A broker should confirm the interest calculation before tendering the payment.
  18. The correct answer is D (Negligent, grossly negligent, and fraudulent violations with varying exposure): Under 19 USC 1592, importers can face civil penalties for negligent, grossly negligent, or fraudulent violations. The exposure can reach multiples of the duty loss or even the full domestic value in fraud cases. A prior disclosure made before a formal investigation can substantially reduce that exposure. The category of violation drives both the penalty exposure and the mitigation options.
  19. The correct answer is C (With CBP at the port where the entry was liquidated by the agency): The protest is filed with CBP at the port where liquidation occurred. It must set out the specific reasons the liquidation was incorrect and the relief sought. Only after CBP acts on the protest does judicial review before the Court of International Trade become available. A broker should file the protest at the correct port to preserve the remedy.
  20. The correct answer is D (A tender, or prompt follow-up tender, of the actual loss of revenue): A prior disclosure must disclose the circumstances of the violation. It must be accompanied, or promptly followed, by a tender of the actual loss of revenue. That tender means the unpaid duties plus interest, not merely a promise to pay later. The tender is what makes the disclosure complete under the statute.
  21. The correct answer is C (The liquidation is final and conclusive on all parties unless a protest is timely): Once liquidation occurs, it becomes final and conclusive on all parties absent a timely protest under 19 USC 1514. The liquidation can still be corrected through reliquidation in specified circumstances. The importer's window to challenge the result runs from the date of liquidation. A broker should confirm the liquidation date before advising on any challenge.
  22. The correct answer is B (Reliquidation, which can fix a clerical error or mistake of fact): Reliquidation happens when CBP itself corrects a liquidation. One example is fixing a clerical error or mistake of fact within statutory time limits. Reliquidation can also occur as the outcome of a successfully protested liquidation. A broker should distinguish reliquidation from a new entry or a protest filing.
  23. The correct answer is A (Immediate exportation allows export directly from the port of arrival): Immediate exportation allows goods to be exported directly from the port where they arrived. The cargo does not transit another domestic port before leaving the country. That differs from transportation and exportation, which covers passage through the United States to another country under bond. The cargo never enters the commerce of the United States in that arrangement.
  24. The correct answer is C (The entry is deemed liquidated one year after the date of entry): When CBP takes no affirmative action and no valid extension applies, the entry is deemed liquidated by operation of law. That occurs generally one year after the date of entry under 19 USC 1504. The deemed liquidation rule gives importers certainty that a transaction will not stay open indefinitely. That figure is one of the numbers the exam expects a licensed broker to know.
  25. The correct answer is B (Most entries are liquidated within one year of the date of entry): Under 19 USC 1500 and related provisions, most entries are liquidated within one year of the date of entry. That period can be extended for specified reasons, such as an open antidumping proceeding or a pending protest. If CBP takes no affirmative action and no valid extension applies, deemed liquidation can occur. A valid extension must rest on one of the specified grounds in the chapter.
  26. The correct answer is A (The immediate transportation entry, which moves goods under bond to another port): The immediate transportation entry moves merchandise under bond from the port of arrival to another CBP-designated location. Formal entry is filed later at the destination port. This is a common arrangement when cargo arrives at a coastal seaport but the importer's business sits inland. The bond covers the goods while they move toward the destination.
  27. The correct answer is C (The balance is available to the true owner if that ownership is later established): Auction proceeds first cover unpaid duties, taxes, storage, and other charges. Any remaining balance can be made available to the true owner if that ownership is later established. That rule does not erase the harm of losing the merchandise to auction in the first place. That is why timely entry protects the owner's financial interest in the goods.
  28. The correct answer is D (The entry is reliquidated at a corrected amount reflecting the protest grant): CBP either grants the protest, in whole or in part, or denies it. A grant results in reliquidation at a corrected amount. A denial leaves the importer the option of seeking judicial review before the Court of International Trade. The corrected amount then becomes the liquidated duty for that entry.
  29. The correct answer is C (The port of arrival and the port where entry is filed are not always the same): Cargo often arrives at a coastal seaport while the importer's business or a bonded warehouse sits somewhere else. The cargo can then move under bond to another CBP-designated location where formal entry will be filed. That is why the port of arrival and the port of entry are not always the same place. That is why a broker should identify each port involved in the movement.
  30. The correct answer is B (Duty is not owed yet, and the deadline clock for liquidation has not started): In-bond merchandise is physically present but is not yet entered for consumption. Because no consumption entry has been filed, duty is not owed at that stage. The clock on other deadlines, including liquidation, has not started while the goods move under bond. The entry that eventually starts the liquidation clock is filed later at the destination.
  31. The correct answer is D (Perishable or hazardous merchandise may be disposed of on a shorter timeline): General order merchandise that remains unclaimed can eventually be sold at public auction. Perishable, hazardous, or otherwise unsuitable merchandise may be disposed of on a shorter timeline than the standard process. That difference reflects the practical reality that such goods cannot wait as long. A broker should treat a perishable shipment as an urgent file from the moment it arrives.
  32. The correct answer is B (ACE replaced the older Automated Commercial System and is mandated for filings): ACE replaced the older Automated Commercial System. It is now mandated for entry summary filing across virtually all entry types. Brokers use ACE both to file documents and to check entry and liquidation status. Filing outside ACE for a covered entry type is not an option today.
  33. The correct answer is D (In a bonded general order warehouse designated for such merchandise): General order merchandise is taken into the custody of a bonded general order warehouse. The chapter describes it as CBP's storage lot for goods nobody has properly claimed. Storage continues there while the importer remains out of compliance. The goods stay in that custody until the importer resolves the entry problem.
  34. The correct answer is B (Filing both together satisfies the entry and entry summary deadlines at once): When entry and entry summary are filed together as a live entry, both deadlines are effectively satisfied at once. The two filings still remain distinct legal acts, and the exam often separates them in a fact pattern. A broker should be ready to calculate either deadline on its own. One transmission can satisfy both deadlines without eliminating either legal duty.
  35. The correct answer is D (The in-bond carrier has generally 30 days to deliver the goods to the destination): In-bond carriers have their own time limit to deliver in-bond merchandise to its final destination. That limit is generally 30 days. A broker coordinating an in-bond movement should track the delivery deadline along with the entry deadline that follows. Missing that delivery window can expose the carrier to liability on its bond.
  36. The correct answer is C (Friday, March 18, which is the fifteenth calendar day after arrival): The entry filing deadline runs 15 calendar days from the date the merchandise arrives within the port limits. Counting every day, including weekends, the fifteenth day after a March 3 arrival is March 18. A broker should write out each date rather than estimate the deadline. A single off-by-one error can turn a timely filing into a missed deadline.
  37. The correct answer is B (Entry must be filed within 15 calendar days after the merchandise arrives): Entry, filed on CBP Form 3461 or its ACE equivalent, must generally occur within 15 calendar days of the date merchandise arrives within the port limits. The rule comes from 19 CFR Part 141. If entry is not made within that window, the merchandise is subject to being sent to general order. Counting the fifteen days correctly is one of the most heavily tested skills in this domain.
  38. The correct answer is D (The Automated Commercial Environment, known as ACE, CBP's single window): ACE is the Automated Commercial Environment, CBP's single window system. Importers, brokers, and other agencies use it to process import and export data electronically. Nearly all entry and entry summary filings today go through ACE. That single window reduces the need for separate filings with each agency.
  39. The correct answer is B (A do not load message instructing the carrier to hold the container): A late or inaccurate Importer Security Filing can trigger a do not load message from CBP. That message instructs the carrier not to load the container until the issue is resolved. The shipment waits at the foreign port until the filing problem is corrected. Resolving the filing problem is what allows the container to be loaded afterward.
  40. The correct answer is D (The importer can face liquidated damages under the bond posted for the entry): Missing the entry summary deadline in real practice can cause a client to miss a legal deadline. The consequence described in the chapter is exposure to liquidated damages under the importer's bond. That is why the ten-working-day rule is treated as a hard deadline. A broker should track the deadline from the date of release to protect the importer.
  41. The correct answer is C (The importer or its agent files the ten importer-side elements with CBP): Under the Importer Security Filing program, the importer or its agent files the 10 elements. The ocean carrier files the 2 remaining elements, which is why the program is called 10+2. A broker acting as the importer's agent commonly handles this filing. That division of labor is why the program carries the name 10+2.
  42. The correct answer is B (The container stuffing location where the cargo was loaded into the box): The ten importer-side elements include the manufacturer or supplier, the seller, the buyer, and the ship-to party. They also include the container stuffing location and the consolidator. The stow plan and container status messages are filed by the carrier rather than the importer. The carrier's own elements cover the loading and movement of the container.
  43. The correct answer is A (A bond guarantees the goods leave or are destroyed, so liquidated damages may apply): A bond posted for a temporary importation under bond guarantees that the goods will actually leave the country or be destroyed. If they are not, liquidated damages can be assessed against the bond. The importer's failure to follow the declared purpose turns a duty-free entry into a costly one. The importer therefore faces both the lost duty benefit and the bond exposure.
  44. The correct answer is B (The goods may not be sold or used inconsistently with the purpose declared): Temporary importation under bond is conditioned on the goods leaving or being destroyed, and the entry declares the purpose for which they are admitted. Selling the goods domestically or using them for a different purpose conflicts with that declaration. A broker should warn the importer that such a change can trigger liability on the bond. A broker should flag that restriction to the importer as soon as the plan changes.
  45. The correct answer is C (The goods must be exported or destroyed within the allowed period): Temporary importation under bond allows duty-free entry for a limited period, generally up to one year with possible extensions. The condition is that the goods are exported or destroyed within that period. A broker must track the expiration date so the importer can act before the bond is breached. The importer should arrange the export or destruction well before the period expires.
  46. The correct answer is A (Informal entry has historically applied to shipments valued at $2,500 or less): Entry type 11 is the informal entry used for lower-value shipments and certain other categories where formal procedures are not required. Historically the threshold has been $2,500 or less for most commercial merchandise, with narrower limits for a few import-sensitive categories. A broker should confirm the current threshold, since CBP can adjust it over time. The exact limit depends on the category of merchandise being entered.
  47. The correct answer is C (Under CBP supervision, goods may be repacked, manipulated, or even manufactured): Depending on the class of warehouse involved, merchandise stored under bond may be manipulated, repacked, or even manufactured while under CBP supervision. That flexibility is one reason importers choose warehouse entry. The activity still occurs under CBP oversight, and duty is assessed when the goods are withdrawn. The importer should confirm which operations the particular warehouse class allows.
  48. The correct answer is A (A live entry, where entry and entry summary are filed together electronically): Many transactions, especially those filed through ACE, transmit the entry and the entry summary together, and that combined filing is called a live entry. Even so, the two filings remain distinct legal acts with distinct deadlines. A broker must still know both deadlines because exam fact patterns often separate them. The single transmission does not merge the two legal filings into one obligation.
  49. The correct answer is C (The tariff rate quota is reported on the applicable consumption entry, not a separate code): There is no separate tariff-rate-quota entry type. The in-quota and over-quota rates for the same provision come from the Harmonized Tariff Schedule, and the quota status determines which rate applies rather than a distinct entry type code. Entry type 03 covers merchandise under an antidumping or countervailing duty order rather than a two-tier duty rate. A broker should watch for this arrangement when the same good can enter at two different rates.
  50. The correct answer is B (Entry type 02, which covers consumption entries subject to quota or visa limits): Entry type 02 is the consumption entry for merchandise subject to a quantitative restriction. That category includes an absolute quota, a hard numeric cap after which no more of the good may enter during the period, and merchandise requiring a visa or export certificate from the exporting country's government. Entry type 03 instead covers merchandise subject to an antidumping or countervailing duty order, and entry type 06 is the foreign trade zone consumption entry. A broker should confirm the visa or export certificate before the entry is submitted.
  51. The correct answer is A (The importer of record must make entry using reasonable care under 19 USC 1484): The statute places the legal duty to make entry on the importer of record, and it applies a reasonable care standard to that duty. A broker acting for an importer is expected to meet that same standard when gathering information and preparing filings. This standard is central to import compliance and runs through the entire entry process. The importer of record remains accountable for the accuracy of what is filed, even when a broker prepares the documents.
  52. The correct answer is D (Duty is assessed only when the goods are formally withdrawn for consumption): Warehouse entry lets the importer defer duty while the merchandise is stored under bond. Duty is assessed only if and when the goods are formally withdrawn for consumption. That deferral is available for the period the goods may remain in the bonded warehouse. The importer should plan the withdrawal date so the duty is paid when the goods are actually needed.
  53. The correct answer is C (Informal entry appears largely in 19 CFR Part 143): The chapter identifies 19 CFR Part 141 as the home of the general entry regulations, and informal entry is one of the special entry categories covered in 19 CFR Part 143. Mail importations are governed separately by 19 CFR Part 145, and baggage by 19 CFR Part 148, so Part 143 is not the home of mail or baggage. A broker should cite the part that actually governs the entry type being filed, and a broker filing an unusual entry should check Part 143 before relying on the general rules.
  54. The correct answer is D (Informal entry thresholds are narrower for import-sensitive categories such as textiles): Informal entry under entry type 11 is available for lower-value shipments, but the dollar limit is not uniform across all goods. For a handful of import-sensitive categories, such as certain textiles, the threshold is narrower than the general commercial limit. A broker should therefore check the category before assuming informal entry will be accepted. For those goods, a shipment that exceeds the narrower limit must be entered formally.
  55. The correct answer is A (Penalty exposure is generally limited to interest owed on the unpaid duties): A valid prior disclosure under 19 USC 1592(c)(4) generally caps monetary penalty exposure at interest on the unpaid duties when no investigation had begun. That is the typical result for the Harborline Freight Corporation undervaluation at Charleston. Option B is wrong because making the Charleston disclosure does not increase the penalty as an admission, and it limits the exposure to interest.
  56. The correct answer is B (The required entry documentation and any required bond must be filed with CBP): Merchandise is not legally released for use in the United States until CBP authorizes its release. CBP will not authorize that release until the required entry documentation and any required bond have been filed. Filing the documentation is what converts a physical arrival into a lawful entry. Only after those filings are accepted may the cargo lawfully leave CBP custody.
  57. The correct answer is D (The tariff classification and the country of origin of the merchandise): CBP Form 7501 declares the tariff classification under the Harmonized Tariff Schedule and the country of origin. It also reports the value for duty purposes, the applicable rate, any preferential program claimed, and the estimated duties owed. These elements give CBP the basis for assessing and later liquidating the entry. That data is what CBP uses to compute the estimated duty at the time of entry.
  58. The correct answer is B (The disclosure must be made before CBP has begun a formal investigation): A valid prior disclosure under 19 USC 1592(c)(4) must be made before CBP has begun a formal investigation of the violation. The disclosure by Harborline Freight Corporation about the Laredo classification error was submitted before any investigation began. Option C is incorrect because waiting until CBP issues a formal penalty notice for the Laredo entries would defeat the prior disclosure.
  59. The correct answer is D (Gather accurate information, ask the importer questions, and check the paperwork): A broker acting for an importer is expected to exercise reasonable care rather than serve as a passive conduit. That means checking the information the importer and the shipper provide instead of accepting it at face value. A broker who simply files whatever documents arrive creates penalty exposure for the importer and professional risk for itself. Checking the paperwork before filing is the practical step that keeps the reasonable care duty satisfied.
  60. The correct answer is B (Penalty exposure is generally limited to interest owed on the unpaid duties): When a prior disclosure is valid, 19 USC 1592(c)(4) generally limits the monetary penalty to interest on the unpaid duties. That is the typical consequence for the Bluewater Trading Company undervaluation at New Orleans. Option C is wrong because the New Orleans disclosure does not wipe out the duty obligation, and the unpaid duties plus interest still must be paid.
  61. The correct answer is A (Within 180 days after the date the entry was liquidated by CBP): Under 19 USC 1514, a protest must be filed within 180 days after the date of liquidation. Counting from the August 22 liquidation of the Bluewater Trading Company entry at Seattle, the protest window runs 180 days. Option C is wrong because the one year period measures liquidation, not the protest deadline for the Seattle liquidation.
  62. The correct answer is C (Within 180 days after the date the entry was liquidated by CBP): A protest under 19 USC 1514 must be filed within 180 days after liquidation. The March 3 liquidation of the Bluewater Trading Company entry at Savannah therefore allows 180 days for a protest. Option D is incorrect because the one year period governs liquidation, not the Savannah protest deadline.
  63. The correct answer is D (The disclosure must be made before CBP has begun a formal investigation): To qualify under 19 USC 1592(c)(4), a prior disclosure must be made before CBP has begun a formal investigation of the violation. The disclosure by Vantage Point Traders about the Chicago classification error meets that condition because no investigation had started. Option A is incorrect because a disclosure made only after CBP issues a penalty notice for the Chicago entries is not a prior disclosure.
  64. The correct answer is C (Within 180 days after the date the entry was liquidated by CBP): 19 USC 1514 requires a protest to be filed within 180 days after the date of liquidation. The February 17 liquidation of the Bluewater Trading Company entry at Detroit therefore opens a 180 day protest period. Option A is incorrect because the protest window is 180 days, not 90 days, for the Detroit liquidation.
  65. The correct answer is A (It postpones the date on which deemed liquidation would otherwise occur): 19 USC 1504 permits CBP to extend the liquidation period, which moves the date on which deemed liquidation would otherwise occur. That is the practical effect of the extension CBP issued for the Harborline Freight Corporation entry at Newark. Option B is wrong because the extension does not waive CBP's authority to liquidate the Newark entry, and it delays the deemed liquidation date.
  66. The correct answer is B (The liquidation becomes final unless it is timely and properly protested): Liquidation becomes final and conclusive under 19 USC 1504 unless it is timely and properly protested. The Bluewater Trading Company entry at Norfolk therefore becomes final once no valid protest is filed. Option C is incorrect because a prior disclosure addresses violations and does not make the Norfolk liquidation final.
  67. The correct answer is C (It postpones the date on which deemed liquidation would otherwise occur): Under 19 USC 1504, a valid extension postpones the deemed liquidation date that would otherwise apply. That is what the extension did for the Harborline Freight Corporation entry at Chicago. Option B is wrong because extending liquidation does not shorten the protest period for the Chicago entry.
  68. The correct answer is D (The rate and amount of duty that CBP assessed upon liquidation): 19 USC 1514 lists the CBP decisions that may be protested, and the rate and amount of duty assessed on liquidation is among them. The liquidated duty rate on the Ferro Metals USA entry at New Orleans is therefore a proper subject of a protest. Option A is incorrect because a pending Importer Security Filing evaluation is not one of the protestable decisions listed in 19 USC 1514 for the New Orleans entry.
  69. The correct answer is C (One year after the reconciliation entry summary is filed, absent suspension): Under 19 USC 1504, a reconciliation entry summary is liquidated within one year after the reconciliation summary is filed, absent suspension or a valid extension. That one year period is what governs the Vantage Point Traders reconciliation entry at Cleveland. Option A is wrong because the 180 day figure is the protest window and does not measure the liquidation period for the Cleveland reconciliation entry.
  70. The correct answer is A (It is sent to a general order warehouse, held at the importer's risk and expense): Merchandise that is unentered and unclaimed is held in general order storage at the importer's risk and expense under 19 CFR Part 127. The Bluewater Trading Company shipment at Miami falls into that general order treatment. Option C is incorrect because CBP is not required to re-export unclaimed general order goods from Miami at its own expense.
  71. The correct answer is C (No entry was timely made and the goods then went unclaimed within the allowed period): General order status under 19 USC 1490 and 19 CFR Part 127 results from a failure to make timely entry followed by the goods remaining unclaimed. Those facts produced the general order cargo status of the Harborline Freight Corporation shipment at Laredo. Option B is wrong because an importer cannot simply elect general order treatment, and the Laredo goods went unentered and unclaimed.
  72. The correct answer is A (It is sent to a general order warehouse, held at the importer's risk and expense): Under 19 USC 1490 and 19 CFR Part 127, unentered and unclaimed merchandise is taken into general order and stored at the importer's risk and expense. The Harborline Freight Corporation shipment at Houston meets that description, so it goes to a general order warehouse. Option D is wrong because the carrier does not take the unclaimed Houston goods as its own free property.
  73. The correct answer is B (No entry was timely made and the goods then went unclaimed within the allowed period): Goods become general order cargo under 19 USC 1490 and 19 CFR Part 127 when entry is not made on time and the merchandise remains unclaimed. The Ferro Metals USA shipment at Seattle reached general order status for that reason. Option A is incorrect because a classification error on an earlier summary does not create general order status for the Seattle cargo.
  74. The correct answer is D (No entry was timely made and the goods then went unclaimed within the allowed period): Cargo becomes general order merchandise under 19 USC 1490 and 19 CFR Part 127 when no entry is timely made and the goods go unclaimed. That is precisely what happened to the Vantage Point Traders shipment sitting at Chicago after arrival. Option A is wrong because an expired power of attorney is not what creates general order status, and the failure to enter and claim the Chicago goods is the cause.
  75.
