The correct answer is B (Sell the merchandise at public auction to satisfy unpaid duties and storage charges): After the general order storage period, 19 CFR Part 127 permits CBP to sell unclaimed merchandise to cover duties and storage costs. That is the disposition available for the unclaimed Harborline Freight Corporation goods at Charleston. Option D is wrong because the ocean carrier does not receive permanent legal title to unclaimed general order goods at Charleston.
  76. The correct answer is A (Immediate Transportation, commonly abbreviated in the trade as an IT movement): 19 CFR Part 18 governs in-bond movements, and immediate transportation allows cargo to move from the port of arrival to another port for entry there. The Bluewater Trading Company merchandise at Seattle is moving under bond to an inland port where it will be entered, so an IT movement applies. Option B is incorrect because transportation and exportation covers goods that leave the country, not the Seattle cargo that will be entered for consumption inland.
  77. The correct answer is D (The merchandise remains formally unentered and under CBP bond throughout the movement): Merchandise moving in bond under 19 CFR Part 18 remains unentered and stays under CBP bond until it reaches the destination port. That is the status of the Vantage Point Traders goods traveling in bond from Laredo. Option C is wrong because acceptance of custody by the inland carrier does not make the Laredo in-bond merchandise formally entered.
  78. The correct answer is C (Warehouse Withdrawal for Transportation, which is commonly abbreviated as a WDT movement): Warehouse withdrawal for transportation allows bonded merchandise to leave a warehouse under 19 CFR Part 18 and move to another port without duty payment. The Ferro Metals USA withdrawal at Los Angeles/Long Beach is exactly that movement, so the WDT category applies. Option A is wrong because entry type 23 for temporary importation under bond is a different program from the Los Angeles/Long Beach warehouse withdrawal.
  79. The correct answer is D (Serving as the primary electronic system through which importers transmit trade data): ACE is the primary electronic system through which importers and brokers transmit trade data to CBP under 19 CFR Part 143. That function is exactly what option B describes, and it is what the Harborline Freight Corporation broker uses for the Newark summary. Option C is wrong because ACE does not issue customs broker licenses to filers who open an account.
  80. The correct answer is A (The Automated Commercial Environment, commonly referred to in the trade as ACE): The Automated Commercial Environment, known as ACE, is the electronic filing system required for entry and entry summary transmission under 19 CFR Part 143. It is the system the Ferro Metals USA broker uses for the Savannah shipment. Option B is incorrect because the Automated Broker Interface is an older messaging method and not the current mandatory platform for the Savannah entry.
  81. The correct answer is D (CBP may assess liquidated damages against the entry bond for the late filing): Filing the entry summary past the 10 working day period in 19 CFR 142.12 can result in liquidated damages against the entry bond. That risk arises for the Harborline Freight Corporation entry at Laredo, where the summary was late. Option B is wrong because a late Laredo summary does not cause the entry to be deemed liquidated in the importer's favor.
  82. The correct answer is B (Transportation and Exportation, which is commonly abbreviated as a T&E movement): Under 19 CFR Part 18, a transportation and exportation movement carries in-bond cargo through the United States for export from another port. That describes the Baltimore cargo bound for a third country, which is why the T&E category governs the movement. Option D is wrong because immediate transportation ends in an entry at an inland port rather than the export that the Baltimore cargo is destined for.
  83. The correct answer is D (Within 10 working days after the time of entry of the merchandise): Under 19 CFR 142.12, the entry summary and estimated duties must be filed within 10 working days after the time of entry. The August 22 release of the Bluewater Trading Company merchandise at New Orleans started that 10 working day count. Option A is incorrect because the one year period measures liquidation rather than the summary deadline for the New Orleans release.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The Automated Commercial Environment, commonly referred to in the trade as ACE): 19 CFR Part 143 governs electronic entry filing, and the system CBP maintains for transmitting entry and entry summary data is the Automated Commercial Environment. ACE is the mandatory electronic platform for most trade filers, so it is the system used for the Vantage Point Traders filing at Cleveland. Option D is wrong because the Automated Broker Interface is a legacy message format rather than the current ACE platform used for Cleveland entries.
  2. The correct answer is D (CBP may assess liquidated damages against the entry bond for the late filing): When the entry summary is filed after the 10 working day period in 19 CFR 142.12, CBP may assess liquidated damages against the entry bond. That is the direct exposure created by the late summary for the Vantage Point Traders release at Chicago. Option A is incorrect because deemed liquidation in the importer's favor is not the remedy for a late summary and does not apply to the Chicago filing.
  3. The correct answer is C (Within 10 working days counted from the date the merchandise was entered): The entry summary and estimated duties are due within 10 working days after entry under 19 CFR 142.12. That is the deadline facing the Vantage Point Traders release at Baltimore. Option B fails because the Baltimore deadline runs in working days from entry, not in calendar days from the bill of lading.
  4. The correct answer is D (From the date the merchandise arrives within the limits of the port): Under 19 CFR 142.3, the 15 calendar day entry period runs from the date the merchandise arrives within the limits of the port. The Harborline Freight Corporation goods unloaded at Houston therefore start their entry clock on the arrival date. Option C is incorrect because the liquidation of an unrelated prior entry has no effect on the entry deadline for the Houston merchandise.
  5. The correct answer is B (Electronically, through the Automated Commercial Environment system that CBP maintains): 19 CFR Part 143 requires most entry and entry summary data to reach CBP electronically through ACE. The Vantage Point Traders broker therefore transmits the Houston entry data through the Automated Commercial Environment. Option C is wrong because filing in person at the port counter does not satisfy the electronic filing requirement for the Houston entries.
  6. The correct answer is C (Within 15 calendar days after the date the merchandise arrives, or October 20): Entry must be made within 15 calendar days after arrival under 19 USC 1484 and 19 CFR 142.3. Fifteen calendar days from the October 5 arrival of the Vantage Point Traders container at Newark yields October 20. Option B is incorrect because the Newark entry deadline is measured in calendar days, and the 10 working day rule applies to the later summary.
  7. The correct answer is A (No later than 24 hours before the cargo is laden aboard the vessel): The ISF deadline in 19 CFR Part 149 is 24 hours before the cargo is laden aboard the vessel at the foreign port. The Bluewater Trading Company ocean shipment at Norfolk has not yet been loaded, so the filing must be made within that pre-lading window. Option B is wrong because a filing 24 hours after the vessel leaves the foreign port would be late for the Norfolk shipment.
  8. The correct answer is B (Within 10 working days counted from the date the merchandise was entered): 19 CFR 142.12 requires the entry summary and the estimated duties, taxes, and fees within 10 working days counted from the date of entry. The Detroit release on behalf of Ferro Metals USA is therefore followed by a 10 working day summary deadline. Option C is incorrect because the Detroit count uses working days from entry rather than calendar days from the bill of lading.
  9. The correct answer is D (Cargo that is arriving in the United States by vessel from a foreign port): The Importer Security Filing requirement in 19 CFR Part 149 reaches cargo that arrives by vessel from a foreign port. The Charleston shipment moves by vessel, so it is subject to the ISF filing obligation. Option A is wrong because cargo arriving only by international air freight is not the vessel cargo covered by the Charleston ISF rule.
  10. The correct answer is B (CBP may assess liquidated damages against the entry bond for the late filing): A summary filed outside the 10 working day period of 19 CFR 142.12 can trigger liquidated damages against the bond. The late summary for the Ferro Metals USA release at Seattle exposes the entry to that claim. Option C is wrong because a late summary does not produce deemed liquidation in the importer's favor for the Seattle entry.
  11. The correct answer is D (Cargo that is arriving in the United States by vessel from a foreign port): 19 CFR Part 149 applies the Importer Security Filing requirement to cargo arriving in the United States by vessel from a foreign port. The Houston shipment is moving by vessel, so it falls within the ISF requirement rather than an exemption. Option B fails because cargo arriving only by truck across a land border is outside the vessel ISF rule and is not how the Houston shipment arrives.
  12. The correct answer is A (Entry type 03, the consumption entry code for antidumping and countervailing duty cases): Entry type 03 is the consumption code CBP uses for merchandise subject to an antidumping or countervailing duty order. The Vantage Point Traders steel pipe at Baltimore is covered by an outstanding antidumping duty order, so entry type 03 applies. Option C fails because entry type 01 is the general consumption entry and does not designate the antidumping exposure of the Baltimore shipment.
  13. The correct answer is B (Within 10 working days after the time of entry of the merchandise): 19 CFR 142.12 gives the filer 10 working days after entry to submit the entry summary with estimated duties, taxes, and fees. That 10 working day clock began with the April 4 release of the Harborline Freight Corporation goods at Charleston. Option A is incorrect because the 15 calendar day period applies to making entry, while the Charleston summary follows the 10 working day rule.
  14. The correct answer is D (Entry type 11, the code reserved for informal entry processing): Entry type 11 is reserved for informal entries, which use a simplified declaration for qualifying low-value shipments. The small commercial Harborline Freight Corporation shipment at Laredo meets that description, so entry type 11 is the correct code. Option B is incorrect because entry type 01 is the formal consumption entry, and the Laredo shipment is an informal entry filed under type 11.
  15. The correct answer is B (The merchandise may be sent to general order storage as unclaimed cargo): Merchandise that remains unentered past the 15 calendar day period in 19 USC 1484 and 19 CFR 142.3 may be placed in general order storage as unclaimed cargo. That is the outcome the Harborline Freight Corporation shipment at Cleveland faces after sitting unentered. Option A is wrong because deemed liquidation under 19 USC 1504 concerns an entry summary that was actually filed, and no summary exists for the Cleveland cargo.
  16. The correct answer is D (Entry type 21, the code reserved for warehouse entries): Under the entry type codes applied with 19 CFR 142.3, entry type 21 is the code for merchandise entered into a bonded warehouse. Because Ferro Metals USA is placing the dutiable Seattle goods into bonded storage instead of consumption, entry type 21 is the proper code. Option C is wrong because entry type 23 is a temporary importation under bond, which is a different program from the warehouse entry made at Seattle.
  17. The correct answer is C (Entry type 23, the temporary importation under bond entry code): Entry type 23 is the temporary importation under bond code used when goods are entered under bond and are expected to be exported later. The Harborline Freight Corporation trade show equipment at Charleston will be re-exported without duty, so entry type 23 is the correct filing. Option A is incorrect because entry type 22 covers re-warehouse entries and has nothing to do with the temporary importation under bond of the Charleston equipment.
  18. The correct answer is D (Entry type 11, the code reserved for informal entry processing): 19 CFR 142.3 lists entry type 11 as the code for informal entries processed under the simplified declaration procedure. The low-value Bluewater Trading Company shipment at Miami qualifies for that streamlined handling, so entry type 11 is the code that fits. Option C is wrong because entry type 01 covers formal consumption entries, and an informal Miami entry is filed under type 11 instead.
  19. The correct answer is A (The entry summary that was reported to CBP on Form 7501): Under 19 USC 1484, the entry summary is the formal filing that CBP uses to assess the duties owed and to liquidate the entry. The entry summary reported on Form 7501 is therefore the primary basis for the assessment and liquidation of the Vantage Point Traders entry at Laredo. Option D fails because the immediate delivery request only obtained early release of the Laredo goods and carries none of the classification or value detail CBP needs to liquidate.
  20. The correct answer is C (The tariff classification, entered value, and the estimated rate of duty owed): Under 19 USC 1484, Form 7501 must report the tariff classification, the entered value, and the estimated rate of duty on the merchandise. Those elements are what the Bluewater Trading Company entry at Savannah must state so that duty is assessed correctly and the entry can liquidate. Option B is insufficient because the bill of lading number and arrival date do not establish the classification, value, or duty rate for the Savannah entry.
  21. The correct answer is D (Entry type 03, the consumption entry code for antidumping and countervailing duty cases): The two-digit entry type codes applied with 19 CFR 142.3 identify the nature of the transaction, and entry type 03 is the consumption entry used when antidumping or countervailing duty orders apply. Because the Ferro Metals USA steel pipe at Norfolk falls under an outstanding antidumping duty order, entry type 03 is the code that matches the shipment. Option A is wrong because entry type 01 is the ordinary consumption entry, and it does not flag the antidumping order that governs the Norfolk merchandise.
  22. The correct answer is A (A continuous or single transaction bond sufficient to cover duties and fees owed): A filing under 19 CFR Part 142 for immediate delivery must rest on a continuous or single transaction bond large enough to cover the duties, taxes, and fees owed. That bond requirement is met for Ferro Metals USA at Miami, where the Form 3461 obtains release before the summary and duty payment. Option B is incorrect because full payment of estimated duties before release is not required under 19 CFR Part 142 for the Miami shipment, since the bond backs the later payment.
  23. The correct answer is B (Entry type 06, the foreign trade zone consumption entry code): A withdrawal from a foreign trade zone for domestic consumption is entered under entry type 06 in the CBP two-digit coding system. That is the transaction Bluewater Trading Company is making at New Orleans, so entry type 06 is the code reserved for it. Option D is wrong because entry type 01 is the standard consumption entry, and the CBP coding rules do not use it for foreign trade zone withdrawals like the New Orleans one.
  24. The correct answer is D (The entry summary, which is filed on CBP Form 7501): The formal return that lists tariff classification, appraised value, and estimated duty is the entry summary required by 19 USC 1484. That filing is called the entry summary and is made on CBP Form 7501, which makes option A the correct name for the Ferro Metals USA filing at New Orleans. Option C is wrong because a protest under 19 USC 1514 challenges a completed liquidation, and it is not the mechanism that reports value and duty on the New Orleans shipment.
  25. The correct answer is A (CBP Form 3461, the entry or immediate delivery application filed for release): Under 19 CFR Part 142, the filing on CBP Form 3461 is the application for entry or immediate delivery that lets CBP release merchandise before the entry summary is presented. Form 3461 fits the Bluewater Trading Company fasteners at Los Angeles/Long Beach because the broker needs release ahead of the duty computation that appears on the summary. Option B fails because Form 7501 is the entry summary that follows release, and 19 CFR 142.12 does not require the summary or duty payment before CBP releases cargo at Los Angeles/Long Beach.
  26. The correct answer is B (It obtains conditional release of the goods while entry summary is completed later): Filing CBP Form 3461 under 19 CFR Part 142 produces a conditional release of the merchandise while the entry summary remains due later. That is what happens for Vantage Point Traders at Houston, because the release of the perishable cargo is granted on the bond and the formal summary is still filed afterward. Option A overstates the filing, since 19 CFR 142.12 still requires an entry summary with estimated duties within 10 working days after the Houston release.
  27. The correct answer is C (A continuous or single transaction bond sufficient to cover duties and fees owed): The immediate delivery filing under 19 CFR Part 142 must rest on a bond adequate to cover the duties, taxes, and fees that may be owed on the merchandise. A continuous or single transaction bond satisfies that condition for Ferro Metals USA at Savannah, which is why the bond accompanies the Form 3461 rather than payment of duties. Option B fails because 19 CFR Part 142 permits release on the bond before estimated duties are paid, and full payment is not a condition of immediate delivery at Savannah.
  28. The correct answer is A (Substantially reduced penalty exposure by coming forward before CBP investigates): Rather than wait for CBP to find the error, the importer can proactively disclose the violation before CBP begins a formal investigation of it. The benefit is substantially reduced penalty exposure. Made before a formal investigation begins and with no ongoing pattern of undisclosed violations, a prior disclosure generally limits exposure to interest on the unpaid duties for a negligence level violation. The unpaid duties themselves still must be tendered.
  29. The correct answer is D (CBP Form 3461, requesting entry or immediate delivery of the arriving cargo): The entry regulations in 19 CFR Part 142 allow CBP to release merchandise on an immediate delivery application supported by an adequate bond. CBP Form 3461 is that application, so it is the document that releases the Harborline Freight Corporation shipment at Chicago before final duty calculation. Option A is wrong because the Form 7501 summary does not have to post duties before release, and 19 CFR 142.12 allows the summary for Chicago merchandise to follow within 10 working days after entry.
  30. The correct answer is B (Advise the client promptly so a prior disclosure can be filed in time): Brokers often discover these errors during a routine post entry audit or classification review, such as an incorrect tariff classification or an undeclared assist or royalty includible in dutiable value. Advising a client promptly so a prior disclosure can be filed before CBP starts asking questions is one of the most valuable things a broker's expertise contributes to a client relationship. A prior disclosure made before a formal investigation begins substantially reduces penalty exposure. Waiting for CBP to find the error removes that advantage.
  31. The correct answer is A (Reliquidation at a corrected amount that reflects the relief granted): The chapter states that CBP either grants the protest, in whole or in part, resulting in reliquidation at a corrected amount, or denies it. That reliquidation is CBP's own correction of the earlier liquidation. If the protest is denied, the importer generally may pursue judicial review before the United States Court of International Trade. A protest granted in part therefore produces a partial correction rather than a full reversal.
  32. The correct answer is D (Before CBP begins a formal investigation of the disclosed violation): The chapter describes a prior disclosure under 19 USC 1592(c)(4) as a proactive step taken before CBP begins a formal investigation. The benefit is substantially reduced penalty exposure for the importer. A properly filed prior disclosure, made before a formal investigation begins and with no ongoing pattern of undisclosed violations, generally limits exposure to interest on the unpaid duties for a negligence level violation. Waiting until CBP starts asking questions removes that benefit.
  33. The correct answer is C (The ACE Portal, which shows entry, liquidation, and bond status): Brokers use ACE both to file and to monitor entry status, liquidation status, and bond sufficiency. That monitoring function is exactly what the ACE Portal supports. The bulletin notice of liquidation is the official notice posted by CBP, but it does not by itself show bond sufficiency. A protest and a prior disclosure are separate procedures with their own purposes and deadlines.
  34. The correct answer is D (A denial of a claimed preference program or a reclassification of goods): A protest under 19 USC 1514 challenges CBP decisions such as a reclassification, a higher duty rate, or the denial of a claimed preference program. The protest must be filed with CBP at the port where liquidation occurred, generally within 180 days after the date of liquidation. It must set out the specific reasons the liquidation was incorrect and the relief sought. Carrier freight charges and a broker's internal filing choices are not CBP decisions reached at liquidation.
  35. The correct answer is B (From the date of liquidation, rather than entry or notice): The 180 day clock runs from the date of liquidation, not from entry, not from the entry summary filing, and not from when the importer happened to notice a bulletin notice. A broker evaluating whether a protest is still timely must pin down the actual liquidation date first. The protest is filed with CBP at the port where liquidation occurred. It must state the reasons the liquidation was incorrect and the relief sought.
  36. The correct answer is D (With CBP at the port where the liquidation actually occurred): The protest is filed with CBP at the port where liquidation occurred. It must set out the specific reasons the liquidation was incorrect and the relief sought. CBP either grants the protest in whole or in part, resulting in reliquidation at a corrected amount, or denies it. If the protest is denied, the importer generally may pursue judicial review before the United States Court of International Trade.
  37. The correct answer is B (Judicial review before the United States Court of International Trade): If CBP denies a protest, the importer generally may pursue judicial review before the United States Court of International Trade. That court step is covered elsewhere in the study guide, but the chapter notes it as the next avenue after a denial. A protest granted in whole or in part instead results in reliquidation at a corrected amount. The protest itself must be filed within the 180 day period after liquidation.
  38. The correct answer is C (It is available to the true owner if that owner is later established): Auction proceeds are first applied against unpaid duties, taxes, storage, and other charges. Any remaining balance is available to the true owner if later established. That rule gives a former owner a possible path to recover value after the sale. The chapter presents general order status as a practical reminder of why timely entry filing matters.
  39. The correct answer is D (An antidumping proceeding, a pending protest, or a request for information): The chapter states that the liquidation period can be extended for specified reasons, including an open antidumping or countervailing duty proceeding, a pending protest, or an outstanding request for information. Those grounds appear in the rules that set the normal one year liquidation period under 19 USC 1504(a)(1). Without a valid extension and affirmative action, the entry is deemed liquidated by operation of law. The deemed liquidation generally occurs one year after entry.
  40. The correct answer is C (It may be disposed of on a shorter timeline than ordinary merchandise): The chapter explains that perishable, hazardous, or otherwise unsuitable merchandise may be disposed of on a shorter timeline than other general order cargo. Ordinary unclaimed merchandise can be sold at public auction after an extended period measured from the date of arrival. Perishable goods cannot wait that long, so CBP handles them differently. The entry deadline itself is unchanged for such shipments.
  41. The correct answer is B (The absence of a timely protest, unless CBP reliquidates the entry): Once liquidation occurs, CBP posts a bulletin notice of liquidation, and it becomes final and conclusive on all parties absent a timely protest under 19 USC 1514. Reliquidation is the situation in which CBP itself corrects a liquidation, for example to fix a clerical error or mistake of fact within statutory limits. A successful protest can also lead to reliquidation at a corrected amount. Liquidation therefore closes the matter only if no protest is filed in time.
  42. The correct answer is D (It is only an estimate that liquidation may confirm or adjust later): The duty paid at entry summary is only an estimate, based on the importer's or broker's own calculation of classification, value, and rate. Liquidation is when CBP reviews the entry and either confirms that estimate as final or adjusts it. An adjustment produces either a bill for additional duty or a refund. The summary stage therefore does not fix the final amount owed.
  43. The correct answer is B (A bulletin notice of liquidation that CBP posts after liquidating): Once liquidation occurs, whether affirmatively by CBP or by the passage of time, CBP posts a bulletin notice of liquidation. The liquidation then becomes final and conclusive on all parties, absent a timely protest, unless CBP reliquidates the entry. The 180 day protest clock runs from the date of liquidation rather than the date the bulletin notice is seen. A broker should therefore pin down the actual liquidation date.
  44. The correct answer is D (They are applied first to unpaid duties, taxes, storage, and charges): When general order merchandise is sold at public auction, the proceeds are first applied against unpaid duties, taxes, storage, and other charges. Any remaining balance is available to the true owner if later established. That means the importer may recover something only after the government and storage charges are satisfied. Missing the entry deadline can therefore cost the importer the merchandise itself.
  45. The correct answer is C (Formal entry is filed at that port so the goods can be released there): An in bond movement carries merchandise under bond from the port of arrival to another CBP designated location. The Immediate Transportation entry is used when formal entry will later be filed at that other port. Because the goods were not entered for consumption while they moved, duty was not owed during the movement. Entry is then filed where the merchandise is to be entered and released.
  46. The correct answer is D (Immediate Transportation, which moves goods under bond to another port): The Immediate Transportation entry moves merchandise under bond to another port, where formal entry will later be filed. It suits cargo that arrives at a coastal seaport while the importer's business or a bonded warehouse sits somewhere else. Transportation and Exportation covers merchandise merely passing through the United States to another country. Immediate Exportation allows export directly from the port of arrival without transiting another domestic port first.
  47. The correct answer is C (The port of arrival and the port of entry are not necessarily the same place): Cargo often arrives at a coastal seaport while the importer's business, a bonded warehouse, or another designated location sits somewhere else. In that situation the cargo can move under bond, duties not yet paid, from the port of arrival to another CBP designated location. Entry is then filed where the goods are actually entered rather than at the first point of arrival. That is why a broker must distinguish the two locations carefully.
  48. The correct answer is B (Thirty days from the start of the in bond movement): The chapter states that in bond carriers have their own time limit, generally 30 days, to deliver in bond merchandise to its final destination. In bond movements carry merchandise under bond without duty being paid while it moves. The goods are physically present but not entered for consumption, so the clock on other deadlines has not started. Other figures in the choices belong to entry, summary, or warehouse storage deadlines.
  49. The correct answer is C (Transportation and Exportation, which covers goods transiting to another country): Transportation and Exportation covers merchandise that is merely passing through the United States to another country under bond. The goods are never entered for consumption in the United States. Immediate Exportation is different, because it allows export directly from the port of arrival without transiting another domestic port first. Immediate Transportation moves goods to another domestic port where entry will later be filed.
  50. The correct answer is A (Liquidation can confirm the estimate or adjust it, producing a bill or refund): The duty paid at entry summary is only an estimate based on the importer's or broker's own calculation of classification, value, and rate. Liquidation is CBP's final official computation of the duties, taxes, and fees owed on the entry. At liquidation, CBP either confirms the estimate as final or adjusts it, which produces either a bill for additional duty or a refund. Payment at the summary stage therefore does not close the matter.
  51. The correct answer is C (The deposit accompanies the entry summary and is due within ten working days): The entry summary, CBP Form 7501, is the vehicle through which the importer pays estimated duties. Filing the entry summary and depositing estimated duties must generally occur within ten working days after entry is filed and the merchandise released. The amounts are based on the importer's or broker's own calculation of classification, value, and rate. Liquidation later confirms or adjusts that estimate.
  52. The correct answer is D (19 CFR Part 141 and 19 USC 1484, which govern the entry summary): Filing the entry summary and depositing estimated duties must generally occur within ten working days after entry is filed and the merchandise released, under 19 CFR Part 141 and 19 USC 1484. The same statute places the duty to make entry on the importer of record using reasonable care. Part 149 deals with the Importer Security Filing, and 19 USC 1514 deals with protests. A broker should cite the entry summary authority when documenting this deadline.
  53. The correct answer is A (The entry summary and the deposit of estimated duties, taxes, and fees): Filing the entry summary and depositing estimated duties must generally occur within ten working days after entry is filed and the merchandise released. The entry filing itself comes earlier, within fifteen calendar days of arrival within port limits. The protest and the prior disclosure serve entirely different purposes and have their own deadlines. A broker should track the summary deadline from the actual release date.
  54. The correct answer is B (ACE replaced the older Automated Commercial System for electronic filing): The Automated Commercial Environment, or ACE, is CBP's single window system for electronic processing of import and export data. It replaced the older Automated Commercial System and is now mandated for entry summary filing across virtually all entry types. Brokers use ACE to file and to monitor entry status, liquidation status, and bond sufficiency. Paper filing is therefore not the standard route for these transactions.
  55. The correct answer is A (Liquidated damages under the importer bond, because a legal deadline was missed): The chapter warns that mixing up calendar days and working days between the two deadlines could cause a client to miss a legal deadline and face liquidated damages under the importer's bond. The entry summary and estimated duty deposit are due within ten working days after entry is filed and the merchandise released. No automatic extension applies to that period. A protest addresses a liquidation decision rather than a missed filing deadline.
  56. The correct answer is B (Entry is due in fifteen calendar days, and the summary is due in ten working days): Filing entry, CBP Form 3461 or its ACE equivalent, must generally occur within fifteen calendar days of arrival within the port limits. Filing the entry summary and depositing estimated duties must generally occur within ten working days after entry is filed and the merchandise released. Calendar days include weekends and holidays, while working days exclude them. Mixing up the two deadlines is a common exam trap.
  57. The correct answer is C (19 CFR Part 141, the general entry regulations for imported goods): The chapter places the general entry regulations in 19 CFR Part 141, and the 15 calendar day entry deadline comes from that rule. Part 149 contains the Importer Security Filing requirements, and Part 143 covers informal entry, while mail importations are in Part 145 and baggage in Part 148. The warehouse storage statute at 19 USC 1557 governs bonded warehouse entries instead. A broker calculating the entry deadline refers to Part 141.
  58. The correct answer is B (It is the tax return for the shipment, declaring classification and duty owed): The chapter describes CBP Form 7501, the entry summary, as the actual tax return for the shipment. It declares the tariff classification under the Harmonized Tariff Schedule, the country of origin, the value for duty purposes, the applicable rate of duty, any preferential trade program claimed, and the total estimated duties, taxes, and fees owed. The 3461, by contrast, is the document that lets the truck through the gate because it requests release. The summary is filed within ten working days after release.
  59. The correct answer is A (ISF, then entry, then the entry summary and estimated duty deposit): Before cargo is laden aboard an ocean vessel, the ISF must be filed no later than 24 hours before loading. Once cargo arrives within a United States port's limits, the importer or broker has 15 calendar days to file entry. Once entry is filed and the goods are released, the broker has 10 working days to file the entry summary and deposit estimated duties. That sequence is the full life cycle in order.
  60. The correct answer is D (The goods are not entered for consumption, so duty is not owed yet): An in-bond movement carries merchandise under bond from the port of arrival to another CBP designated location, with duties not yet paid. Because the goods are physically present but not entered for consumption, duty is not owed. The clock on other deadlines, including liquidation, has not started either. The port of arrival and the port of entry are not always the same place.
  61. The correct answer is C (The importer or its agent, such as a broker acting for the importer): The chapter states that the importer, or its agent, files the ten importer side data elements. A customs broker acting for the importer commonly performs that filing. The ocean carrier files the two additional elements, which is why the program is called 10+2. The filing must be made no later than 24 hours before the cargo is laden at the foreign port.
  62. The correct answer is B (Arrival of the merchandise within the limits of the port of arrival): The fifteen calendar day period begins when merchandise arrives within the port limits, under 19 CFR Part 141. Time spent at an anchorage outside those limits does not start the clock. If entry is not made within the window, the merchandise can be sent to general order. That is why a broker should pin down the actual arrival date within port limits rather than the date a vessel first appears offshore.
  63. The correct answer is C (The container stuffing location and the six digit tariff number): The ten importer side elements generally include the manufacturer or supplier, the seller, the buyer, the ship to party, the container stuffing location, the consolidator, the importer of record number, the consignee number, the country of origin, and the six digit Harmonized Tariff Schedule number. Both the container stuffing location and the six digit tariff number appear on that list. Voyage numbers, carton weights, and port director names are not among the ten elements. The ocean carrier files two additional elements of its own.
  64. The correct answer is B (Fifteen calendar days from arrival, counting weekends and holidays): Filing entry, CBP Form 3461 or its ACE equivalent, must generally occur within 15 calendar days of the date merchandise arrives within the port limits under 19 CFR Part 141. Fifteen calendar days means every day counts, including weekends and holidays. A broker therefore cannot count only business days for this deadline. The ten working day period applies later, to the entry summary and the deposit of estimated duties.
  65. The correct answer is A (ISF applies to vessel cargo, while air, truck, and rail have separate manifest rules): The chapter states that ISF does not apply to air, truck, or rail cargo in the same form. Those modes have their own separate advance manifest requirements. The Importer Security Filing program itself is tied to cargo arriving by vessel, with the importer side filing ten elements and the carrier filing two. A broker moving goods by air therefore looks to a different advance filing rule.
  66. The correct answer is B (The penalty can reach $5,000 per violation and is subject to mitigation): The chapter states that a late or inaccurate Importer Security Filing can draw monetary penalties historically up to $5,000 per violation subject to mitigation. It can also trigger a do not load message before the cargo sails. Those consequences give the broker a strong practical reason to transmit the filing correctly and on time. Penalties for fraud under the false statements statute are a separate matter from the ISF penalty.
  67. The correct answer is D (A penalty of up to $5,000 per violation and a do not load message): A late or inaccurate Importer Security Filing can draw monetary penalties, historically up to $5,000 per violation subject to mitigation. It can also trigger a do not load message, which instructs the carrier not to load the container until the issue is resolved. That message is the practical reason a broker must watch ISF transmission closely before vessel loading. The protest right and the informal entry process address unrelated customs decisions.
  68. The correct answer is C (It covers lower value shipments and categories not needing formal entry): Entry type 11 is the informal entry, used for lower value shipments and certain other categories where formal entry procedures are not required. It is not limited to baggage, and it is not reserved for goods above the formal entry threshold. Informal entries carry lighter documentation and bonding requirements, which is the simplified treatment described here. The warehouse entry is a separate type used to defer duty on goods stored under bond.
  69. The correct answer is D (Up to five years, with duty owed only upon withdrawal for consumption): Goods may remain in a bonded warehouse for up to five years without duty being owed. Duty is assessed only if and when the goods are formally withdrawn for consumption. While stored, the merchandise can be manipulated, repacked, or even manufactured under CBP supervision, depending on the class of warehouse. The one year period in the other choice belongs to temporary importation under bond, not to warehouse storage.
  70. The correct answer is B (Hold the container and not load it until the filing issue is resolved): A late or inaccurate Importer Security Filing can trigger a do not load message from CBP. That message instructs the carrier not to load the container until the issue is resolved. The filing is tied to the overseas loading event, so a missing filing can stop the cargo before it ever sails. Bonded release, informal entry, and general order all involve different customs procedures.
  71. The correct answer is D (A value of $2,500 or less, though some sensitive categories are narrower): Historically the informal entry threshold has been $2,500 or less for most commercial merchandise. The limit is narrower for a handful of import sensitive categories such as certain textiles. Informal entries also carry lighter documentation and bonding requirements than formal entries. A broker should confirm the current dollar threshold, because CBP can adjust it over time.
  72. The correct answer is B (It is allowed under CBP supervision, depending on the class of warehouse): The chapter explains that goods in a bonded warehouse can remain up to five years without duty being owed. Depending on the warehouse class, the importer may manipulate, repack, or even manufacture goods under CBP supervision while they are stored. That flexibility is one reason an importer chooses a warehouse entry. Duty is assessed only if and when the goods are withdrawn for consumption.
  73. The correct answer is A (When the goods are formally withdrawn from the warehouse for consumption): Goods can remain in a bonded warehouse up to five years without duty being owed. Duty is assessed only if and when the goods are formally withdrawn for consumption. The arrival of the merchandise does not by itself create a duty obligation when the goods go into bonded storage instead. Filing the warehouse entry summary does not accelerate the duty either, because the deferral is the point of the warehouse entry.
  74. The correct answer is D (Entry type 02, the consumption entry for merchandise subject to a quota or visa): Entry type 02 is the consumption entry used when merchandise is subject to a quantitative restriction. That category covers both an absolute quota, a hard numeric cap after which no more of that good may enter during the period, and merchandise requiring a visa or export certificate from the exporting country's government. Entry type 06 is instead the foreign trade zone consumption entry, and there is no separate tariff-rate-quota entry type. A shipment subject to an absolute quota and a visa requirement fits entry type 02.
  75. The correct answer is C (Entry type 01, the ordinary consumption entry for domestic use or sale): Entry type 01 is the ordinary consumption entry for merchandise brought in for use, sale, or consumption in the domestic market. It applies when there is no quota and no special program restriction, which makes it the everyday entry. Entry type 11 is used for lower value shipments under informal entry procedures. Entry types 21 and 23 involve bonded storage and temporary importation rather than an outright domestic sale.
  76. The correct answer is B (Entry type 21, the warehouse entry used for bonded storage of merchandise): Entry type 21 is the warehouse entry, used when merchandise goes into a bonded warehouse under 19 USC 1557 rather than being entered for consumption immediately. Goods may remain there up to five years without duty being owed. Duty is assessed only if and when the goods are formally withdrawn for consumption. Entry type 22 is the re-warehouse entry, which applies when goods are placed back into bonded storage.
  77. The correct answer is C (A live entry, which satisfies both filing deadlines at the same time): In many transactions today, especially with ACE, the entry and the entry summary are filed together electronically, and that combined filing is called a live entry. Conceptually and for exam purposes the two filings remain distinct, with two distinct legal deadlines. When they are filed together, both deadlines are effectively satisfied at once. A reconciliation entry is different, because it flags elements such as value or classification that will be finalized later.
  78. The correct answer is D (CBP Form 3461, the entry or immediate delivery application for release): Filing the 3461, or its ACE equivalent, lets the goods leave the port and move into the importer's possession. The form carries the importer of record, the entry number, the port of entry, a description of the merchandise, the estimated value, and the bill of lading number. CBP Form 7501, the entry summary, comes afterward and is where duties are declared and paid. The 3461 is best understood as the document that lets the truck through the gate.
  79. The correct answer is A (Generally up to one year, with extensions possible in some situations): Entry type 23, the temporary importation under bond, allows merchandise to enter duty free for a limited period. That period is generally up to one year, with possible extensions. The goods must be exported or destroyed within the period and must not be sold or used inconsistently with the purpose declared at entry. The five year period belongs to bonded warehouse storage, which is a different arrangement.
  80. The correct answer is D (19 CFR Part 141, the general entry regulations for imported merchandise): The chapter places the general entry regulations in 19 CFR Part 141. Part 143 covers informal entry, while mail importations are in Part 145 and baggage in Part 148, so none of those governs ordinary commercial entries. Part 149 contains the Importer Security Filing requirements for cargo arriving by vessel. A broker reviewing a routine consumption entry should begin with the general entry regulations in Part 141.
  81. The correct answer is B (It requests release so goods can leave CBP custody before duties are final): Filing the 3461, or its ACE equivalent, is what allows the goods to leave the port and move into the importer's possession. Duties are not necessarily finally calculated or paid at that point. The entry summary that declares classification, value, and estimated duties comes later on its own deadline. The form does not change ownership, extend a deadline, or liquidate the entry.
  82. The correct answer is A (Entry type 01, the ordinary consumption entry carrying the special program claim): Entry type 08 is not a free trade agreement or preference entry type. A United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement preference claim is made on an ordinary consumption entry by reporting the special program claim indicator on the entry summary, not by changing the entry type. The broker must still confirm that the importer holds or can produce the certification of origin supporting the claim, because CBP can verify the claim after the fact and assess back duties, interest, and penalties if it is unsupported. Entry type 09 is instead the reconciliation entry used when value or classification is finalized later.
  83. The correct answer is D (Filing documentation that declares value, origin, and duty rate with CBP): Entry is a formal legal act that declares what the merchandise is, who owns it, what it is worth, and the duty rate that applies. Arrival happens when the vessel physically reaches the United States and reports to CBP, which is a separate legal event from entry. CBP will not authorize release of the merchandise until the required entry documentation and any required bond have been filed. The general entry regulations governing this process appear in 19 CFR Part 141, and the duty to make entry with reasonable care rests on the importer of record under 19 USC 1484.
